### On the Planetary Kick (Latta)
Now he describes the <term>Latta</term><note>Latta literally means "a kick"; it refers to a specific astrological affliction where a planet "kicks" or affects a birth star (nakshatra) from a certain distance</note>. From Mars, the third, the twenty-second, and the third <insert>from the Sun</insert>... <note>The text here uses numerical code words: 'tri' (3), 'akriti' (22), 'vahni' (3)</note>. The planets strike the <term>Nakshatra</term><note>Lunar mansion/star sign</note> through the Latta. The Sun strikes the twelfth star ahead. Jupiter strikes the sixth. Venus strikes the twenty-fourth. Saturn strikes the eighth. Rahu strikes the twentieth. The Sun strikes the twelfth. The Moon strikes the seventh. These strike the stars ahead of them.

In the region of Magadha <note>Ancient Bihar</note>, if this occurs, survival is not possible. Following that, the sages say: This applies to the Magadha region, to Saurashtra <note>Gujarat</note> in the case of an eclipse, and to Kashmir in the case of the <term>Ekargala</term><note>A specific astronomical yoga/alignment</note> flaw. One should avoid the <term>Vaidha</term> flaw everywhere.

### On Other Flaws
Now he describes other flaws. "Indeed, for a month..." means that for a month following an eclipse, if the eclipse occurred in one's birth star, or if the Moon or the Ascendant (<term>Lagna</term>) enters that sign, it must be avoided. On the day the Moon is joined with that star—meaning the star of the day—it is considered "entering the body." For example, if an eclipse occurs in <term>Ashwini</term>, and the Ascendant later becomes Ashwini...
<margin>Birth Star 6</margin>
...it should be avoided. Similarly for the Sun and others. By "eclipse," it means stars afflicted by portents or other evils should be avoided. Both the birth star and the birth sign, when they become the Ascendant, are to be avoided. To know the star within the Ascendant... one should avoid the fourth star.

### Prohibited Time (Ghatikas) for Planetary Transitions
Now he states the <term>Ghatikas</term><note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> to be avoided during the <term>Sankranti</term><note>The moment a planet enters a new zodiac sign</note> of the Sun and others. For the Sun, Moon, Mars, and Mercury, the prohibited times are as follows:
*   **Sun:** 20 Ghatikas <note>8 hours</note> are to be avoided.
*   **Moon:** 1 Ghatika.
*   **Mars:** 9 Ghatikas.
*   **Mercury:** 2 Ghatikas.
*   **Jupiter:** 84 Ghatikas.
*   **Venus:** 6 Ghatikas.
*   **Saturn:** 150 Ghatikas <note>3.75 days</note>; one should avoid two and a half days.

### On the Flaw of Junctions (Sandhi)
Regarding the "Six": At the junction of seasons (<term>Ritu Sandhi</term>), such as the start of Spring, sixty-six (66) Ghatikas are to be avoided. As stated in the *Ratnamala*: At the junction of the signs starting with Capricorn, and at the junctions of lunar days (<term>Tithi</term>), stars, and yogas, half a day or two Ghatikas should be avoided.

### On the Duration of Eclipse Effects
Regarding what was said previously about the disturbances caused by planets: he describes the duration of the eclipse's effect. From the time of the eclipse...
*   For a **quarter eclipse**: avoid 3 days.
*   For a **half eclipse**: avoid 4 days.
*   For a **three-quarter eclipse**: avoid 6 days.
*   For a **total eclipse**: avoid 8 days.
This should be avoided from the time the eclipse is first seen.

# Muhurta Martanda Commentary, Page 21

<margin>Muhurta Martanda Commentary 21</margin>

As it is said regarding the duration of an eclipse: Before the beginning of the eclipse, one should avoid one and a half days. If three-quarters of the orb is eclipsed, avoid three days; if it is a total eclipse, avoid six days. So it is stated in the *Marriage Chapter* of the *Sharngiya*: "In a total eclipse, one should avoid auspicious works for eight days. If three parts are eclipsed, six days; if half is eclipsed, four days; and if a quarter is eclipsed, it shall be three nights—this applies if the eclipse is of the Sun or Moon." <note>original: "सूर्ययोरिति" - referring to the two luminaries.</note> In intermediate cases, the proportion should be understood through calculation. Thus says Vashistha: "Three days before the eclipse and seven days after" refers to the subject of a total eclipse. In the *Jyotish-Prakasha*: "For a total eclipse, a nearly total one, or a partial one, the days to be avoided are respectively eight, six, and four."

Now he describes the time to be avoided due to portents (*Utpata*): Regarding portents in the house, etc. Portents include earthquakes, fierce winds, great meteors, lightning strikes, and the like. Regarding the cause of planets: the planets are the essence. In the event of a planetary portent, one should avoid seven nights. This means when a portent occurs, one should avoid auspicious ceremonies for seven nights. As Vashistha said: "After a portent, one should avoid seven nights; when a comet appears, avoid auspicious acts."

Now he describes the *Bhadra* <note>A specific inauspicious astrological period or "karan"</note> and the Ascendant: On the full moon and the eighth lunar day, in the first half, there is the flaw known as *Vidushpa*. On the eighth and fourteenth days, in the first half, there is *Vidushpa*. At the end of the seventh (*Krtta*) and eleventh (*Rudra*) days, in the latter half, there is *Vidushpa*. These four occur during the dark fortnight. <note>The text here details complex calculations for the 'Bhadra' period based on lunar dates.</note> In the *Sharngiya Marriage Chapter*, this is considered corrupted; therefore, it is not mentioned here without cause.

Now he describes the characteristics of *Gandanta* <note>term: Gandanta; "end of the cheek/joint," referring to the inauspicious junctions between solar and lunar zodiac signs.</note>: Half a *Jyotish-martanda*... at the end of the constellations, specifically at the end of Revati. From that point, the time before and after is half a *ghatika* <note>A unit of time approximately 24 minutes</note>—this is *Gandanta*. Here, it means four *ghatikas* in total. This is what is said: two *ghatikas* before the end of the constellation and two *ghatikas* after; thus, the four-*ghatika* period is the Constellation-Gandanta.

Now he describes the Lunar Date (*Tithi*) Gandanta: At the end of the "Full" dates (5th, 10th, and 15th), the period of two *ghatikas* (one before and one after) is *Gandanta*.

Now he describes the Ascendant (*Lagna*) Gandanta: From the end of the signs Cancer (*Kita*), Pisces (*Antya*), and Scorpio (*Ali*), a period of one *ghatika* <note>original: "कु घटिकां" - 'Ku' represents the number one.</note> is the Lagna-Gandanta.

<margin>52</margin> Next, he describes the *Chandayudha* <note>term: Chandayudha; "Fierce Weapon," a specific dangerous astrological alignment.</note> in the latter half of the verse: It occurs at the end of the turnings of the *Sadhyas*, *Vaidhriti*, *Vyatipata*, and *Harshana* yogas. At the end of these yogas, whatever <margin>Rama 21</margin> constellation is present, that is *Chandayudha*. It is also called the "Fierce Wind Weapon" or the "Beloved Thunderbolt Weapon." Therefore, that constellation must be avoided.

Now he describes the *Ekargala* flaw: In the event of "bad yogas" (*Duryoga*)... these include Vishkumbha, Atiganda, <unclear>Lagan</unclear>da, Vyaghata, Vajra, Vyatipata, Parigha, and Vaidhriti. These are the nine bad yogas. When any of these occur, the day including the Abhijit nakshatra and the Sun... the day's constellation <unclear>nine parts</unclear>...

<summary>This page details the specific durations for which auspicious activities must be paused following eclipses and natural portents, while also defining the "Gandanta" (danger zones) at the junctions of lunar dates, constellations, and zodiac signs.</summary>
<keywords>eclipse duration, portents, Utpata, Bhadra, Gandanta, Chandayudha, astrological timing, Vashistha, Sharngiya, Hindu astrology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of astrological timing (Jyotisha), specifically focusing on inauspicious periods and planetary combinations that must be avoided for successful undertakings.</meta>

The beginning starts with the seventy days and so forth. It accompanies the constellation *Abhijit* <note>The 28th intercalary nakshatra</note>, and therefore it is called *Samijit*. What occurs there? The Sun is in a position that creates a "Single Bolt" (*Ekargala*). This is an obstacle to auspicious results. It is that in which the obstruction of good fortune resides.

**Now, the defects of the days are described.**
By the verse "The Sun's half-watch" (*Suryoyama dalam*), it is explained that in the sequence of the days of the week, starting from Sunday, one should abandon the "half-watch" (*yamardha*) <note>A 'yama' is a 3-hour period; a 'yamardha' is 90 minutes</note> as advised by the sages. When the Sun is at the measure of a pair (2), on Monday the 7th, on Tuesday the 8th (measured by *Ashvini* and others), on Wednesday the 5th, on Thursday the 9th, on Friday the 3rd, and on Saturday the 6th—these half-watches are to be avoided during the day only, not at night.

**Now, the *Kulika* periods are described.**
Starting with the Sun (Sunday) and the other days of the week, during the daytime, the 16th part and so forth are called *Kulika*. Then at night, it is one less than that (the 15th part). Thus, for the day and night of the Sun (Sunday), it is the 13th; for Monday, it is the 14th during the day and the 11th at night. This applies to all.

**Now, the hours (*Muhurtas*) of Indra and others.**
From the positions of the planets like Mars and others, the "Thorn of Time" (*Kalakantaka*) and the "Bell of Death" (*Yamaghanta*) are determined. It is said that from Jupiter comes the "Thorn" for the Sun and others, and from Mercury comes the "Bell of Death" for Venus and others.

**Now, the inauspicious hours (*Durmuhurtas*) are described.**
By the half-verse "The face of the moon..." (*Mukhendu-ja*). In the latter half, for Venus, Jupiter, and Saturday, their hours are equal to the Sun's and are considered painful due to various afflictions. On Tuesday night, the first and seventh hours are inauspicious. Furthermore, other inauspicious hours such as those of the Sun and Moon are mentioned elsewhere. Because they are numerous, only a few are stated here. Other inauspicious hours read in other texts are all contained within these "watches" (*yamas*). Therefore, I have not mentioned them here. Since by abandoning the "watches," those others are also abandoned, why expand further? This is understood by the wise; why dwell on the difficult?

**Now, the time of the occurrence of the *Pata* <note>An astronomical node or intersection</note> is described.**
Regarding the *Pata*: presently, in the second quarter, one should look at the *Brahma Siddhanta* <note>A major astronomical treatise by Brahmagupta</note> to observe the second part of the *Brahma* yoga.

**Now, since it is said that "Death" (*Mrityu*) should be avoided everywhere, the "Yoga of Death" (*Mrityuyoga*) is described.**
The Sun and other planets, when combined with *Ashvini* and other constellations, become givers of death. *Anuradha* for the Sun; *Uttara Ashadha* for the Moon; *Magha* for Mars; *Ashvini* for Mercury; *Mrigashira* for Jupiter; *Ashlesha* for Venus; and *Hasta* for Saturday. This is what is meant: on a specific day, the combination with these constellations results in a "death-giving" period.

**Now, the "Poisonous Minutes" (*Vishaghatika*) are also described.**
"Fifty, twenty-four, and thirty..." Starting from *Ashvini*, in the constellations, after these specific counts, there are four minutes (*nadis*) of "poison." This is what is meant: in *Ashvini*, after the 50th minute, there is a four-minute interval of poison. The same applies to others. The numerical figures provided here serve as the explanation itself.

# 22
## Commentary on the Muhurta Martanda

Now he explains the correction of these fixed points (<term>Dhruvas</term> <note>The constant values used in astronomical calculations</note>). Regarding the **Nakshatra** (lunar mansion): the fixed point multiplied by the movement of the Nakshatra, added to seven, starting from five and twenty-four, divided by sixty, becomes the corrected value. Beyond this, after the corrected fixed point, the minutes and seasons should be made clear in the manner described. These clarified values, after the corrected fixed point, are to be understood as the change of days.

Now, an exception regarding these **poisonous periods** (<term>Vishaghatis</term> <note>Inauspicious time segments within a Nakshatra</note>): Regarding the **Moon**: if the Moon is positioned in a sign of a benefic planet, it destroys the fear of poison. Or, if it is in a corner position <note>the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</note>, it likewise destroys the fear of poison. In this context, if the King <note>the ruling planet</note> is in the Ascendant (<term>Lagna</term>), it destroys the fear of poison. Thus, there are five ways to cancel this affliction. An inferior planet is condemned in marriage, as stated here. Therefore, one should avoid those condemned positions and accept the favorable ones for all rituals.

As **Brihaspati** <note>The divine preceptor and authority on law/astrology</note> says: "The Moon destroys the defect of the poisonous period when in a corner, except for the Ascendant; or if the lords of the centers or the Ascendant are present." In the text *Phalapradipa*: "The Moon destroys the defect of the poisonous period even if it has traveled half its course, is aspected by a friend, or is in its own division or in the Ascendant."

Now he describes the favorable and unfavorable planets in the **Marriage Ascendant**: If the Moon and malefic planets are in the Ascendant, they cause grief regarding children. As it is said: "If the Moon and all the cruel planets are in the seventh house, or if Mercury is in the eighth..." Likewise, Jupiter and Rahu <note>the North Node of the Moon</note> in the fourth house—there is no disease for one who has the rays <note>of grace</note>. In the *Brihajjataka*, the names of Rahu are listed as "Sikhin" and others. Likewise, Mars and the Sun in the eighth house, or Venus in the third—each of these in their respective places causes grief regarding children.

Now he describes the favorable positions: All planets in the eleventh house are auspicious. Benefic planets in any house are good, except for the condemned ones. Malefic planets are good in the second <gloss>wealth</gloss>, the third, and the sixth houses. Among those, the eighth house is condemned, except for those malefic planets that may be accepted there. The Moon and the Sun in the third, fourth, or second—the Moon in these positions specifically grants auspiciousness and protection. This means they are "makers of prosperity."

Thus, having discussed the movement of favorable and unfavorable planets, he mentions a specific exception regarding the defect of the eighth house: **Venus** in the sign of Virgo... <note>The text here discusses technical planetary strengths</note> ...it does not cause a defect. As **Kashyapa** says: "Whether in debilitation, exaltation, or a friend's house, if Venus is in the sixth house during the day, there is no doubt that there is no defect."

Now he explains the exception for the defect of **Mars** in the eighth house: Even if Mars is in the eighth house (the house of death), if it is situated in the house of an enemy or its own sign, or <insert>--</insert> if it is combust (hidden by the Sun's light), then it destroys the affliction. Its enemy is Mercury. Mars is known as the "knower of enemies"...

# The Twelve Divisions and the Aspect of the Auspicious
-> Seen in the twelve divisions, seen by the auspicious, seen in the good divisions, obtained from here, from the sight of the good divisions 2 <-

<margin>3</margin> Joined. Of that 2, Gemini and Virgo are the houses of Mercury <note>original: "shashi griham" - though Shashi usually means Moon, the context of Gemini/Virgo refers to Mercury's domain</note>, and Cancer is the house of the Moon. Even if it is in its debilitation, or set <note>astagata - disappeared behind the sun's light</note>, or placed in the eighth house, it does not cause the "breaking of the Ascendant" <term>Lagna-bhanga: a condition where the primary auspicious timing of a chart is ruined</term>. "Set" means exactly that. Regarding that, Kashyapa says: "Even if Mars is set, debilitated, or in the sign of Aries or Pisces, the certain fault of Mars in the eighth house does not exist at all."

Now, he speaks of the exception for the Moon in the twelfth year/house: "In the bright half." In the bright half <term>Shukla Paksha: the waxing phase of the moon</term>, the Moon <note>original: "vidhu"</note> in the twelfth or sixth house, if joined with the sight of an auspicious division <note>original: "suvarga-drishti"</note>, such a Moon becomes a giver of good. When does this happen? When Jupiter <note>original: "ijya"</note> or Venus is in the Ascendant. What kind of Jupiter? One that is "joined with strength" <note>original: "strio-jo-yuji"</note>. It is said: "It does not join with that strength," meaning when it is very powerful.

In this case, the fault is removed. This is what is said: Even if Jupiter or Venus is in the Ascendant, if the Moon in the bright half is accompanied by the sight of an auspicious division, then even in the twelfth or sixth house, it gives good results. It will not cause the "breaking of the Ascendant." Otherwise, in the *Mirror of Muhurta* <note>a classical astrological text</note>: "When Venus or Jupiter is strong in the body (Ascendant), seen by an auspicious planet, in an auspicious division, the waxing Moon creates good."

Now he speaks of the exception for the Moon when joined with other planets <note>original: "sagraha"</note>. "Joined with a planet" means with one or many planets, excluding Mercury and Jupiter. As previously stated, "joined with planets other than these." Such a Moon, when placed in a center <term>Kendra: the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</term> or its own division, becomes a giver of good. "Center" is well known. "Own division" means its own sign. If it is seen by Jupiter staying in its own division or a center, the Moon joined with planets gives good and does not cause a "break." Jupiter, staying in its own division, being strong and situated in a center, destroys the fault of the Moon being with other planets.

Now he speaks of the general exception to faults: "Of the faults." In the "spokes" <note>Kantaka: another name for the Kendra/Center houses</note>, the Ascendant, the Center, or the Trine: Mercury <note>original: "induja"</note> destroys a hundred faults. Venus staying there destroys two hundred faults. Jupiter staying there destroys a hundred thousand faults.

He then gives a further exception: "When the Sun is in the Ascendant and Saturn is in the house of faults, the strength of the Sun and Moon is present." Such things as the "falling of the Ascendant" are not destroyed. As said by the sage Vasishtha: "The faults arising from the solar/lunar eclipses, the falling of meteors, or the 'scissors' of the seventh house, all perish when the Ascendant is endowed with the strength of the Moon and Sun, just as the rising of the Sun destroys the darkness."

Now he speaks of the "breaking of the cancellation": "It would break." Those cancellations of faults which are powerless do not produce results; they are not capable of canceling the fault. Therefore, one should declare the cancellation of a fault only after considering the strength of the cause. Thus it is said: "The faults mentioned in a general sense are indeed like enemies; if the cancellations are weak, they result in a lack of praise (success)."

Now he speaks of the seventh house...

The degrees are combined; then, in the calculation for a birth chart (<term>Jataka</term>), the digits are determined. By the method of "Kshemamita" etc., this is what is said: having calculated the time of the Sun, and having calculated the <term>Nata</term> at that time,

the sum becomes the degrees. These are applied in order, meaning by adding or subtracting for the East and West. That Sun position becomes the Tenth House. Six signs from there is the "Happiness" or Fourth House.

This has been stated. Similarly, six signs from the "Fire" <note>Southeast/Agneya</note> direction becomes the 6th house. Having thus calculated the four cardinal houses, he explains the calculation of the intermediate houses: "By the inverse of the three parts." "Vigata" means gone; the "Asta" or setting house is that from which it is derived. By that inverse, one-third of the difference is taken in signs and degrees.

Add the "three-h-r-ku" <note>mnemonic for numerical values</note> and subtract from two. Both, as he said, "6-r-2," these two are to be applied to the "Fire" <note>Agneya/Southeast</note>. When added to the <term>Lagna</term><note>The Ascendant or First House.</note>, it becomes the second house. When two parts are added there, it becomes the third house. The one-third part added to the "three-h-r-ku" becomes the fourth; when added to the "ku-pra," it becomes the fifth house. When added to "ha-pra," it becomes the sixth house. By adding six signs to these, the seventh and subsequent houses are known. From the <term>Lagna</term> to the seventh is called the "Mada" <note>Desire/7th house</note>, and so the houses beginning with the body (<term>Tanu</term>/1st house) are found.

Now, the calculation of the <term>Sandhi</term> (Junction): "The junction is half the sum." The junction is the midpoint of the sum of two houses. Having thus calculated the house junctions, as said before, a planet equal to that degree has no fruit. One should consider the strength of the planet and, by proportion, determine the result based on the distance between the house center and the junction. This is a simplified method for house calculation from the <term>Jataka Paddhati</term><note>A classic manual on natal astrology.</note>; for details, one should consult the <term>Jataka</term> texts. definition of the <term>Kartari</term><note>Literally "Scissors" - an affliction where a house or planet is hemmed in by malefic planets.</note> defect and its exceptions, he speaks in a verse: "From the Moon or the Ascendant..." If from the Moon or from the <term>Lagna</term> (Ascendant), there are two cruel <note>malefic</note> planets on either side, it is <term>Kartari</term>.

This <term>Kartari</term> is not powerful [in all cases]. Like the "servant's way," if a malefic is in the 2nd house in direct motion, and in the 12th house there is a malefic in direct motion, then it is a "Great <term>Kartari</term>." Now he describes the cancellation (<term>Bhanga</term>) of this defect:

If Jupiter is aspecting, that <term>Kartari</term> is cancelled. This is the first cancellation. Or, if the <term>Lagna</term> is a sign of a benefic planet, it does not exist. This is the second cancellation. If Mercury (<term>Budha</term>) is in the 2nd house, it does not exist; this is the third cancellation. Or,

if a benefic is in the <term>Lagna</term> or the 2nd, it does not exist; this is the fourth cancellation. Or, if the "Benefics" — Jupiter (<term>Guru</term>), Venus (<term>Shukra</term>), or Mercury (<term>Budha</term>) — are in the angles (<term>Kendras</term>) or trines (<term>Konas</term>) up to the 7th house, that <term>Kartari</term> is not present; this is the fifth cancellation. Or, if the two malefic planets causing the <term>Kartari</term> are in their signs of debilitation (<term>Nicha</term>), then the defect does not exist. This is the certain cancellation of <term>Kartari</term>.

# Exceptions to the Five-Fold Flaws of the Ascendant

Regarding the exceptions to the heat of the five-fold flaws <note>Panchaka Dosha: a set of five specific inauspicious configurations in Vedic astrology</note>, it is said: from the Ascendant <term>Lagna: the rising sign at the time of an event</term>, the lunar day <term>Tithi: a lunar day in the Hindu calendar</term>, the planets, and so on. From the Ascendant 8, from the Ascendant the lunar day is 18, the lunar day is 5, the elapsed lunar day is 8.

As far as the measure goes, that is the lunar day. Lunar day 11, day of the week 12, sign 10, [number] 10, [number] 8, [number] 8. These are added and divided by nine in sequence. This is known as the calculation method. In sequence: 1, 5, 8—these are the "thieves" <note>metaphorical terms for specific types of astrological afflictions</note>. The five are 5, and the remainder is divided by the planets.

Here, by dividing by nine, the remainders are: 8, 8, 8, 2. The "Vedas" are four (4), the "Seasons" are six (6), and "Earth" is one (1). These are the five-fold factors. This is what is being said:

Take the Ascendant 11, add the elapsed lunar days starting from the first day of the fortnight, and divide by nine.
- If the remainder is 1: Disease <note>Roga</note>
- If the remainder is 2: Fire <note>Agni</note>
- If the remainder is [missing/implied]: King/Government <note>Raja</note>
- If the remainder is 6: Thief <note>Chora</note>
- If the remainder is 8: Death <note>Mrityu</note>

One must avoid these in specific activities. For fire-related rituals or the sacred thread ceremony <note>Maunji/Upanayana</note>, avoid the "Fire-Panchaka." On Sundays, avoid the sacred thread ceremony. The "Fire-Panchaka" should be avoided during the day. On Mondays, avoid entering a new house.

<margin>Value 6</margin>

Saturn, the Moon, Mercury, and the Sun. On Wednesdays and Saturdays, avoid serving a master. On Mondays, avoid the "King-Panchaka." On Thursdays <note>Ijya</note> and Tuesdays <note>Ara</note>, avoid the "Thief-Panchaka" during travel. This is the rule: during a journey, avoid the "Thief-Panchaka" as far as the lunar day is concerned. Regarding marriage: Friday <note>Sita</note>, Wednesday <note>Vitta</note>, and twilight. During marriage, which is well known, avoid these times.

Now, the exception for departure is stated: When the body (the Ascendant) is weak, but the Ascendant [lord] is strong, even then, if the Ascendant lacks strength, the result is fruitless. This is stated in the *Laghujataka* <note>A foundational 6th-century astrological text by Varahamihira</note>. If the 1st or 8th house is occupied or aspected by Mercury or Jupiter, that sign becomes strong.

Regarding the calculation of the Moon and the Ascendant in the two verses: "From the birth sign to death." This means from the birth Ascendant or the birth sign <term>Rashi: the zodiac sign the moon occupies at birth</term>. If the lords of the 3rd and 8th houses <note>houses associated with longevity and death</note> are placed in the house of death, or if their portions <term>Amsha: a divisional chart, usually the Navamsha</term> are in the Ascendant, then death is certain for the person. However, this is only if the lords of the birth house and death house are not in harmony.

If the lords of the birth sign and the death sign are mutual friends, or if there is a sense of "oneness" between them, then there is no flaw. This is the settled conclusion. If the lord of the 8th house or the Ascendant is in a bright sign, the man attains death only after seeing the "Three-Eyed One" <note>Lord Shiva; implies a long life or a holy death</note>.

# Commentary on the Muhurta Martanda
## Page 24

<margin>Commentary on Muhurta Martanda, Page 24</margin>

...and also from the two [the Moon and the Ascendant]. Birth occurs from the Moon and the Ascendant; the eighth signs from these two are considered. If the lords of these signs are situated in the Ascendant, then one should abandon those portions <note>original: "anshas"</note>. As stated in the *Chudamani*: "The Ascendant, the eighth house, and the eighth lord are destroyed in all works." Even there, some say it is like a hundred mouths <note>meaning extremely destructive</note>, therefore it causes defects in that very place. There is no defect if the eighth Ascendant is the lord of the second half of the birth sign. If they are friends, then the sages know that an auspicious ceremony may be performed.

The fourth and eleventh houses have been discussed from the perspective of the house of invocation. Since the fourth and twelfth houses from the Ascendant are endowed with virtues, this is the opinion of all teachers. Keeping this in heart, only that which is endowed with virtue is accepted. In the *Ratna* [treatise], it is said that the lord of the body <note>the Ascendant lord</note> and the five planets should not be in the eighth house, and the lunar division should not be in the eighth. Since only the virtuous is accepted, what is the use of further elaboration?

Now, the author explains the results of the divisions <note>anshas</note>: "These divisions..." etc.
The divisions of the Water-bearer <term>Kumbha/Aquarius</term>, the Twins <term>Mithuna/Gemini</term>, the Timid one <term>Kanya/Virgo</term>, and the Bow <term>Dhanu/Sagittarius</term> are beautiful. These are: Aquarius, Libra, Gemini, Virgo, and Sagittarius. These are well-known. These are declared as excellent and praised.

Furthermore, he states a specific rule: "Divisions devoid of Mercury..." etc. Those from which Mercury <term>Budha</term> has departed are "devoid." The divisions of the signs <note>Navamshas</note> of those [planets] create the form of the body. From this, it follows that even the division of Mercury does not cause a defect. Just as when the Moon is joined with malefic planets, or by a malefic planet alone, they cause death <note>original: "mriti"</note>. This refers to the bride and groom. In the case of one who is weak or devoid of strength, the sign divisions cause misery and poverty.

He states another distinction: "The best ninth-division" <margin>is...</margin> The best ninth-division <term>Vargottama</term> is a producer of results. One should not give <note>select</note> the ninth-division of the Scorpion <term>Vrishchika/Scorpio</term> or the Crab <term>Karka/Cancer</term> unless it is a *Vargottama* <note>where the planet occupies the same sign in both the main chart and the ninth-division chart</note>, as it causes destruction. In a movable Ascendant, one should not give that same ninth-division even if it is *Vargottama*. In a movable or dual-natured Ascendant, one should not give a movable portion. When is this? When the Moon is in the Scales <term>Tula/Libra</term> or the Deer <term>Makara/Capricorn</term>. This movable yoga should not be given.

Furthermore, regarding the Moon being between the Scales and the Deer: it is said that this is because of the absence of marriage constellations in Cancer. Therefore, one should not give an Ascendant where the five planets are diminished. The meaning is that an Ascendant in which the five desired planets are not present should not be given.

Now he explains the method for calculating the Ascendant and the ninth-division of the desired Ascendant. First, he speaks of the divisions starting from the Ram <term>Mesha/Aries</term> and so on. In the Ram and others, the divisions are stated in sequence by repeated counting. They are as follows:
*   In **Aries**: Aries and the following.
*   In **Taurus**: Capricorn and the following.
*   In **Gemini**: Libra and the following.
*   In **Cancer**: Cancer and the following.
*   Similarly, in **Leo**: Aries and the following.
*   In **Virgo**: Capricorn and the following.
*   In **Libra**: Libra and the following.
*   In **Scorpio**: Cancer and the following.
*   Again, in **Sagittarius**: Aries and the following.
*   In **Capricorn**: Capricorn and the following.
*   In **Aquarius**: Libra and the following.
*   In **Pisces**: Cancer and the following.

<margin>Rama 24</margin>
Thus, the ninth-divisions <term>Navamshas</term> are explained. The degrees passed in all ninth-divisions up to thirty are multiplied by ten (10), and when divided, the result in degrees and minutes becomes the body of the Ascendant.
Here is an example: The desired Ascendant is Aries. Its portion starting from Aries proceeds <gloss>50 51</gloss>. The ninth-divisions are five (5). These are multiplied by ten... <margin>24</margin>

<meta>This page continues the technical calculations for the Lagna (ascendant) and introduces the method for determining the Hora (planetary hour) and its effects on marriage compatibility.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>

The obtained <term>Lagna</term><note>The Ascendant; the sign of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a specific time.</note> is 0;16;40;0. Combined with the desired Lagna, it is 0;12;6;40;00. Now, the author explains the wisdom regarding the rising and setting of the signs. If the degrees of the Lagna and its own position are joined or aspected by their own lords, they do not grant happiness. Alternatively, if they aspect each other, they are similarly ineffective. From these, the seventh house is considered in the same way for the bride; if these conditions are not met, they lead to "death" <note>In astrological terms, 'death' often refers to the end of a specific auspicious condition or a severe affliction rather than literal mortality.</note>. Doubling the sun's position and subtracting the Lagna... the lords of the <term>Hora</term><note>An astrological unit of time roughly equal to one hour; also refers to the division of a sign into two halves.</note> are determined from the day-lord in the sequence: Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars. In a "harsh" <note>malefic</note> week-day, the results are undesirable; in a "gentle" <note>benefic</note> week-day, they are middling. Regarding the degrees of the Lagna: the Lagna <margin>Page 4</margin> or the <term>Navamsha</term><note>The ninth part of a zodiac sign, used for fine-tuned predictions.</note> of the Lagna, if joined with or aspected by its own lord, does not grant happiness to the groom. Or, if they are aspected by each other's lords, they do not grant happiness. Having spoken of the purity of the groom, he now speaks of the purity of the setting <note>the 7th house, representing the partner</note>. "From those two, the seventh..." and so on. From those two (the Lagna and its Navamsha), the seventh house and its Navamsha are considered. If they are joined with their own lords, they grant happiness to the young woman (the bride). Or, if those two—the seventh Lagna and seventh Navamsha—are aspected by each other's lords, they grant happiness to the bride. If this is not the case, then they lead to the "death" of the two respectively.

### Now, the method for calculating the Hora at the desired time.

"That doubled..." etc. When the Sun is subtracted from the Lagna and the result is doubled, the lords of the Hora are found starting from the lord of the day. He states the sequence of calculation. Who are those Hora lords? **The Sun, Venus, Mercury, Moon, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.**
*   **The Sun** is well known.
*   **Sita** is Venus.
*   **Jna** is Mercury.
*   **Indu** is the Moon.
*   **Arki** is Saturn.
*   **Jiva** is Jupiter.
*   **Vasuja** is Mars.
In a harsh <note>malefic</note> week-day, the Horas arising from harsh planets are undesirable. In a gentle week-day, they are middling; that is to say, a "good" Hora on a "good" day is auspicious. On a malefic day, it is middling. 

**Here is an example:**
On a Thursday, the desired Sun is 0;2;52 and the desired Lagna is 5;0;0. Subtracting the Sun and doubling the result gives 8;18;47. The resulting Hora is the 4th completed, meaning the 5th Hora is currently active. Counting from the lord of the day (Jupiter), following the sequence of Jupiter, Mars, etc., the current Hora is that of **Mercury**.

### Now, the method for knowing the cause of friendship or enmity in the Lagna and Hora.

He states the result: In the Lagna, if the Hora lord is in the seventh house, the results are auspicious or inauspicious...

# ## **The Calculation of the Body (Anga)**

**Now, the Body:** The **Body in the Ascendant** <note>original: *Ango lagne*</note> is not the same as the **Body** elsewhere. By subtracting the **Ascendant** from the **Seventh Planet's time** <note>the longitudinal position of the planet in the 7th house</note>, and converting the result into minutes of arc (*kalika*) through calculation...

When the **Planet** <note>khaga</note> is in the seventh, and the **Body** <note>anga</note> is calculated, we divide by 200 <note>represented by the numerical code 'Self-Space-Twin-Yama'</note>. The result obtained is 54 <note>represented by 'Arrows-Seasons-Five'</note>. When the planet is in its **own division** <note>nijansha</note>...

<margin>
Benefic, Benefic.
H: 2
Good Ascendant.
House 4 or 3.
The Seventh House (Jamitra) = 2.
</margin>

...one should observe the whole. In the 2nd or 11th houses. 10. From the **Water** <note>4th house</note>, the **Angle** <note>8th house</note>, or the 4th, the **Body** is obtained. 3/2. Regarding the **Hora in the Ascendant**...

In the **Hora of the Ascendant**, if a benefic **Planet** is situated in the **Seventh House** <note>the house of desire/marriage, termed 'Smara'</note>, then there is mental distress <note>adhi</note>. If a malefic is present, **Death** <note>mrityu</note> occurs. Then, by calculating from that **Planet** in the seventh house, converting to minutes, and dividing by 200, the result is fifty-four (54). This indicates the presence of mental distress; otherwise, it does not exist.

**Here is an example:** Previously, the **Ascendant** <note>Lagna</note> was 4/10. A certain planet is in the **Seventh House** at 10/11. Subtracting the Ascendant gives 6/17. This, converted to minutes, is 9986. Dividing this by 200 gives 54.

Now he describes the **Cancellation** <note>Bhanga</note> of these effects: If the **Planet** is in its **own division** <note>nijansha</note> or enters the **Setting House** <note>astam-bhavanam</note>, then the negative effects of the **Seventh House** <note>Jamitra</note> do not occur. If it is a **Benefic Friend**, or in its **Exaltation** <note>uccha</note>, or in its **own Navamsha division**, or in the **division of an exaltation**, or in its **own specific degree**... then the negative effects are canceled. These are the **Six Cancellations** <note>shad-bhanga</note>.

Alternatively, if the **Sun** is in the **6th, 3rd, or 11th houses**, there are three **Cancellations**. Or, if a benefic is in the **10th, 4th, or the Trines (9th and 5th)**, and if the **Moon** is viewed with a full aspect, then the negative Seventh House effect does not occur. In total, there are fourteen such **Cancellations**.

Next, he speaks of the planetary aspects: **Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury** <note>Guru, Bhrigu, Budha</note> respectively. They aspect the houses. All planets aspect the 7th. Now, regarding the **Trines (9th and 5th)**... these are the **Three Duties** <note>Dharmatraya</note> in order. **Saturn** <note>Shani</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Guru</note>...

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of planetary aspects and begins the detailed calculation of the Shadvarga (six-fold division) system in Vedic astrology.</meta>

The sons of the earth <note>original: "bhūputrāḥ"; here referring to the planets, specifically Mars</note>, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars act in this way. Namely: Saturn looks with a vision of three and ten <note>3rd and 10th houses</note>. Jupiter looks with a vision of the corners, nine and five <note>9th and 5th houses</note>. The son of the earth <note>Mars</note> looks with a vision of four and eight <note>4th and 8th houses</note>. All planets look at the seventh house. Now, to explain the calculation of the six-fold divisions <term>Shadvarga: the six divisional charts used to assess planetary strength</term>, he first states their names:

The Sign, the Hour, the Third, the Ninth, the Twelfth, and the Thirtieth divisions are the sequence of the divisions. Some say seven are auspicious, while others say the divisions of friends are auspicious. <margin>= Lord 1</margin> Regarding the "Sign and Hour": The Sign is the house; the Hour <term>Hora: a 15-degree division, half of a sign</term> is half a sign; the Third <term>Drekkana: a 10-degree division</term> is the decan; the Ninth <term>Navamsha: a 3.33-degree division</term> is well-known; the Twelfth <term>Dwadashamsha: a 2.5-degree division</term> is well-known; the Thirtieth <term>Trimshamsha: a 1-degree division</term> is well-known. These are declared to be the divisions in order. A division belonging to a benefic planet is called "seven-auspicious." Other teachers say the divisions of a friend <note>a planet in a friendly sign</note> grant seven-fold auspiciousness.

Now he explains the calculation of the Hour <term>Hora</term>: In an odd sign, the first half belongs to the Sun and the second to the Moon. In an even sign, it is the Moon and then the Sun. In the Decan <term>Drekkana</term>, the first part is the self, the second is the fifth, and the third is the ninth. The Ninth division <term>Navamsha</term> is said to start from the sign itself. The Thirtieth divisions <term>Trimshamsha</term> for Mars, Saturn, Jupiter, Mercury, and Venus are defined by the degrees five, five, eight, seven, and five <note>original: 5, 5, 8, 7, 5</note> in odd signs; in even signs, this order is reversed. This is the Six-fold Division.

Regarding the houses: the house of whichever planet it is, that is what is declared. Such as Aries being the house of Mars, and so on. Now for the calculation of the Hour: In an odd sign like Aries, the Hours of the Sun and Moon occur in sequence. In an even sign, the Hours of the Moon and Sun occur. In the first fifteen-degree portion, it is the Sun's Hour; in the next fifteen-degree portion, it is the Moon's Hour.

Now for the calculation of the Decan <term>Drekkana</term>: The Decan is of three types, consisting of ten degrees each. These are known in order as the first, the son <note>5th house</note>, and the dharma <note>9th house</note>. This means: the lord of the first Decan is the lord of the rising sign; the lord of the second Decan is the lord of the fifth house from it; the lord of the third Decan is the lord of the ninth house from it.

Now for the calculation of the Twelfth division <term>Dwadashamsha</term>: It starts from itself. This means from the sign where the planet or ascendant is located. The twelve divisions are to be known. Namely: the thirty degrees of a sign are divided by twelve. The result is two and a half degrees per division. <unclear>...</unclear>

230 | Here begins the **Twelfth-part** <note>original: "Dvadashamsha"; a 2.5-degree division of a zodiac sign</note> division. That alone is to be calculated. Two and a half degrees constitute the Twelfth-part of a **Ascendant** <note>original: "Lagna"; the rising sign</note>. The same applies to the person <note>the native of the horoscope</note>. One should count from the beginning of the Ascendant's own degrees until the end. The lord of the zodiac sign where the count rests shall be the Lord of the Twelfth-part.

### Now, the Method for the Thirtieth-part
<term>Trimshamsha: A divisional chart where a sign is divided into 30 degrees, assigned to five planets (excluding the Sun and Moon) used to determine character and strength.</term>

"Shukra" refers to the famous Venus. "Jna" is Mercury. "Ijya" is Jupiter. "Yama" is Saturn. "Agra" is Mars. Thus, for these five, in order, the portions are: **Sages** (7), **Wives** (8), **Arrows** (5), **Wealth** (5), and **Arrows** (5).
<margin>26</margin>
The portions for Venus and the others are: 5, 7, 8, 5, and 5. This is to be understood for **Even Signs** <note>Taurus, Cancer, Virgo, Scorpio, Capricorn, Pisces</note>. There too, one should count from the beginning of the Ascendant's degrees until the end; where it rests, that is the Lord of the Thirtieth-part. In **Odd Signs** <note>Aries, Gemini, Leo, Libra, Sagittarius, Aquarius</note>, these same planets and degrees are to be known in reverse order. This has been described by many learned scholars.

### Now, the Method for Calculating the Desired Time
<term>Ishtakala: The specific moment for which a horoscope is cast, usually measured from sunrise.</term>

The author describes the method for calculating the transit of the Sun. The number of days elapsed since the **Solar Ingress** <note>original: "Sankranti"; the moment the Sun enters a new zodiac sign</note> is divided by the total days between two Ingresses. By adding the result to the Sun's position at the start of the sign (Aries, etc.), and accounting for the **Precession of the Equinoxes** <note>original: "Ayanamsha"</note>, the "Current Sun" is found. If the calculation is for the night, six signs must be added. Multiply the remaining degrees to be traversed by the local rising time, divide by thirty... then the time is clarified by the Ascendant and other factors.

"Between Ingresses" means the interval between two Solar Ingresses. The number of days is divided by this. The resulting number of days is the "Desired Day" from the start of the Ingress. From that, the degrees and minutes of the Sun's entry into the sign are obtained. What kind of Sun? The one that has traveled from Aries onwards, meaning the sum of the signs already traversed. This is the "Current Sun."
<margin>26</margin>
**Here is an example:**
The days between the Libra and Scorpio Ingresses are 28 days and 56 minutes. The desired elapsed time from the Libra Ingress is 5 days and 11 minutes. Dividing this elapsed time by the total interval between Ingresses, we get the signs and degrees: 0 signs, 5 degrees, 51 minutes, and 16 seconds. Adding this to the signs already traversed by the Sun (6 signs), the Current Sun is: 6 signs, 5 degrees, 7 minutes. This Sun must be adjusted by the **Precession of the Equinoxes** <note>original: "Ayanamsha"</note>. The Precession values are mentioned in other texts... <margin>Loss/Expenditure</margin> When calculating for the night, six signs must be added to the Sun adjusted by the Precession. The product of the degrees to be traversed and the local rising time...

The calculation is completed; the time is thirty. For the Sun, including the <term>precession of the equinoxes</term> <note>original: "Sayanansha"; the correction for the wobbling of the Earth's axis</note>, one must determine the degrees yet to be traversed (the "remaining degrees"). By multiplying these remaining degrees by the local rising times of the zodiac signs <note>original: "nijodaya"</note> and dividing the product by thirty <note>original: "khakharamapta"; literally 'sky-sky-Rama' or 300, but here referring to the 30 degrees of a sign</note>, the "remaining time" of the Sun is obtained. The local rising times for one's own location should be understood from the relevant tables. 

How is this time further refined? It is combined with the time elapsed for the current Ascendant. The method for calculating this elapsed time is as follows: it is not the time of the signs already passed, but specifically the degrees of the current Ascendant that have already risen. This is what is meant: 

Add the precession to the desired Ascendant <note>Lagna</note>. Multiply the degrees already elapsed by the local rising time of that sign, divide by thirty, and the result is the "elapsed time of the Ascendant." This value must be added to the Sun's remaining time. Furthermore, one must add the rising times of all the intervening zodiac signs between the Sun and the Ascendant. The resulting sum is the "moving time," which, when clarified, becomes the <term>Ishtakala</term> <note>the precise time elapsed since sunrise</note>. It should be understood that when this is divided by sixty, it is converted into <term>Ghatis</term> <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>.

Now, a special case in calculating the desired time is explained: If the Ascendant and the Sun are in the same sign, the time is found by taking the difference in their degrees, multiplying by the local rising time, and dividing by thirty. A further distinction is made: if the Ascendant <note>tanu</note> is "less" than the Sun <note>ina</note>—meaning it precedes it—then that calculated time is the time *before* sunrise. This too, when <term>divided by sixty</term> <note>original: "shashtyabhakto"</note>, results in the measure of Ghatis.

Now, since this time cannot be accurately determined without an instrument, the characteristics and the setup of the <term>Ghati-yantra</term> <note>a water clock consisting of a sinking bowl</note> are described:

->**On the Vessel**<-

The vessel should be deep enough to allow the bowl to submerge fully. In this water-filled vessel, place the well-crafted Ghati bowl, which is calibrated to sixty <term>Palas</term> <note>a unit of weight or time; 60 Palas = 1 Ghati</note>. When this is done...

# On Inauspicious Times and the Wedding Altar

In vain. <note>original: "mogham"</note> One should consider the Ascendant <term>Lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> along with the planets. Mars <note>original: "Kuja" or "Bhauma"</note> situated in the eighth house is problematic. Regarding Jupiter and Saturn: the fall of Jupiter and Saturn's days, characterized by great winds, is understood through the established astronomical treatises <term>Siddhanta</term>. At the Sun's entry into a new sign <term>Arka-samkrama</term>, the faults arising from that transition must be avoided. 

Now, concerning the avoidance of faults on the days of Jupiter and Saturn: on Jupiter’s day (Thursday), one must avoid the half-watch <note>approximately 1.5 hours</note> before and after the "Hour of Death" <term>Yamaghanta; an inauspicious time segment</term>. On Saturn’s day (Saturday), the period known as <term>Kulika</term> occurs before and after sunset. According to the Martanda <note>a reference to the astrological text Muhurta Martanda</note>, these portions are to be abandoned in auspicious works. By this logic, the periods associated with Kulika are condemned on Thursdays and Saturdays.

### On the Twilight Hour (Godhula)

Next, the text discusses the application of the "Dust of the Cows" hour <term>Godhula; the auspicious time of sunset when returning cattle kick up dust</term> for Brahmins and others in specific urgent situations. In times of great distress, if a proper Ascendant is unavailable, this Godhula time is considered. It is stated in the *Jyotir-nibandha*:

-> "When a precise calculated Ascendant <term>Ghati-lagna</term> is not available, then the Godhula time is remembered. The wise say this is generally for the Shudra class and not for the Twice-born <note>the three upper castes: Brahmins, Kshatriyas, and Vaishyas</note>. However, if a marriage is imminent and the proper Ascendant is delayed, then Godhula is considered auspicious for all classes." <-

### Dimensions of the Wedding Altar (Vedika)

Now, the characteristics of the wedding altar <term>Vedika</term> are described. The altar should slope toward the east. For a Brahmin, it should be four cubits <term>Hasta; the length from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</term> square and one cubit high. 

For the other classes, the measurement is reduced by a specific number of finger-widths <term>Angula</term>. Specifically:
* **For a Kshatriya:** Reduced by two fingers.
* **For a Vaishya:** Reduced by three fingers.
* **For a Shudra:** Reduced by four fingers.

This altar is to be constructed to the left of the planetary positions. 

### On Various Ritual Acts

Next, it is asked: in which lunar mansion <term>Nakshatra</term> should the various parts of the ritual be performed? Whatever limb of the ritual is prescribed for a specific deity, one should perform it under that deity's star. However, one should not perform the marriage, the first shaving, or the first feeding of solid food <note>original: "annaprashana"</note> during the transition of the Sun.

Regarding the primary and secondary parts of the ritual, it is said that if a mistake occurs in the main part, one should not perform it in that way. Here, in this secondary action, one should not look at the *Rada* <note>original: "rada" - referring to the number 32 or specific astrological teeth-related calculations</note> or the strength, but rather it must be carefully considered.

Regarding the wedding, he explains the specifics: The carving of gems, the decoration of the altar, the house, the gathering place, and the pavilions for exit and entry. All of these, including the wedding itself, should be performed within the center of the household. Regarding the wedding, he states a specific rule: Before the wedding day, or on the eighth, <margin>28 28</margin> or ninth day, one should avoid the *Rada* limb <note>a specific astrological division</note> as a limb of the wedding. The limbs of the wedding are as follows: The carving of gems, the preparation of fans and water, the pavilion, the altar, and the application of saffron color.

Now he describes the "Entry of the Bride" <term>Vadhu Pravesha - the ceremonial first entry of a bride into her husband's home</term> which has been attained. From the time of the wedding <term>Lagna - the specific astrological moment of the marriage</term>, within eight days, or on even days, or on the seventh, fifth, or ninth days. Beyond sixteen days, it should be on odd days, in odd months, or in odd years. After five years, one may go as they please. This is what the learned say. One should perform the bride's entry into the house at night, during a stable sign <note>an astrological sign that suggests permanence</note>, under the constellations of Shravana, Rohini, Uttaraphalguni, Uttarashadha, Uttarabhadrapada, Chitra, Swati, or Revati.

"From the Lagna" means: From the time of the wedding, within the interval of eight days or up to sixteen days, on even-numbered days. "Sages" refers to the seventh day; "Rada" refers to the fifth; "Anka" refers to the ninth. On these days, the entry is auspicious. However, beyond sixteen days, it should be on an odd-numbered day. Within the first months, it should be in an odd-numbered month. They have said the bride's entry should occur then. This is the rule. Within sixteen days, it must be done on even days. If sixteen days have passed, the bride's entry should be done on an odd day within the first month. If the first month has passed, it should be done in an odd-numbered month within the first year; <margin>28</margin> at that time, the rule for odd days does not apply. Likewise, if the first year has passed, it should be done in an odd-numbered year up to the fifth year. At that time, the rule for odd months does not apply. After the fifth year, there is no rule regarding odd years.

Now, he states in which month and fortnight the bride's entry should be performed: In the bright fortnight <note>the waxing moon</note> of the months of Vaishakha <note>April-May</note>, Phalguna <note>February-March</note>, or Margashirsha <note>November-December</note>.

By the two Shrutis <note>the lunar mansions Shravana and Dhanishta</note>, the twelve <note>referring to the twelve signs or specific lunar days</note>, and by the Ashvinis <note>the first lunar mansion, Ashwini</note>. Shrutiyuga <note>Shravana and Dhanishta</note> and the twelve are indeed these. Chitra is well-known. Ashwini is well-known. Ijya is Pushya <note>the eighth lunar mansion, associated with nourishment and the priest of the gods</note>. 

By these lunar mansions <term>Nakshatras: lunar mansions or star clusters used in Vedic astrology</term>, one should not cause a newly married girl to enter a house that is not new <note>an old or established house</note>. Because of this prohibition, it is said "in a house that is not new." 

And it is stated: 
->The entry of the bride should be performed on the fifth or seventh day, or on the ninth, on an auspicious day of the week, under a favorable planetary alignment <term>Lagna: the ascending sign</term> and when the Moon is strong.<-

Narada <note>a legendary sage and author of legal and astrological texts</note> says: 
->From the start, on the twelfth day, or when the Moon is strong on the eighth day, the entry of the bride leads to prosperity; likewise on the tenth or any even-numbered day.<-

The Compendium <note>Sangraha</note> states: 
->The entry of the bride should occur in the month of the marriage, on an even-numbered day within sixteen days. Beyond that, even in an odd month, it is permitted by the learned up to the fifth month; after that, there is no restrictive rule.<-

Narada stated: 
->If a woman enters the house in an even-numbered year or an even-numbered month <note>counting from the marriage</note>, she takes away the life of her husband, and that woman shall meet her death.<-

In the *Bhaskara-vyavahara* <note>The Practice of Bhaskara</note>: 
->At night, when the marriage stars are favorable, at an auspicious moment <term>Muhurta: a unit of time approximately 48 minutes long</term> and during a stable ascent, the entry of the bride is recommended. However, the wise do not perform it in the direction of Venus <note>facing the planet Venus/Shukra is considered inauspicious for travel</note>. Some say that the moment of the wedding <insert>marriage</insert> itself is always auspicious. The entry of the bride should be done on the fifth day if it is possible.<-

In the months of Margashirsha, Phalguna, or Vaishakha, during the bright fortnight on an auspicious day; under the mansions of Mrigashira, Pushya, the "soft" stars <note>Mridu nakshatras: Mrigashira, Chitra, Anuradha, and Revati</note>, Swati, or the stable signs, Shravana, or Dhanishta. If a woman enters in an odd month <note>the 1st, 3rd, or 5th month after the wedding</note>, she will be blessed with children.

Now, the author explains with a verse the results of a girl residing in specific houses where it is forbidden during the first years or months of marriage:

->If, after the wedding, the girl stays in her husband's house during the first month of Ashadha <note>June/July</note>, she kills her mother-in-law. If she stays there in the month of Jyeshtha <note>May/June</note>, she kills her husband's elder brother or herself. If she stays in her father's house during the "dirty" month <term>Maline: an intercalary or leap month in the lunar calendar</term> or in Chaitra <note>March/April</note>, she kills her own father. If these relatives are not present, there is no fear of these evils.<-

Regarding "after the wedding": From the time of the wedding, if the girl stays in the husband's house during the first Ashadha that occurs, she kills her mother-in-law <note>literally: "the mother of the husband"</note>. If she stays in the husband's house during the month of Jyeshtha, then her own... <note>text breaks off</note>

<margin>59
51</margin> The phrase "first union" <note>original: *prathama rati*</note> applies to all the following conditions. If a bride enters her husband's home in the month of Jyeshtha <note>the third month of the Hindu calendar, May–June</note> on an auspicious day, she may bring harm to her husband's elder brother. If she enters during the month of Jyeshtha, she may bring harm to her mother. If she resides in her husband's house during an intercalary month <note>original: *malina adhimasa*; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year, considered spiritually "unclean"</note>, she may bring harm to her husband. If a girl stays in her father's house during her first union in the month of Chaitra <note>March–April</note>, she brings harm to her father, her own progenitor. Now, the exception is stated: "It shall not be." In the absence of those mentioned—the husband's mother and others—there is no fear. This means if she resides in the husband's house when those relatives are not present, no danger shall occur.

Now, the Gandharva marriage <note>a marriage based on mutual consent and love without formal rituals</note> is discussed. Among Shudras and others, the marriage of a "twice-married" woman <note>original: *punarbhava*; a widow or woman marrying a second time</note> is declared according to the sequence of marriage rules. <margin>59</margin> Having observed the lunar day, month, planetary obstructions <note>term: *vedha*; an astronomical interference where one planet 'pierces' the influence of another</note>, and the setting of Jupiter and Venus, one calculates from the Sun's constellation <note>original: *arkabhat*</note>. The results are: death, wealth, intelligence, death, sons, misfortune, prosperity, and sorrow. These occur at the counts of 6, 8, 22, 4, 25, and so on, including the Abhijit constellation. Among Shudras—the well-known Shudra class and the lowest castes—the remarriage of a woman is described. By whom? By the clear sequence of marriage rites. In that remarriage, one must observe the lunar day, month, planetary obstructions, and the setting of Jupiter and Venus. The rest is clear. As it is stated in the *Jyotirnibandha* <note>a classical compendium of Vedic astrology</note>: "The setting of Venus and such need not be considered, nor the purity of planetary obstructions, in the remarriage of a woman; only the purification of the month and lunar day is required."

Now, the purification of the constellations <note>term: *nakshatra shuddhi*; ensuring the moon is in a favorable star mansion</note> is described by the word "Varusha." In the three groups of three, in order: death in the first three; wealth in the second; intelligence in the third; pleasure in the fourth; sons in the fifth; intelligence in the sixth; misfortune in the seventh; prosperity (Lakshmi) in the eighth; and sorrow in the ninth group of three. Now, the purification of the "Varusha" steps is explained, following from the Sun's constellation: 6, 8, 22, 25, 18, 11, and 4. At the twenty-fifth element, this is the count from the Sun's constellation. Including Abhijit, in those four, there is a conjunction with death. Now, the Sun is very auspicious. Now, the meaning of various scriptures is stated: A man who has enjoyed... <note>The text ends mid-sentence discussing the fruits of various ritual actions</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual purity and the timing of domestic ceremonies (Samskaras), specifically addressing how to handle births or deaths within the family lineage during a scheduled event.</meta>

In cases where a birth or death occurs within one's own lineage <note>original: "svagotra"</note>, how should one proceed? If the ritual has already begun, one should complete it. Others involved may partake in the meal <note>original: "vaja," interpreted here as the sacrificial food or strength</note>. One should commence these rites—such as weddings, the first hair-cutting <term>Choula: the tonsure ceremony</term>, or the investiture of the sacred thread <term>Mauji: the Upanayana ceremony</term>—after considering the auspicious days. Regarding the state of the person: a man who has fasted should give his daughter in marriage only after he has eaten. Similarly, a man who has fasted should impart the Savitri <note>the Gayatri mantra</note> to the young student <note>Vatu</note> only after eating. This is stated in the **Digest of Astrology** <note>Jyotirnibandha</note>:

> One should marry off a daughter and receive the Savitri mantra after having eaten. The father should give the daughter away after fasting, or to a groom who has arrived and been honored.

Another reading says "to a groom who has been worshipped." When a birth or death occurs in one's lineage during a festival that has already started—meaning the ritual work has commenced—one should complete that work in its entirety. Other people may prepare the food. In the **Garland of Meanings** <note>Dhwani Manjari</note>, it is said that "Vaja" means food, and it is also said to mean "strength." The rest is easily understood. It is also stated:

> If impurity <term>Sutaka: ritual pollution from birth or death</term> occurs after a marriage, sacrifice, or festival has begun, one should complete that work, including the distribution of food.

Before the determined day, one should have started the sacrifice, the wedding, the tonsure, or the thread ceremony. In these three <insert>weddings, tonsure, and initiation</insert>, the period of ritual beginning varies: for a sacrifice, it is twenty-one days; for a marriage, ten days; for the tonsure, three days; and for the sacred thread ceremony, three days. As it is said:

> For a sacrifice, twenty-one days; for a marriage, ten days; for tonsure and initiation, three days—this is when the Invocation of Ancestors <term>Nandi-Shraddha</term> is ordained.

Now, the definition of "commencement":

> Commencement is the choosing of the groom; in sacrifices and vows, it is the formal resolve <note>Sankalpa</note>; in marriages and similar rites, it is the Nandi-Shraddha; in funeral rites, it is the preparation of the food.

Regarding the Ancestral Invocation <note>Nandi-Shraddha</note>: members of the same lineage <gloss>those born of the same family</gloss> should not perform certain acts like the daily study of Vedas, bathing in cold water for purification, or crossing boundaries <note>the village limits</note> or great rivers during a period of astronomical disturbance <note>Vyatipata</note>. This restriction lasts until the dismantling of the ritual pavilion <note>Mandapa</note>.

<margin>30</margin>
Now, regarding when the pavilion should be dismantled, the ancient authorities like Krishna and Ganesha say: on the fifth or seventh day. In the case of the two days of the wedding, the fifth or seventh is preferred; even days are generally avoided, except the sixth. It is said: the dismantling of the pavilion should be done on an even day by the wise, but the sixth and odd days are not desired, except for the fifth and seventh. Furthermore: within one year, two marriages should not be performed in the same house for two siblings born of the same mother. Whether two men or two women, they should not perform the "equal ritual" <note>meaning the same type of ceremony, like two weddings or two initiations</note>. There is no fault if they are born of different mothers. So it is said in another book: for a man and woman born of the same mother, one should not perform the same ritual in one year; it is permitted if the mothers are different. "Equal ritual" refers to two marriages or two thread ceremonies <note>term: "vratabandha" - the Upanayana or initiation rite</note>. By "siblings," it is possible they are born of the same mother. Within one year, two marriages in a house must not be done. It is said: in no way should two house-marriages be done in a single year.

<margin>Self</margin>
Regarding the sequence of marriage, initiation, and tonsure <note>term: "cūḍā" - the first haircutting ceremony</note>: once the marriage is completed, the "earth-softening" <note>original: "mṛddala" - likely referring to the ritual handling of earth or clay for the ceremony</note> remains valid for one month. During this time, one should not perform the libations with sesame seeds or the offerings with rice balls <note>term: "piṇḍa" - cooked rice balls offered to ancestors</note>, except for the Great Departure <note>original: "mahālaya" - the fortnight of the ancestors in the month of Bhadrapada</note>, the pilgrimage to Gaya, or the anniversary of a parent's death. Gaya is famous for this, and the parental anniversary is the day of the mother's or father's passing. On those occasions, the offering with sacred grass is performed. It is said: for one year after marriage, initiation, or tonsure,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological prohibitions and auspicious timing for life-cycle rituals, specifically focusing on the dangers of certain birth stars and the spacing of family ceremonies.</meta>

There is no marriage for two born of the same womb <note>Sanskrit: *sahajātayoḥ*; referring to siblings</note>, nor should a marriage and a tonsure ceremony <note>Sanskrit: *vrata* or *mauñjī*; the sacred thread initiation</note> occur within the same period. In the auspicious rite of marriage, one should not perform another auspicious rite <unclear>within three seasons</unclear>. Thus, it is said regarding the young woman:

As stated, a woman born under certain stars brings destruction. As Garga <note>An ancient sage and authority on astrology</note> says: "A girl born in the *Mūla* star destroys her father-in-law; one born in *Āśleṣā* destroys her mother-in-law. <margin>A girl born in *Jyeṣṭhā* destroys her husband's elder brother.</margin>" So too in the *Paṭala* <note>A section of a ritual manual</note>: the last quarter of *Mūla* and the first quarter of *Āśleṣā* are considered harmful. However, there is an exception: if the father-in-law or the specific relative is not alive, there is no fault <note>Sanskrit: *doṣa*</note>. Some teachers say this applies only after the groom's sacred thread ceremony. Narada <note>A divine sage and legal authority</note> states: "The faults of being born in *Mūla* or *Āśleṣā* mentioned for marriage only apply to men after their thread ceremony; some say this is not a factor before then."

Regarding the timing of multiple ceremonies, it is said: "No two marriages." For two children born of the same womb—whether two sons, two daughters, or a son and a daughter—marriages should not be performed within three seasons <note>approximately six months</note>. Narada says: "If two marriages of daughters occur within six months, one will undoubtedly become a widow within three years." 

Furthermore, one should not perform a sacred thread ceremony <term>Mauñjī-bandhana</term> <note>The initiation ritual for young boys</note> following a marriage. Narada states: "After a son's marriage, a daughter's marriage should not occur within three seasons, nor should a thread ceremony or any other auspicious rite follow a marriage." Katyayana <note>A renowned Vedic scholar and grammarian</note> also confirms: "After the marriage of a son, the marriage of a daughter [should be delayed]..."

# On the Triple Auspicious Rites

Regarding the "Triple Auspicious Rites" <note>original: "Mangala-traya"</note>, according to the rules of auspicious ceremonies, one should not perform the <term>Three Deaths</term> <note>Mrita-traya: likely referring to a specific sequence of funerary or inauspicious rites that conflict with celebrations</note> during a marriage, the tonsure ceremony <note>Choula</note>, or the sacred thread ceremony <note>Vrata/Upanayana</note>. On this matter, the sage Katyayana states: In the case of the triple auspicious rites, the decoration of the altar for the gods should not be done. In these three, the triple girdle <note>mekhala</note> and the <term>Five-fold Triple Death</term> should not be performed. The "Triple Auspicious Rites" refers to the collection of three auspicious events. According to the words of Katyayana, "One should not perform the triple auspicious rites." 

Furthermore, Paraskara states: "One should not perform a minor rite after a major one has been initiated." In the case of the sacred thread ceremony and the tonsure, the decoration <note>Mandana</note> must be performed on both sides of the auspicious event. If a marriage has commenced, or if there is a conflict or a crisis, one should perform the previously mentioned auspicious rites after an interval of four days, or after a day has passed, following a ritual bath to remove any impurity.

"Not a minor rite after a major one" means that after the "Great Auspicious Rite" (such as the marriage of an eldest child), a "Minor Auspicious Rite" should not be performed immediately. If there is a ceremony in the hall, the major one takes precedence. Why is this? Except in cases of "Time-driven necessity" <note>Kala-hruta: rites that must be performed at a specific biological or astrological time, such as conception (Garbhadhana) or the rite for a male child (Pumsavana)</note>. In those cases, it is permitted. 

Thus Katyayana says: After performing the <term>Mother-Worship</term> <note>Matri-yajna: a preliminary rite honoring the divine mothers/ancestresses</note> and the major auspicious rite, one should not perform a minor rite during the "Triple Death" period. Whether minor or major, nothing should be done except what is seasonally required. Paraskara agrees with Katyayana on this point. Regarding the tonsure <note>Choula</note> and the sacred thread ceremony <note>Mounji</note>, the decoration must be done for both. As the text says: "The decoration and the tonsure are stated; in the vow of marriage, the tonsure and decoration are prescribed." One should avoid anything else. Where there is the sacred thread, there is its decoration.

Now, regarding the practice during a crisis: If there is a "Difference of the Year" <note>Bheda-trusya: a change in the lunar or solar year</note> or a crisis caused by the region, time, or fear, one may extend or perform the previously mentioned auspicious rite. As stated in the <term>Summary of the Compendium</term> <note>Samhita Saravali: a classical astrological and ritual text</note>: "In the months of Phalguna or Chaitra, the marriage of a son or the initiation should be done even if there is a change in the year..." 32

It outlines exceptions for time-sensitive rites and provides guidance for performing ceremonies during periods of crisis or year-end transitions.</summary>

# On the Timing of Auspicious Rites

The crossing of three lunar mansions <note>original: "nakshatra-traya-langhanam"; refers to the astrological transition between specific star clusters</note> must be observed. Alternatively, one may perform an auspicious rite <term>Mangalam: a general term for a lucky or religious ceremony, here specifically marriage</term> after an interval of four days. Thus, it is recalled in the *Smritisaravali* <note>A classical compendium of remembered laws</note>:

-> "As long as four days have not passed, one should not perform another marriage ceremony." <-

Alternatively, one may perform the rite with an interval of just one day. As it has been said: "A man should never perform a marriage for those born of the same womb <term>Ekodara: literally 'from the same belly'; biological siblings</term> on the same day, nor should the preparation of the marriage pavilions <term>Mandapa: a temporary porch or pavilion set up for religious ceremonies</term> overlap."

Or, if in a different region, such as on a mountain or across a river, one may perform the rite even on the same day. According to the *Jyotirvivarana* <note>An astronomical/astrological treatise</note>: "The marriage of two grooms born of the same womb on the same day leads to death; some say even a one-day interval is required, yet in times of distress or emergency <term>Sankata: a crisis or unavoidable circumstance that allows for the relaxation of religious laws</term>, it is considered auspicious."

Sharangadhara <note>A famous 14th-century scholar</note> also states: "Even with an interval, it is seen as auspicious. Even on the same day, if the parties are in different locations or different pavilions, one may perform the rite." The *Amarakosha* <note>A famous Sanskrit lexicon</note> defines a pavilion as a place of shelter for women. It is further stated in the *Jyotirvivarana*: "It is not forbidden when the right astrological moment <term>Lagna: the ascendant sign or specific auspicious moment</term> is reached during a great crisis; for those born of the same womb, the rite may be done on the same day if in different pavilions."

<margin>Prasara 5</margin> Now, the decision regarding the "doubling" of rites is explained. The wise say that in matters of marriage, this "doubling" or difference should be applied according to the degree of the situation; it should be applied until the crisis is resolved.

Now, the specific rule for twins <term>Yamala: twins</term> is explained. For twins, this "doubling" or separation is not prohibited. For the auspicious rites of twins, the ceremony should be performed under the same lunar mansion, in the same year, and in the same pavilion. This should be applied here. As it is said: "For twins, the marriage should be performed in the same year, on the same day, and in the same pavilion, according to the fears or desires of their own women."

Now, the prohibition regarding the "Direct Door" <note>likely referring to immediate family succession or specific ritual entrances</note> is explained: "One should not marry two daughters to the same man, nor should one give two daughters in a single ceremony; one should not perform the marriage of a brother and sister together without doubt."

"Not in one"—this means one should not give two daughters to a single groom. For those born of the same...

Furthermore: "The father, maternal grandfather, mother, paternal grandmother, the paternal uncle's wife, a son, a brother, or an unmarried sister—the death of any of these is remembered by the wise as an adverse circumstance."
