...so that there should be no obstruction. **Now, the opinions of others are stated:** Others, however, maintain that even if a death occurs among one's own lineage or kinsmen, the marriage should not be [delayed indefinitely]. **As Manu says:** <note>referring to the Laws of Manu, a foundational legal text</note> "If a death occurs in either of the two families after the betrothal <term>Vāgdāna; literally 'giving of the word'</term> has taken place, then the marriage must not be performed due to the defect of the 'two-rival' <note>original: "sapatna-dvaya-doṣataḥ"; likely referring to the conflict between the auspicious wedding and the inauspicious mourning</note>."

**Now, the procedure in times of crisis is stated:** Regarding the death of father, mother, wife, son, daughter, or a member of the family—these are considered "adverse factors" <term>Pratikūla; literally 'against the grain' or 'obstruction'</term>. Upon their death—meaning their passing—under what circumstances can a marriage be performed? Wise men ordain that in a crisis <term>Saṅkaṭa</term>, one should perform the marriage after a certain interval and after performing a propitiatory rite <term>Shāntika</term> and the worship of the Goddess Gauri.

The meaning is as follows:
- Upon the death of the father of the groom or bride, [wait] one year.
- Upon the death of the mother, half of that: six months.
- Upon the death of a wife <note>referring to a previous wife in the case of remarriage</note>, half of that: three months. This is to be understood for second marriages and the like.
- Upon the death of a son, half of that: one and a half months.
- Upon the death of other family members besides the father, mother, wife, or son, the interval is half of that: twenty-three days.

Regarding the specific case of a brother among "others": The death of a brother is to be treated like that of a son. The meaning is that it should be performed after an interval of one and a half months. **As stated in the *Jewel-Necklace of Tradition*:** <note>original: "Smṛtiratnāvalyā"</note> "For the father, the period of impurity is a year; half of that for a brother or son; for other kinsmen <term>Sapiṇḍas; those sharing the same funeral cake</term>, it is measured as one month. In such cases, having performed the propitiatory rite, the auspicious time for the wedding <term>Lagna</term> is then enjoined."

**It is also said in another Tradition:** When a crisis has arrived, the **Yogi Yajnavalkya** <note>a famous sage and legal authority</note> has prescribed a propitiatory rite. Having performed the rite for Lord Ganesha, one should carry out the auspicious event. If one does not perform the propitiatory rite when a prohibition exists, and carries out an auspicious event like a marriage, it leads to harsh consequences. **In the *Light of Astrology*:** <note>original: "Jyotiḥprakāśe"</note> Even when conditions are adverse, a marriage may be performed after one month, provided the propitiatory rite [is done].

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on ritual timing and obstacles (Pratikula) in the context of marriage and domestic rites, specifically addressing exceptions to mourning periods.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

<margin>Om 34</margin>

Having performed the <term>Vagdana</term><note>The formal betrothal or "giving of the word" before the actual wedding ceremony</note>, if the parents should pass away when the daughter has already reached maturity, one should proceed with the rites. Regarding unfavorable circumstances, it is said: in times of fear—such as fear of famine or a king—or when there is a danger to life, or upon the death of the parents, the usual restrictions regarding "unfavorable timing" caused by the death of other relatives do not apply. Thus it is stated in the *Ocean of Astrology* (<note>original: "Jyotih-sagara"</note>): "In times of famine, the ruin of the nation, or when the parents' lives are in danger, or if the daughter is already mature, one does not wait for the end of the mourning period of other family members."

Here, further exceptions are mentioned: there is no "unfavorable timing" (<term>Pratikulata</term>) in the case of those who are chronically ill, those who have renounced worldly life, or those who have gone to a distant land. "Chronically ill" refers to those suffering long-term ailments; "Renounced" refers to those indifferent to the home and planetary influences; "Gone to a distant land" refers to those who have traveled far away (as the *Amarakosha* dictionary defines *ara* as both far and near). The death of such persons does not create a ritual obstacle. As stated in the *Smriti-uttara*: "For one overwhelmed by long-term disease, one residing in a distant land, or one who has lived as an ascetic, no ritual obstacle (<term>Pratikula</term>) exists."

Now, regarding other family lineages, the scripture says: if a man who is a "lineage-bearer" (<note>original: "atryah," referring to a primary male ancestor or descendant</note>) dies, and his funeral rites have not been completed, or if for some other reason the rites are pending, one must not perform the <term>Nandimukha</term><note>A joyful ancestral offering performed at the start of auspicious events like weddings</note>. This applies to those within the same lineage (<term>Gotra</term>). Thus says the sage Medhatithi: "One who performs an auspicious rite while the funeral rites of a deceased relative are still pending seeks his own ruin; such a rite will not bring good fortune."

Furthermore, one should not perform a marriage or auspicious rite for someone suffering from a fever. As the sage Garga says: "One should not perform the ceremony for someone while a fever is manifesting; only after the affliction has left and the person is purified should one perform their religious duties." This also applies to the outbreak of any severe epidemic that might touch the family.

<summary>This page outlines specific exemptions for performing marriages and religious rites during mourning periods, noting that emergencies, chronic illness, or renunciation waive the usual delays. It also warns against performing joyful ceremonies like the Nandimukha while funeral rites are pending or while the subject is ill with fever.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on timing rituals and navigating astrological or ritual obstacles, specifically addressing how to proceed when illness or ritual impurity (sutaka) conflicts with planned ceremonies.</meta>

<margin>63</margin> ->**On the Determination of Severe Fever**<-
One should not proceed with certainty when a severe fever arises <note>original: "daruna-jvara"; a high or dangerous fever</note> due to the fear of adverse results. The disease, known as fever, is also called "the tormentor," "the malady," "the transformation," "suffering," and "the sickness," according to the authority Vagbhata <note>A famous classical scholar of Ayurveda</note>. Thus, Vanga also states: "If a severe fever strikes even a single person, the wise should not perform any auspicious work or make firm determinations."

Now, regarding what should be done in a great crisis, the elders have spoken: "If ritual impurity <note>original: "sutaka"; impurity arising from birth or death in the family</note> occurs at any time, the wise should not begin the work. However, in a crisis, one may proceed after the end of a ten-day period generated by the Kushmandi ghee oblation." <note>Kushmandi-ghrita-homa: A specific Vedic purification ritual involving clarified butter and verses from the Yajurveda to remove sin or impurity.</note>

"If it should be so," refers to all times mentioned—sacrifices, weddings, tonsure ceremonies, or the sacred thread ceremony. In all <margin>3</margin> times, if the work has not yet begun and a crisis arrives, one should perform the ghee oblation with the Kushmandi verses. By this ritual, and by giving the prescribed priestly gifts, the work may be performed after the ten-day period of impurity. As stated in another legal treatise <note>original: "Smriti"</note>: "When a crisis has arrived and ritual impurity has coincided with it, one should offer the Kushmandi ghee oblation and give a milch cow; then one may perform the sacred thread ceremony, marriage, or consecration rituals." To purify a work not yet started, one should sacrifice with the Kushmandi verses, give a cow, and subsist on cow products; then the impure person becomes purified.

Next, he describes the prohibition of auspicious acts when Jupiter is in Leo <note>original: "Simhejya"</note>. When Jupiter is in the zodiac sign of Leo, auspicious acts—other than those that are daily necessities—are not beneficial. This has been explained in detail in the *Determination of Time* <note>original: "Kala-nirnaya"</note>: "One should not perform the first feeding, tonsure, journeys, consecrations, or weddings; all auspicious matters are forbidden when Jupiter is situated in Leo."

<summary>This page discusses the suspension of auspicious ceremonies due to severe illness or ritual impurity, providing the Kushmandi ghee sacrifice as a remedial purification. It also notes the astrological prohibition of major life events while Jupiter resides in the sign of Leo.</summary>

Regarding the phrase "those two" <note>referring to specific planetary positions</note>, Mandavya states: <gloss>For eight years, because the rites of tonsure and similar sacraments have not been performed, one should perform the sacrificial ladle-work and other auspicious deeds even when Jupiter is in Leo.</gloss> <margin>The</margin>

Regarding the phrase "those two," he now explains the practice during times of crisis. Regarding the phrase "the portion of Vishnu" <note>referring to the sun's transit through certain signs</note>: in some cases of crisis—due to the specific region, time, or fear—even when Jupiter is in Leo, the "portion of Vishnu" <note>the period when the sun is in Aries, etc.</note> is considered. South of the Gautami <note>the Godavari River</note> and north of the Jahnavi <note>the Ganges River</note>, auspicious work is beneficial. Thus, it is said in the *Jyotirnibandha*: "When Jupiter is in Leo, or in the Leo Navamsha <note>a specific 1/9th division of the zodiac</note>, for those born on the south bank of the Godavari, if there is fear of the fault of missing the proper time for marriage, one may perform the wedding; so says Vana." 

And so says Garga: "In the region north of the Bhagirathi <note>Ganges</note> and south of the Godavari, rites such as vows and weddings are not corrupted even when Jupiter is in Leo." By implication, between these two rivers—north of the Godavari and south of the Bhagirathi—one should not perform auspicious rites even in a crisis. Thus says Vasishtha: "On the north bank of the Godavari and west <note>or south/downstream</note> of the Bhagirathi, one should not perform weddings and such while Jupiter is in Leo." 

Even there, he explains the practice in cases of extreme crisis with the word "Saturn." In the presence of fears such as weapons or foreign invasion, between the Godavari and the Jahnavi, the "portion of Vishnu" is used; while Jupiter is in Leo, if Saturn is in Capricorn and the Sun is in Aries, one should perform auspicious acts. The condition of "Saturn in Capricorn" refers specifically to the subject of marriage, as indicated by the phrase "fear of weapons and such." 

Thus says Shaunaka: "When the Maker of Day <note>the Sun</note> is in Aries, and Jupiter is in Leo, she whose marriage is performed with a composed mind shall be a virtuous woman and attain wealth and prosperity." However, the binding of the sacred thread <note>Upanayana</note> must not be done in the month of Chaitra when the Sun is in Pisces, as stated previously: "It should not be done when the Sun is in the sign of the Fish <note>Pisces</note>." And so it is said in the chapter on the Sacred Thread (*Maunjipatala*): "When Jupiter is in the tenth house from the birth sign, or in Leo, or in a debilitated or enemy sign, the binding of the sacred thread is declared auspicious in the month of Vaishakha..."

When the desired lunar mansion <note>Nakshatra</note> is seen in the second or third position from the "Ketu" <note>the south node of the moon, here referred to as 'Shikhi'</note>, that position is considered auspicious for all undertakings. Thus, Saunaka states: "When Jupiter is in Leo and the Sun is in Aries, one may perform auspicious works." Narada and Parasara also say this. Regarding the placement of the "Ketu-earth" <note>likely a specific calculation involving the shadow planet Ketu and elemental positions</note> and its influence on food and other matters, one must observe the sight, wings, and feet <note>technical divisions of a planetary influence</note> to perform the task. In times of great crisis, this should be understood even in the absence of such favorable stars.

### Determination of the Name Sign and Birth Sign

Now, the author explains the decision between the Name Sign <term>Nama Rashi; the zodiac sign based on the first letter of a person's common name</term> and the Birth Sign <term>Janma Rashi; the zodiac sign the moon was in at the exact moment of birth</term>. 

In matters concerning:
*   Countries, villages, and houses
*   Fevers <note>illness</note> and legal disputes
*   Gambling and giving gifts
*   Mantras <note>sacred chants</note> and service <note>employment or royal service</note>
*   The *Kakini* <note>a specific mathematical calculation for compatibility</note>
*   The *Varga* <note>alphabetical groupings</note>
*   Battles
*   The meeting of a son and a master

In these cases, the Name Sign is primary. Aside from these, the Birth Sign is primary. If, between a bride and a groom, the birth stars are the same, it is considered auspicious; if they are opposite, it is considered based on the Name Signs.

Regarding "Country," etc.: "Country" is well-known; "Village" is well-known; "House" is well-known. In "Fever" and so on, the Name Sign is the principal factor. This means: for entering or matching with countries, villages, or houses; for observing the Moon's position during a fever; for legal conduct; for gambling; for giving gifts like the *Tulapurusha* <note>donating one's weight in gold or grain</note> to determine the strength of the Moon; for matching a Mantra; for royal service; for observing the *Kakini*; for purity of the *Varga*; for the strength of the Moon in battle; and for the meeting of a son and a master—for all these, the Name Sign should be consulted.

Beyond these, in marriage, journeys, and similar events, the Birth Sign is primary. As stated in the *Jyotirnibandha* <note>a famous 16th-century astrological compendium</note>: "In country, village, house, war, service, and legal disputes, the Name Sign is primary; one should not consider the Birth Sign." Elsewhere it says: "In *Kakini*, *Varga* purity, giving, gambling, fever, mercy, mantras, and the choosing of a master by a son, the Name Sign is primary. In marriage, all auspicious ceremonies, journeys, and the transit of planets <term>Graha Gochara</term>, the Birth Sign is primary; one should not consider the Name Sign."

Now, if between the bride and groom the birth star of one is known but the other is not known, how then should the purity of the match be observed?

<insert>Clearly</insert> <note>The inserted syllable 'spa' likely abbreviates 'spashtam', meaning 'clearly' or 'explained'</note>

It cites authorities like Narada and the Jyotirnibandha to prioritize the Birth Sign for major life events like marriage and travel, while reserving the Name Sign for civic and daily matters.</summary>

It is said that if the <term>birth star</term> <note>Sanskrit: Janmarksha; the lunar mansion or Nakshatra the moon was in at birth.</note> is known for one of the couple (the bride or the groom) but not for the other, then the compatibility should be determined by looking at the birth star of the one whose details are known and the <term>name-star</term> <note>Sanskrit: Namarksha; a star assigned based on the first syllable of a person's given name.</note> of the other. If the birth stars of both are unknown, then the <term>Varna</term> <note>The 'class' or 'caste' of the star, used as one of the eight points of compatibility.</note> and other factors like the <term>Vashya</term> <note>Mutual attraction/control between signs.</note> should be examined for both based solely on their names. As stated by the sage Sharngadhara:

> In the arrangement of a marriage, the strength of the <term>ascendant</term> <note>Lagna; the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon.</note> and the planets should be considered; if the birth details are not known, everything should be considered from the name.

Now, regarding the knowledge of the star based on the name for the purpose of matching: the first letter of the name is used. From the letters of the <term>Avakahada</term> system <note>A traditional grid used to assign Nakshatra quarters to specific phonetic sounds.</note>, the star is determined. If there is a common name or a nickname, the first letter of that name is used to find the corresponding star. For example, from the verse beginning with "Chu, Che, Cho, La," the star is known from the cycle. For a name like "Chudamani," the first letter is "Chu." According to the cycle "Chu, Che, Cho, La" corresponds to the first quarter of the <term>Ashwini</term> star. This is how it should be understood. Thus, it is said in the *Deepika*:

> For those whose birth details are not known, the star is sought through the name; in the <term>Avakahada</term> cycle, the <term>Nadi</term> <note>One of the most important compatibility factors, relating to the 'pulse' or temperament (Vata, Pitta, Kapha).</note> is also determined.

When the star is sought through the name, some say the <term>Nadi</term> should be calculated starting from the star <term>Krittika</term>. However, others consider this view unacceptable. Therefore, the view of the elders is that the <term>Nadi</term> should be observed starting from <term>Ashwini</term> <note>The first star of the zodiac.</note>, following the established rule. 

Next, he explains what should be done if the first letter of the name is a <term>conjunct consonant</term> <note>Sanskrit: Samyuktakshara; two or more consonants joined without an intervening vowel.</note>. From the "joined letters," in a name like "Pradyumna," where there is a conjunct at the beginning, the very first phonetic component is to be understood as the primary letter. For example, in the name "Pradyumna," the first letter is "Pa" <note>The 'P' sound within the 'Pr' cluster.</note>. This is how one's star is known. Similarly, in names like "Priyavachana," the letter "Pi" is the basis.

# On the Consumption of Syllables and Name Selection

**Consumption:** The production of a secondary sound, specifically the sound 'A', is known as "consumption" <note>Sanskrit: *grāsa*; in this context, it refers to the phonetic absorption or substitution of vowels</note>. Thus, in the *Science of Sound* <note>original: *Svara-shastra*; a branch of traditional phonetics and divination</note>, regarding sounds born from subsequent conjunctions, where there are two initial letters <margin>∟</margin> as seen in the **Avakahada Chakra** <note>A traditional grid used to match astrological signs with the first letters of a person's name</note>.

The text asks: what should be done when the initial letter of a name is missing from the grid? It states that 'Va' and 'Ba' are considered equal. Here, 'Sha' and 'Sa', as well as 'Kha' and 'Sha', are also equal. <margin>∟</margin> Where the letter 'Ba' occurs, 'Va' may be used. Similarly, 'Sha' and 'Sa' are interchangeable with 'Kha' and 'Sha'. 

An example regarding a mantra is shown: if the name is **Shiva**, and the letter 'Sha' is missing from the *Avakahada* grid, one should accept the letter 'Sa' in place of 'Sha'. Thus, by making 'Kha' and 'Sha' mutually similar in sound <term>homophonous: sounds that are pronounced the same way</term>, the calculation should be performed. As stated in the *Science of Sound*: "Kha and Sha, Sha and Sa, and Va and Ba are considered identical by those who know the colors of sounds." Furthermore, if these letters appear at the beginning of a name, they do not create a difference in the result; they should be understood in their respective order. The result of the grid is exactly as stated <margin>∟</margin> in the verses beginning with "The connection of the letters of the quarters," which are well-known in the *Avakahada* system of the *Science of Sound*.

Now, a doubt is raised: if a person has many names, which name should be used for the calculation? <margin>॥</margin>

> **Regarding multiple names:** For a person who has many names, the name that is most "prosperous" <note>meaning the name by which the person is most successfully known or the name given at a significant initiation</note> should be taken. 

As it is said in the *Science of Sound*: "If a man should somehow have many names, the experts in sound should perform the calculation using the name by which he is most commonly called."

Next, the teacher speaks regarding the auspiciousness of **Birth Constellations** <note>original: *Janma-nakshatra*; the lunar mansion the moon was in at the time of birth</note>. For marriage, do not perform the wedding <margin>∟</margin> if the birth constellation is not pure. This applies to the movement of all living beings in the world. 

Likewise, the birth constellation is considered for:
* **Hand-binding:** <note>referring to marriage or formal agreements</note>
* **Housing:** The building or first entry into a house.
* **Village:** The planning or first entry into a village.
* **Consecration:** The coronation of a King.
* **Agriculture:** Farming activities.
* **Tonsure:** The sacred thread ceremony <note>original: *Maunji*</note>.
* **First Feeding:** The first time a child eats solid food <note>original: *Annaprashana*</note>.
* **Adornment:** The wearing of fine jewelry.

It also lists life events, such as marriage and coronations, that require an auspicious birth constellation.</summary>

The wise have declared that the combination of the six months <note>the auspicious half of the year or Uttarayana</note>, the lunar fortnights, and the birth star <term>Janmabha; the constellation the moon was in at one's birth</term> bestows auspiciousness. Thus, it is stated in the *Light of Astrology* <note>original: Jyotiprakasha</note>: "The birth star is auspicious for the coronation of a king, for city-related works, for assuming authority, for the thread ceremony <term>Maunjibandhana; Upanayana</term>, and for marriage, as declared by the learned." In the *Royal Sun* <note>original: Rajamartanda</note>: "In the spring season, when there is a desire for rain, the birth month and the birth star are also considered desirable." 

Furthermore, Narada says: "The birth star is auspicious for the binding of the turban <note>investiture</note>, the first hair-cutting <term>Chaula</term>, the first feeding of solids <term>Annaprashana</term>, and the initiation ceremony <term>Upanayana</term>, but it is inauspicious for one specific rite <note>likely referring to certain funeral or restrictive rites</note>." 

Thus ends the Chapter on Marriage in the *Beloved of the Sun* <note>original: Martanda Vallabha</note>, which is a commentary on the *Sun of Auspicious Moments* <note>original: Muhurtamartanda</note>.

Now, regarding the Establishment of the Sacred Fires <term>Agnyadhana; the ritual of lighting the domestic or sacrificial fires</term>. Some have stated that the establishment of the fire should occur at the time of marriage <note>original: dara-samaye</note>, or at the time of the division of inheritance <note>original: dayada-kale</note>. As the wise say: "The establishment of the fire should be done when Jupiter and the Moon are strong, in the ascendant or the ninth house, with other planets in the houses of increase <term>Upachaya; the 3rd, 6th, 10th, and 11th houses</term>." 

The establishment of the fire <note>Agnyadhana</note> is declared by the learned to be performed at the "time of the wife" <note>marriage</note>, specifically after the rites of the fourth day of marriage. Others say it should be at the time of the division of wealth among heirs. Thus Brihaspati says: "The establishment of the fire is ordained at the time of marriage; some say it should be at the time of inheritance." So also says the teacher Katyayana: "The establishment of the domestic fire occurs at marriage or upon the division of inheritance, according to some." This has been settled by the commentators: if the father is deceased, it is at the time of marriage; if the father is living, it is at the time of inheritance. It is stated in the Law Books <term>Smritis</term> that one who is married should not remain even for a moment without the sacred fire. For it is said: "One who has taken a wife must not stay for a single moment without the fire."

One should be devoted to the <term>Pitri-yajna</term><note>Sacrifice or offerings made to the ancestors</note> and likewise remain dedicated to the rites for the mother. One must be steadfast in charity and the study of the <term>Vedas</term><note>The oldest and most authoritative Hindu scriptures</note>, and should never be without the sacred fire. One should worship the Sun. Regarding a wife, it is understood that desire for another's wife is forbidden, even if she is accepted as one's own. For a Brahmin, the <term>Sandhya</term><note>The twilight ritual performed three times a day</note> and the study of the Veda are essential; in that worship, there is no standing still, as established by the authors of the ritual codes such as Katyayana and others over a long period of time.

Now, regarding the establishment of the fires (<term>Agnyadhana</term>): it should be done under specific lunar mansions (<term>Nakshatras</term>). These include the "fixed" stars and the "soft" stars: <insert>specifically</insert> Vishakha, Mrigashira, Krittika, and the three "fixed" ones (the Uttaras) and Rohini; also Shakra (Jyeshtha) and Paushna (Revati). Mrigashira and the others are well-known. The ascendant (<term>Lagna</term>) and the divisional signs should be free of impurities. The "watery" signs are the second half of Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces.
<margin>Yoga</margin>
Cancer is also a watery sign, and the Moon is water-born. This applies to the ascendant and the <term>Navamsha</term><note>A one-ninth division of a zodiac sign used for precision in Vedic astrology</note>. Thus, for a purified vow, one should use Cancer, Capricorn, Pisces, or their divisions.

The planets involved are Jupiter, the Moon, the Sun, and Mars. <note>original: "Jiva-Indu-Arka-Kuja"</note> Jupiter is Guru, Indu is the Moon, Arka is the Sun, and Kuja is Mars. These should be placed in the seventh, ninth, or second houses, or in the <term>Upachaya</term> houses—which are the third, sixth, eleventh, and tenth houses. These planets must not be "combust" <note>original: "astagata"</note>, meaning their power should not be lost by being too close to the rays of the Sun. Furthermore, they should be in their own signs, their signs of exaltation, or in the signs of friendly planets. Other planets should also be positioned in the <term>Upachaya</term> houses (3, 6, 11) to ensure success.

When the perfect accomplishment is achieved, the *Lagna* <note>the ascendant or specific zodiacal moment</note> should be purified of any cruel influences. When that is achieved, it is auspicious as previously stated. One should generally avoid the aforementioned aspects. As stated in the *Ratna Jnana*: when Jupiter, the teacher of the gods, is in the center <note>quadrant</note>, the third house, or the trine, it is beneficial. <margin>Doubtful reading?</margin> Regarding the *Antyadhana* <note>the final establishment of the fire</note>, it is said to be auspicious. If the Moon is in the eighth house, one might face death; if Mars is in an auspicious house, it is favorable. For the rest, if they are in the third, sixth, or eleventh houses and combined with auspicious planets, the performer of the *Antyadhana* becomes successful. One should not perform this if the planets are in their debilitation, in the house of an enemy, or are combust <note>too close to the sun</note>. So also in another *Samhita* <note>collection of laws</note>: one should not perform the *Agnyadhana* <term>Consecration of the Sacred Fires: the ritual of establishing the three Vedic fires</term> when the Moon is in Cancer, Leo, or Virgo, or if the *Lagna* is occupied by malefic planets.

### Here ends the section on the Consecration of the Sacred Fires.

### Now begins the section on Housing.

Regarding the rules for the division of the home, he speaks of the "Name-Direction." From the zodiac sign of one's name, the direction is determined. Having thus explained the auspiciousness of the village, he now explains the measurement of the village directions and the peace of its corners. In those specific corners, for those living there—such as the *Karmakas* <note>laborers/artisans</note> and others—establishing a residence will be auspicious. As stated by sages like Kashyapa: the bamboo-workers, weavers, hunters, and outcaste groups are remembered as those who should reside together. In this way, the corner of the village is auspicious for them. For other castes living in those corners, there would be many faults or inauspicious results. So it is said in the *Jyotir-Nibandha* <note>a classical astrological compendium</note>: those who live in the corners of houses or towns will face faults, except for the aforementioned groups who find success there. <margin>38</margin> Having thus stated the division of auspicious and inauspicious for the various classes, he speaks of the residence according to the Moon's path, starting with Virgo and the southern direction.

By following the sequence of the dice-throw <note>original: "prāsakāṇatakrameṇa," referring to a method of divination or mathematical distribution</note>, the eight directions are inhabited by the zodiac signs beginning with Virgo, Cancer, and Sagittarius. The others reside in the center. This is what is meant: having calculated the ninth-part <note>the Navamsha, a 3°20' subdivision of a zodiac sign</note> of the desired name, if it falls in the middle portion, then in the southern direction, Virgo resides. This means the city should be constructed in the southern part of the Virgo sign.

Similarly, Cancer resides in the Southwest, Sagittarius in the West, Libra in the Northwest, Taurus and Aries in the North, and Capricorn and Aquarius in the Northeast. Likewise, Scorpio resides in the [unspecified] corner, and Pisces resides in the Southeast. The others—Gemini, Leo, and Capricorn—reside in the very center. In this way, the directions are connected to the zodiac signs.

Now, he describes the placement of the directions according to the "classes" <term>Varga: the phonetic groups of the Sanskrit alphabet used to determine auspicious names</term>. Starting from the direction of Indra <note>the East</note>, the eight classes are positioned in the eight directions. These are the well-known classes: A, Ka, Ca, Ta, Ta, Pa, Ya, and Sha. As stated in the verse, these are powerful and radiant.

This is what is meant:
*   **East:** The vowel class (A, Ā, I, Ī, U, Ū, Ṛ, Ḷ, etc.).
*   **Southeast:** The Ka-class (Ka, Kha, Ga, Gha, Ṅa).
*   **South:** The Ca-class (Ca, Cha, Ja, Jha, Ña).
*   **Southwest:** The Ṭa-class (Ṭa, Ṭha, Ḍa, Ḍha, Ṇa).
*   **West:** The Ta-class (Ta, Tha, Da, Dha, Na).
*   **Northwest:** The Pa-class (Pa, Pha, Ba, Bha, Ma).
*   **North:** The Ya-class (Ya, Ra, La, Va).
*   **Northeast:** The Śa-class (Śa, Ṣa, Sa, Ha).

The meaning is this: after identifying the phonetic class of the first letter of one's own name, one should build the city in the corresponding location. This is because it is famous throughout the three worlds that living beings are powerful in their own proper place.

What are the characteristics of these classes? They are "enemies" to the fifth class from themselves. For example, the fifth class from the A-class is the Ta-class; therefore, the Ta-class is its enemy. The fifth from the Ka-class is the Pa-class; therefore, it is its enemy. In this way, the enemies of all classes should be known. This enmity is determined by the "Lord" of the class. As stated in the *Bhupala Vallabha* <note>A classical 11th-century Sanskrit text on architecture and kingship</note>:

->The lords of the classes are the Eagle, Cat, Lion, Dog, Snake, Rat, Elephant, and Sheep. These animals are the natural enemies of the fifth class from their own.<-

<summary>This manuscript page explains the astrological and phonetic rules for city planning, aligning specific zodiac signs and Sanskrit alphabet groups with the eight cardinal and ordinal directions. It emphasizes building in a location that matches the phonetic "class" of the patron's name while avoiding "enemy" directions based on a symbolic animal hierarchy.</summary>

The enemy <note>original: "Ribhu"</note>; therefore, the sense is that one should build a house in the direction of their own enemy group. That is to say, one should understand that building in one's own middle ground, and specifically in one's own direction, is auspicious. Thus, it is established that one should reside after observing the friendship or enmity between one's own astrological group <note>Varga</note> and the village's group. Between the calculations based on the Zodiac sign <note>Rashi</note> and those based on the phonetic group <note>Varga</note>, the Zodiac calculation is more powerful. As stated in the <term>Ocean of Astrology</term> <note>original: "Jyotish-sagara," a classical astronomical treatise</note>: some say a house is auspicious in its own place for those of the same group. Now, regarding the group <insert>calculation</insert> <gloss>by the Kakini method</gloss> <note>a small unit of measurement or credit used in divination</note>, it is said: "Double five" and so on. When determining the residence between one's own group and the village group, the eight directions are doubled; whichever side is greater shall be the giver of wealth. Thus, the rule for village directions has been told. Now, where there is a combination of various names, stars, and so forth, one should first examine the land by its smell, color, and taste. Because it has been said, "by smell, color, and taste," <insert>Narada says</insert> <note>referring to the Sage Narada, a traditional authority on law and architecture</note> the color, taste, and smell of the earth are described according to the social classes <note>Varnas</note>.

<margin>Beginning 39</margin>
White, red, yellow, and black earth are auspicious for the classes starting from the Brahmins. White earth is for the Brahmin <note>priestly class</note>, red earth for the Kshatriya <note>warrior/ruling class</note>, yellow earth for the Vaishya <note>merchant/farmer class</note>, and black earth is auspicious for the Shudra <note>laboring class</note>. This implies that for mixed castes, mixed soils are auspicious. And so says <term>Vashistha</term> <note>another foundational sage of Vedic architecture</note>: "White earth like the moon is for leaders, red earth for kings, yellow for the people, and black for the laborers; and for those of mixed origin, a mixture." Having spoken of color, he now speaks of taste: "Sweet" and so on. Sweet earth is auspicious for Brahmins, pungent earth (tasting like pepper) for the Kshatriya, bitter earth (tasting like neem) for the Vaishya, and astringent earth (tasting like chebulic myrobalan) is auspicious for the Shudra. This implies that for those of mixed classes, the taste is mixed. And so says <term>Narada</term>: "Sweet, pungent, bitter, and astringent are the tastes in order." Thus, the tastes...

->**Thus**<-

Now he describes the smells: the smell of sandalwood <note>original: *chandana*</note> belongs to the Priest <note>original: *Brahmana*</note>; the smell of blood <note>original: *shronita* or *rakta*</note> belongs to the Warrior <note>original: *Kshatriya*</note>; the smell of food <note>original: *anna*</note> belongs to the Merchant <note>original: *Vaishya*</note>; and the smell of liquor <note>original: *madira* or *madya*</note> belongs to the Laborer <note>original: *Shudra*</note>. For those of mixed classes, there is a mixed smell. And so it is said in the *Karika* <note>a traditional instructional verse</note>: "The smells of the earth should manifest according to their own order." Among various smells, the diversity of tastes and smells should be understood as alternatives.

So also says the sage Narada: "In the middle, one should observe the script, smell, and color; then the sweet, sour, bitter, pungent, and astringent tastes in sequence." And as the *Karika* states: "The smells of the earth should manifest according to their own order; sweet, sour, astringent, pungent, and bitter in succession."

Having thus described the colors and other qualities, he now speaks of the land in relation to social classes. In the northern and other directions, whichever land has a slope <note>original: *plava*; referring to the downward incline of the terrain</note> in that direction, a person of that class—Priest and so on—should construct or build a house there. This is what is meant:
- A Priest should build a house on land sloping toward the **North**.
- A Warrior on land sloping toward the **East**.
- A Merchant on land sloping toward the **South**.
- A Laborer on land sloping toward the **West**.

Having thus stated the characteristics of the land according to class, he mentions a special rule for the Priest: "Another in all..." <note>referring to a specific verse fragment</note>. This means a Priest may build a house in the areas designated for all classes. Others have said: "The slope toward the North and other directions for Priests and the rest follows a clockwise <note>original: *pradakshina*</note> pattern; one should dwell everywhere according to the movement of the classes."

Now he describes the universal preference: "Where..." <note>referring to the next verse</note>. All classes should build a house where the digging of the earth <note>original: *mrit-karana*</note> is auspicious and firm. This is the meaning: on whatever land the preparation of the soil is praised, only there should a house be built. And so, in the *Vastu Purana*...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Shalya-jnana (the science of detecting hidden underground obstacles) within the context of Vastu Shastra (sacred architecture). It specifically details the method of "Varga-varna" divination, where the first letter of a client's question is mapped to a directional grid to locate buried objects.</meta>

<margin>
Om <note>The sacred syllable, used here as an auspicious invocation at the start of the page.</note>
4
</margin>

<margin>
<note>3x3 Grid Diagram containing directional letters: Kha, Ra, Ya, Ka, A, Ya, Va, Pa, Ta, Ta. This represents the spatial distribution of the Sanskrit alphabet for divination.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
The positions starting from the East.
</margin>

Salutations to Him <note>likely referring to the Divine Architect or the Supreme Being</note> from whom all knowledge arises. Regarding the construction of a house for all people, this is the opinion of Garga and other ancient sages. Now, the knowledge of *Shalya* <term>Shalya: literally 'a thorn' or 'arrowhead,' referring to hidden underground impurities like bones, charcoal, or metals that must be removed before building</term> is explained in the following half-verse.

Regarding the seven types of inquiries: when a person asks, "Should I build a house here?" the first syllable uttered by the questioner is analyzed. One should observe which of the letter groups—*A, Ka, Cha, Ta, Ta, Pa, Ya, Sha*—emerges first. A wise man should then declare the presence of a hidden object in the direction corresponding to that specific letter group.

This is the explanation: On the ground measured for the house, divided into a grid by north-south and east-west lines, the letter groups *A, Ka, Cha, Ta, Ta, Pa, Ya, Sha* are assigned in sequence to the directions starting from the East. If the question begins with a letter from the **A-group**, the hidden object is in the **East**. If it begins with the **Ka-group**, it is in the **South-East**. If with the **Cha-group**, it is in the **South**, and so on for the eight directions.

Furthermore, if the question begins with the letters *Ha, Ya, Ra, Va*, the hidden object is located in the **center** of the plot. This is also stated in the *Jyotirnibandha* <note>A famous medieval compendium on Vedic astrology and ritual</note>: after considering the entrance and the deity of the site, one must investigate the presence of hidden obstacles.

The groups *A, Ka, Cha, Ta, Ta, Pa, Ya, Sha* (and the central group) determine the location of the *Shalya*. If a house is built over such an object, it brings destruction to those living within. This is clearly stated: "If the inquiry points to the **East**, there is a **human bone** <note>original: 'nara-shalya'</note> buried at a depth of one and a half cubits <note>approx. 27 inches</note>, which causes great suffering."

If the inquiry points to the **South-East** (the Ka-group), the hidden object is a **donkey's bone**, leading to punishment from the king. If the inquiry points to the **South** (the Cha-group), it indicates a **crow's bone**...

<summary>This page describes a divinatory system where the first letter of a builder's question is used to locate harmful buried objects (Shalya) in specific directions of a construction site. It cites the sage Garga and the text Jyotirnibandha to emphasize that building over such objects leads to domestic ruin.</summary>
<keywords>Shalya-jnana, Vastu Shastra, divination, Garga, Jyotirnibandha, hidden objects, architectural ritual, Sanskrit alphabet grid</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Vastu Shastra (architectural science), specifically focusing on the consequences of hidden objects (Shalya) found in different cardinal directions and the methods for testing soil quality before construction.</meta>

->**On the Detection of Hidden Objects and Soil Testing**<-

If a hidden object is located in the **Southwest** <note>Nairutya</note> during an inquiry, it signifies the death of the master of the house after a long illness. If one digs one and a half cubits <term>Hasta: a traditional unit of measurement, roughly 18 inches</term> deep in this direction and finds a bone, it will cause the death of children or create great fear for the safety of infants. Furthermore, if such an object remains at that depth, the master of the house will never find stability or stay long in that home.

If the inquiry concerns the **Northwest** <note>Vayavya</note> direction, charcoal will be found there. This leads to constant bad dreams and ill omens for the inhabitants. If the inquiry concerns the **North** <note>Udichi</note>, there will be little wealth and much hardship; even a man as rich as Kubera <note>the god of wealth</note> would quickly face poverty. If the inquiry concerns the **Northeast** <note>Ishanya</note>, and a hidden object is found at one and a half cubits, it leads to the loss of cattle and the eventual ruin of the householder's prosperity.

In the **center of the plot**, if one finds burnt material, human skulls, ashes, or iron, it causes the total destruction of the lineage.

<insert>Having described the hidden obstacles, he now explains the means of knowing the auspicious and inauspicious nature of these obstacles.</insert>

He says: "Dig a pit." One should dig a pit <term>Shvabhra</term> measuring one cubit. At nightfall, one should fill this pit to the brim with water. If the pit remains full of water in the morning, then that site is excellent, auspicious, and will bring growth. If the water has receded significantly, it is mediocre. If the ground has cracked and the water is gone, it is inauspicious and will cause various types of loss.

Furthermore, having filled it with water at the start of the night: if water is seen in the morning, there is growth; if there is only mud, it is neutral; if the pit is dry and cracked, it signifies decay.

Having thus described the auspicious and inauspicious nature of the site, he now explains the purification of the earth <note>Bhumi Shodhana</note>. Having understood the site in the manner described, one should dig the area designated for the house down to the water table. One should dig the earth slightly beyond the planned dimensions of the house until reaching the level of moisture or water. This deep digging ensures the ground is purified.

<summary>This manuscript page details the dire consequences of finding hidden "obstacles" like bones or charcoal under a house and describes a traditional water-fill test to determine soil porosity and site suitability.</summary>

<keywords>Vastu Shastra, soil testing, architectural omens, hidden objects, Shalya, purification of land, Indian architecture</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for site preparation and foundation laying in Hindu architecture (Vastu Shastra). It transitions from soil testing to the physical excavation and the astronomical determination of the cardinal directions.</meta>

<margin>Om <note>A sacred invocation at the beginning of the page.</note></margin>
<margin>41</margin>
For the purpose of constructing the walls, one should dig the ground. Within this pit, there are various layers of earth; one should dig until the inner layer is reached. Alternatively, one should dig to the depth of a cubit <term>hastamātra; approximately 18 inches</term> and then fill it with water. <gloss>By that</gloss> In that place, the depth should be the height of a man <term>purusha-pramāṇa</term>, which is five "jewels." As the Sages say: five "jewels" equal ten spans <term>vitasti</term>, which is one hundred and twenty finger-widths <term>angula</term>; this constitutes the measure of a man. What should be done after that? Having performed the purification, one should then fill the excavated area with 
<margin>baked bricks</margin>
stones shaped like the heads of monkeys <term>kapishīrsha; a specific type of coping stone or masonry block</term>. One should make the foundation firm. Thus says Mandavya <note>An ancient authority on architecture and law</note>: "Even if the person is of excellent lifespan, one should only begin the masonry work after thoroughly clearing and preparing the field." Likewise, in the Matsya Purana <note>One of the eighteen major Puranas, containing significant sections on architecture</note>: "One should fill the excavated area firmly with stones the size of monkey-heads; having made the excavation firm, one should then establish the eastern direction." The results of finding certain items while digging the field are mentioned in the *Karika* <note>A collection of instructional verses</note>: "When digging, if a stream of water is found, it brings wealth and long life. If bricks are found in that place, it signifies the coming of wealth. However, if skulls, charcoal, or hair are found, one will be afflicted by disease." Having thus described the purification of the site, he now describes the determination of the directions. The determination of the East is done via Ujjain <note>The prime meridian of ancient Indian astronomy</note>. From the location of Ujjain, in the direction of Yama <note>the South</note>... <insert>3</insert> from the interior of the fire-altar, the East is to be determined. This means the East is found between the constellations Chitra <term>Spica</term> and Swati <term>Arcturus</term>. This is how it is done: if possible, at night, one sits facing East and looks through a sighting tube at the star Chitra when it has risen just a horn's length. Marking the spot on the ground where the line from the tube ends, and then without moving, observing the star Swati with the same eye through the tube...

<summary>This page details the ritual and physical preparation of a building site, including the specific depth of foundations measured in "man-lengths" and the auspicious or inauspicious signs found during excavation. It also begins explaining a method for finding true East using a sighting tube and the stars Chitra and Swati.</summary>

<keywords>Vastu Shastra, foundation, excavation, Matsya Purana, Mandavya, soil omens, orientation, Chitra, Swati, Ujjain</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
The hall <note>original: "shāllaṁ"; likely referring to the construction area or a specific building hall</note> should be measured by dropping a plumb line. Having marked the spot where the plumb line falls, and having dropped the line again in the same manner, one should make a mark. After making three such marks, one should take a cord <note>original: "rañjuṁ"</note> equal in length to the distance between the marks for the stars Spica <note>original: "citrā"</note> and Arcturus <note>original: "svātī"</note>. Bringing the ends of the cord together, one should mark the midpoint. Then, spreading it out again in that same way, one should fix a gnomon <note>original: "śankuṁ"; a vertical pillar used for astronomical measurements</note> at the midpoint. From that point, one should extend the cord to the western mark. This establishes the East-West line <note>original: "prācīsūtra"</note>. Having thus determined the direction, he describes the method for finding the East from the south, and then the method for finding the East from the north.

In the region north of Ujjain <note>original: "ujjayinyāḥ"; a city central to Indian meridian calculations</note>, the East is to be determined from the rising of Orion <note>original: "mṛga"</note> or the rising of the Pleiades <note>original: "kṛttikā"</note>. Thus, having observed the Pleiades through a sighting tube <note>original: "nalikā"</note> when they have risen to the height of a yoke <note>a specific unit of measure</note>, and having dropped two plumb lines from the base of the tube, one should make two marks on the ground. The East-West line is drawn over them.

Now he describes the method for finding the North. The North direction is to be determined from the face of the Pole Star <note>original: "dhruva"</note> using the gnomon. This is common to all locations. Now he describes the fault of being "direction-bewildered" <note>losing one's orientation</note>. In the *Lamp of Divine Sacrifice* <note>original: "devayajana dīpikā"</note>, it is clearly stated: "For the Brahmins living in the southern path from Ujjain, the East is determined by the Pleiades; for those living in the northern path, it is different."

And so says the *Aphorisms of the Cord* <note>original: "sulvasūtraṁ"; ancient texts on geometry and altar construction</note>: "The Pleiades, Shravana, Pushya, and the interval between Spica and Arcturus—these represent the form of the Eastern direction when they have risen to the height of a yoke." A "yoke" measure is eighty-six fingers <note>original: "aṅgula"</note> in length. Furthermore, the *Aphorisms of the Cord* state that the measure of a finger-breadth is standardized; there, eighty-eight or eighty-six are mentioned.

As stated in the *Clear Calculation* <note>original: "sphuṭakaraṇa"</note>: "The line between the Pole Star and the anchor point defines the North and South directions. From the 'tail and mouth of the fish' <note>a geometric construction technique using intersecting arcs</note>, one should know the West and East." So says the Elder Narada <note>original: "vṛddhanāradaḥ"</note> regarding temples, houses, and the installation of the sacred pillar.

In the hollow <note>original: "rekuṇḍe"</note>, especially facing the direction of the entrance, there may be the destruction of the lineage; therefore, one should accomplish it well. Now, he explains in which months a house should be built.

One should build a house in the months beginning with Madhava <note>the month of Vaishakha, typically April-May</note> and under the Nakshatras <note>lunar mansions</note> beginning with Mriga. Madhava is Vaishakha. Phalguna is well-known. Nabhasya is Shravana <note>July-August</note>. Marga is Margashirsha <note>November-December</note>. This is the meaning of "in those months." The "soft" stars are Chitra, Anuradha, Revati, and Mrigashirsha. "Anila" refers to Swati. "Arka" refers to Hasta. From these... <unclear>abhashah</unclear> the full stars are Vasu (Dhanishtha) and the "fixed" stars (Rohini and the three Uttaras). In a good movable sign, with the center and eighth houses being auspicious—meaning those places are free from malefic planets—one should establish the house firmly. The rest is as previously stated.

Now, he mentions a specific rule even regarding the aforementioned Nakshatras: "Not when the fire is at the front or back." When the "Back-Fire" rule <note>a technical calculation in Vedic astrology to avoid bad luck</note> is in effect, one should not construct. This is what is being said: Place the seven Nakshatras starting from Krittika in the East, seven from Ashlesha in the South, and so on, within the Seven-Spike Chart <note>term: Shalakachakra; a grid used to determine auspicious directions</note>. Among these, whichever direction the Moon's Nakshatra falls in, that is known as the "Face of the Direction" for the house. Thus, a house should be built when the Moon is in a favorable position.

As Bhrigu <note>an ancient sage and authority on Vastu</note> says: "In the chart known as the Seven-Spike, one should arrange the stars starting from Krittika. A cruel planet or the Moon at the back of the site is inauspicious." Likewise, the Brahmapurana states: "Wealth, travel, and fearlessness occur respectively when the Moon is at the front, left, or back of the house-builder." The pain caused by the Southwest direction is clear. One should not build during the days or divisions of the Sun or Mars. Even among the mentioned Nakshatras, one should avoid when the Moon is in Aquarius or Pisces.

The pillar should not be moved once set; one should observe all established boundaries. Thus says Mandavya <note>An ancient sage and authority on Vastu Shastra</note>: The raising of the pillar should be performed during the *Panchaka* <note>A group of five specific lunar mansions or Nakshatras considered sensitive in Vedic astrology</note> constellations. If not done then, one should perform the rituals for laying the foundation stone and other such procedures. Now, he describes the "Bull-Vastu" <note>Vrishabhavastu: A specific diagrammatic method to determine auspiciousness based on the sun's position</note>: Starting from the Sun's constellation, add three and so on. One should calculate in groups of four up to fifteen... <note>The text here describes a complex mathematical grid for astrological compatibility</note> these constellations are used for the secret construction of the house and for the first entry into the residence.

As stated in the *Vyavahara Sara* <note>The Essence of Customary Law/Practice</note>: By dividing into groups of three and four, one should ensure the prosperity of Lakshmi <note>Goddess of wealth</note>. If done incorrectly, this same Lakshmi brings poverty and disease instead of wealth at the time of beginning the construction or entering the home. Brihaspati <note>The preceptor of the gods and authority on law/astrology</note> also concurs.

<margin>8</margin>

Now, he explains the decision regarding the door/entrance with three and a half verses. Starting from the month of Bhadrapada <note>August-September</note>, every three months the *Vastu Purusha* <note>The presiding deity or cosmic energy of the site, envisioned as a man lying down</note> changes position. His head points toward the East and other directions in sequence.

<margin>Y</margin>

For the three months starting with Bhadrapada, his head is to the East. For the three months starting with Margashirsha <note>November-December</note>, his head is to the North. This is how the *Vastu Purusha* is described. The entrance of the house should be made in the direction where his head lies.

Now, he explains the decision for the entrance based on the *Sankranti* <note>The Sun's transit into a new zodiac sign</note>. When the Sun is in Taurus, Scorpio, Aries, or Libra, the house should face South or North. <note>original: "Golya" (Taurus), "Ali" (Scorpio), "Mrija" (Aries), "Ghata" (Libra)</note>. This means when the Sun resides in these signs, a South-facing or North-facing entrance should be constructed.

When the Sun is in Aquarius, Capricorn, Cancer, or Leo, the house should face East or West. <note>original: "Rudroga" (Aquarius), "Raga" (Capricorn), "Kulira" (Cancer), "Leya" (Leo)</note>. This means when the Sun is in these signs, the house entrance must be made facing either the East or the West.

</margin>

When the Sun is in an unfavorable zodiac sign, a house should not be constructed. Now, wishing to explain the determination of the house's facing direction based on the *Aya* <note>A mathematical formula used in Vastu to ensure the dimensions of a building harmonize with cosmic energies</note>, the author describes the method of calculating *Aya* using the "Arm" and the "Side." By multiplying the Arm and the Side, the remainders become the *Ayas*. The "Arm" is the name given to either the length or the breadth of the house, and the other is called the "Side." As it is stated in the <term>Lilavati</term> <note>A famous 12th-century mathematical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>: "Whichever side is desired is the Arm; the side perpendicular to it is the other Arm. In a triangle or rectangle, one is the Arm and the other is called the Side." Their product (multiplication) results in a remainder which determines the directions starting from the East, such as the Banner and others. These *Ayas* occur in sequence. The meaning is that the Banner and other signs are powerful in their respective directions starting from the East.

These *Ayas*—Banner and the rest—should be applied in their own proper positions and places. Among these, the Banner is the foremost, and so on. This means: in houses that are equal in size to the main house, these should be maintained. Or rather, they should be maintained in a residence that is suitable to oneself. Or, they should be maintained across seven similar houses. Or, for those performing fierce deeds, they should be maintained according to the sequence of the inhabitant's power. Or, for those who live contentedly with suitable possessions, they should be maintained in their houses. Or, in a house where the objects are arranged according to the inhabitant's suitability, they should be maintained as appropriate.

This is clearly stated in the <term>Vastupradipa</term> <note>An authoritative text on the "Lamp of Architecture"</note>: In small villages, markets, and granaries, the Bull, the Lion, and the Elephant signs are auspicious. Wise men should assign the Elephant sign to wells and ponds. One should employ the Lion for seats (thrones) and the Elephant for beds. Assign the Bull to vessels used for eating, and the Banner to parasols and similar items. In the kitchen or the fire-hall, or in the houses of those who live by their speech (scholars or priests), one should assign the Smoke sign. Likewise, avoid the Crow and the Jackal. In a stable for horses, use the Donkey; in a shed for cows, use the <unclear>Yodyodho</unclear>. In the houses of <unclear>Yattiyate</unclear>, one should assign the Bull, the Lion, or the Elephant, as well as in palaces and temples.

# Auspiciousness 2

For the workshops of artisans, clothing stores, and guesthouses <note>original: "dharmashala"</note>, the Banner <note>original: "dhvaja"</note> is the appropriate formula. In villages, cities, and ordinary houses, it is generally auspicious. For armories where weapons and violence are stored, the Elephant <note>original: "gaja"</note> is used. For granaries, water tanks, and stables for cows or horses, the Bull <note>original: "vrishabha"</note> is prescribed. From the parts of the calculation, the Lion, Elephant, and Bull are considered auspicious. Having thus described the means of calculating these 'Aya' formulas, we now describe their orientations. The Banner should face all directions or have doors on all sides. The Elephant faces East or South. In a gathering, the Bull clearly faces East. Whichever direction a formula faces, the city or house should be made to face that same direction. 7

Having thus stated the orientation of city houses according to the 'Aya' formulas, the text now mentions the auspiciousness of these formulas in passing. Three are considered inauspicious, while four are praised as auspicious for everyone.

-> As the sage Garga has proclaimed: Fame, sorrow, enmity, imprisonment, poverty, happiness, disease, and death are the results of the Banner and the others, respectively, when beginning a house. <-

Now, regarding the specific cases of the Camel and Smoke formulas: Smoke is not desired, and the Camel is generally avoided. However, for those of lower castes, the Dog is acceptable; for those who keep donkeys, the Camel is praised. For those living in mountainous regions or rugged terrain, the Donkey formula is considered auspicious. Having established the orientation of city houses based on these formulas, the text now speaks in relation to the zodiac signs <note>original: "rashis"</note>. It is said: For the signs of the End (Pisces), the Scorpion, and the Crab, a house facing East is beneficial. <gloss>The "End" refers to Pisces; the Scorpion and Crab are well-known.</gloss> For the Virgin (Virgo), the Pair (Gemini), and the Sea-Monster (Capricorn), a house facing South <note>original: "yama"</note> is beneficial. For the Ram (Aries), the Lion (Leo), and the Bow (Sagittarius), a house facing North <note>original: "saumya"</note> is beneficial. Thus the teachers have spoken. Having thus described the orientation of the house...

He explains the determination of that [the entrance] here: "The face of the bricks and wood." In the context of house entrances, the beauty should be executed according to the form; the facing of the bricks and wood is desired to be consistent.
Having made a void, its face should be made like the "peak-window" <note>original: "kaku-gavākṣa"; a cow's-eye or oriel window</note> faces. Some, however, think otherwise: that the windows themselves are the faces, as many as there are facing the directions.
"The Earth" <note>original: "Urvi"</note> refers to that which is to be made facing the primary directions. Thus, in the **Science of Architecture** <term>Vāstuśāstra: the traditional Indian system of architecture and design</term>, it is said: "The door is declared to be of three types." Having calculated the architectural transition of the zodiac signs, the face [entrance] and other windows are placed. Having thus discussed the determination of the house entrance, he now speaks of the defect of an "obstructed face" <term>viddha-mukha: an entrance blocked by an obstacle</term> at the corners and its exceptions. "A misplaced face" refers to a house whose face is pierced or obstructed by corner pillars and the like. One should not desire an entrance obstructed by corners—"mad-corners" <note>original: "matta-koṇa"; likely referring to protruding or irregular angles</note>, pillars (which are well known), trees, or a "level-whirl" <note>original: "sama-bhrama"; a mechanical device like a potter's wheel or a water-wheel</note>, or a path (which is well known), or mud (permanent mire caused by bathing and the like). The meaning is that one should not desire a face obstructed by these.
He states the exception to this: "By equal height," etc. If the obstruction is separated by a distance greater than the height [of the building], there is no defect. Indeed, it is said that if the distance is twice the height of the house, the obstruction by corners and such causes no defect. "Indeed" <note>original: "kila"</note> indicates traditional authority or certainty. Thus it is said in the **Mattatara**: "Paths, trees, and corners are inauspicious for a door; but if separated by the height and other qualities, the 'whirl' is not a defect." In the **Kātyāyana Household Rules** <term>Gṛhyakārikā: a manual of domestic rituals and construction</term> it is also said: "A form pierced by trees, pillars, machines, or mud is always declared an inauspicious door, as is one that is too high or too low."
Next, the calculation of the house's asterism <term>Nakṣatra: lunar mansion used for astrological timing</term>... [the text transitions to mathematical area calculations involving the number 108]. 44

When the total is divided by twenty, the remainder indicates the House Asterism <term>Nakshatra: one of the 27 lunar mansions used in Vedic astrology</term>. Having thus explained the Income and Expenditure, we now consider the specific results: a house where the income is low and expenditure is high is considered inauspicious. Conversely, a house where the income is high and expenditure is low is auspicious. 

Regarding the determination of the zodiac signs <note>original: "Rashis"</note> arising from the asterisms, it is said: In **Aries**, there are the three beginning with **Ashvini**. -> "The three beginning with Ashvini are said to be in Aries; the three beginning with Magha are in Leo; the three beginning with Mula are in Sagittarius; in the remaining signs, there are two asterisms each." <-

Next, the text discusses the compatibility between the owner and the house: The relationship between the Lord <insert>the owner</insert> and the House should be analyzed as previously described in the **Chapter on Marriage** <note>referring to the Vivaha Prakarana, suggesting that the bond between a person and their home is as significant as a marriage</note>. To determine the auspiciousness of the house's dimensions, one counts from the entrance: starting from one, the numbers increase in a clockwise direction. This means: in the direction of the door, the number is one (1); in the second direction, two (2); in the third direction, four (4); and in the fourth direction, eight (8). This is the method for calculating the proportions of the hall <note>Shala</note>.

# On the Construction of the Hexagon

The method known as the **hexagon** <note>original: "Shatkona"; a six-sided figure often used in ritual diagrams</note> is described. In the two "eyes" <note>likely referring to the intersection points of circles or specific vertices</note> the action called "drawing" <note>original: "karshi"; the act of pulling a cord to mark a line</note> is performed. The wide... <gloss>called a corner</gloss>... the cloth of the night, the twisting, the means of the fruit <note>the "fruit" or "phala" refers to the area or the result of a mathematical operation</note>.

He speaks of the two loops: having placed the two loops at the stop-points of the field and the two pegs <note>original: "shanku"; gnomons or stakes driven into the ground for measurement</note> being firmly fixed, one should draw this [cord] by the method called "drawing." This is what is meant: in the middle portion of the field, having extended a cord measured to the limit of the determined direction of the field, and having fixed a peg at the two ends of that cord, one should place the two loops at the ends of the twisted cord.

Holding the mark called the "drawing-point," one should pull from one side. In whatever way the <unclear>equal</unclear> cord passing through both pegs is to be drawn, in that way he should draw it—this is the meaning. Regarding the "corner-peg": at every corner, at the mark where the cord has arrived, one should fix a peg; this is the implied meaning. (27)

By this method of fixing pegs, the following is stated: that very same drawing-mark [should be moved] from the other... toward the equal twenty. In the series starting from one up to an equal twenty, the results such as the "visible process" and others would be the "fruit" <note>area</note>. The fruit for the infinite... (28) When multiplied by the desired number, it would be the true area. (29)

Here, in the case of the eight and the desired [quantities], the division of the two is their sum—this is the meaning. (38) Even in the area of the field of the desired path, having marked it, that is the explanation. And so, in the formula of Manganatha Deva, the light is multiplied by eight. (30) Whatever the gain <note>original: "aya"; often refers to the remainder or a specific astrological/mathematical result</note> is, it is diminished by those... multiplied by three... (20) If there is an absence of the desired gain... the meaning of this is: the eighth part of the product of the desired light and the pole... the product of the pole and the thought. By this, even for one... whatever the gain is, the desired gain is diminished, multiplied by three... (27) By this obtained [value], not struck... by the zeros... even for one of them. (39) Among the products of the light and the pole, there would be an exceeding field; the effort of the gain, and in the eighth of the gain.

# On the Calculation of Area and Site Layout

In this matter of the <term>Aya</term><note>A set of traditional architectural formulas used to ensure the harmony and prosperity of a building</note>, if the remainder is not divisible by three, then one must add as many parts as necessary until it becomes divisible by three. As the great astrologer Mahalagi stated: "Subtract one from twelve, then multiply by the teeth <note>The number 32</note>, the arrows <note>The number 5</note>, and the zodiac signs <note>The number 12</note>, resulting in 152." Furthermore, subtract one from seventeen; these are also added to the one hundred and eighteen <insert>118</insert> which are then multiplied by six and divided by eight. The remainder shall be the <term>kshetraphala</term><note>The area of the plot</note>. If there is any doubt, the desired width is determined by the length. This verse is clear. By such considerations, the area is found; if the distance is fixed, one should achieve the <term>sthulasadhana</term><note>A rough or practical calculation, as opposed to a purely theoretical one</note>.

Now, for the sake of this practical calculation, he describes the <term>sutra-motana</term><note>Literally "bending the cord"; a method of marking out a site using a knotted or folded measuring string</note>. The phrase "double the shadow's measure" means one should arrange the cord in two parts and mark it accordingly. "Double the shadow" refers to a length equal to twice the measured length. Having made the cord into two parts with loops at the ends, one should mark it from one end.

This is what is meant: Having prepared a cord measured to the length of the entire imagined area, and having made loops at both ends, one should mark it. From the total length, at a distance of three parts—this means when the length of the field is divided into four parts—one should leave three <term>anghri</term><note>A "foot" or a quarter-part of a whole measure</note> and make a mark. From that same point, one should mark half of the width of the field. These two marks are called the "Puller" and the "Corner." The mark made at the three-quarter measure is the "Puller," and the mark made at the half-width is the "Corner."

Thus, by the method of bending the cord, he explains the practical calculation. Regarding the loops: having fixed the loops to the pegs at the boundaries of the field, the length, the marks, and the corners...

In this manner, one should pull the cord. Holding the mark for pulling, one should pull it toward one direction. The meaning is that one should pull it in such a way that the cord passing through both pegs becomes equal in form. "A peg at the corner"—this implies that where the corner mark appears, one should strike a future peg there. In this way, by this method, one should fix the pegs. This is what is said: pulling that same mark toward the other direction, one should fix a peg at the corner. Having thus described the accomplishment of two corners, he describes the accomplishment of the other two corners by "exchange." By exchanging them—that is, by holding them in reverse—one should fix pegs at the other two corners.

This is what is meant: by holding the moving ends in reverse and pulling as before, one should fix pegs at the two corners. Having thus described the preparation of the field, he describes the laying of the cord. "From the Southeast," etc. Starting from the Southeast <term>Agneya; the direction governed by the fire-god Agni</term>, moving in a clockwise direction, one should establish the "Man" <term>Purusha; the cosmic being whose body constitutes the temple or altar plan</term> at the proper interval. In the middle of that, or in the middle of the field, having meditated upon the Man with loops, the equal Man, the Man whose head is the breath <note>original: "pranata purusha shiraskam"</note>, they should meditate upon him. How should they meditate on that Man? As having eight limbs; at the time when he is composed of eight parts. This is the meaning: having projected the measure of the field as three units or seven "Man-lengths" <term>purusha; a unit of measure based on a man with upraised arms</term>, one should then divide it into twenty-eight (28) parts. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Vastu Shastra (architectural science), specifically focusing on the ritual preparation of the ground and the placement of the gnomon or foundation pillar. After the division of the parts is completed, one should fill the foundation pit with soft earth. According to the *Brahmasambhu Sutra* <note>A technical treatise on ritual measurements</note>, the ceremony for laying the foundation stone and the pillar should be performed. Kashyapa and Narada state that this should be done between the East and the South. In the months beginning with Nabhasya <note>The month of Bhadrapada, roughly August-September</note>, in groups of three months each, the orientation shifts clockwise starting from the East.

As stated in the *Shilpashastra* <note>The science of arts and crafts</note>: "The head of the Vastu deity lies in one direction, the mouth in another, and the feet in the sixth part." Life and desire reside in the foundation; digging on the left side is considered auspicious. Having determined the measurements accordingly, one should first fill the areas from the Southeast to the Northwest.

Lalla <note>An ancient astronomer and authority on architecture</note> further explains: "One must avoid the head portion, which consists of one-eighth of the area. The middle part is known as the navel; one must not establish the gnomon <term>Shanku: a wooden peg or pillar used for astronomical and architectural alignment</term> there." Wherever the head of the Vastu deity is located, one must calculate the proportions from that point. Based on the length and width, the parts for the gnomon are measured.

Now, the placement of the gnomon is described: "Not at an inauspicious time." The gnomon must be placed by a person at a calculated, auspicious moment <note>Muhurta</note>. Regarding the measurements, the text states: for the highest grade of construction, the gnomon should be 24 fingers <term>Angula: a traditional unit of measurement roughly equal to a finger's width</term> long; for the middle grade, 20 or 18 fingers; and for the smallest, 12 fingers.

This is what is meant: The learned experts prescribe a gnomon of 24 fingers for Brahmins, 20 fingers for Kshatriyas, 16 fingers for Vaishyas, and 12 fingers for Shudras <note>These measurements correspond to the four traditional social classes of ancient India</note>. The gnomon should be made from the wood of auspicious trees. It should be divided into three sections: the first part square, the second part octagonal, and the third part circular. This means that after dividing the gnomon of the specified measurement into three equal parts, the first part is shaped as a square...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for ritual measurements and the construction of sacred tools, specifically focusing on the gnomon (ritual peg) and the drawing of lines for site preparation.</meta>

<margin>
Om <note>A sacred invocation</note>
48
48
</margin>

One should understand the intent: one-third should be octagonal <note>original: "ashtasram"</note> and the final third should be made circular <note>original: "vartulam"</note>. Thus it is said: in that interval, there should be twenty-four or twelve finger-breadths <note>term: angula (a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of a finger)</note> respectively. According to the measurement of the gnomon <note>term: shanku (a ritual peg or gnomon used to cast shadows for orientation)</note> for the different social classes—starting with the Brahmins—one should mark it with colors or with gold and other materials. 

Materials include Khadira wood, silver, lotus stalks, wool, fragrant woods, curved sandalwood, Palasha wood, Sharat, Prala, and the expansive Jania-Karaka-Tanai; or it may be made of bamboo or Bilva wood. Dividing this gnomon into three parts: the first part is square <note>original: "chaturasram"</note>, the next is octagonal, and the final third is smooth and rounded. Having prepared it with these characteristics, they speak of the first drawing of lines upon the earth when the sun's rays appear.

One pedestal is easily understood. Regarding the explanation of the other: gold and other materials correspond to the Brahmins and other classes. There, they should clearly perform the action with the forefinger <note>original: "tarjanya"</note>, as if using a measuring cord. This means one should move clockwise <note>original: "pradakshinam"</note> starting from the Southeast <note>the direction of Agni, the fire god</note>. 

This is what is meant: A Brahmin, being clear in mind, should draw the line reaching the end of the cord with a gold tool using the forefinger, moving clockwise from the Southeast. A Vaishya should draw the lines clearly at the level of the pair of thighs. A Shudra should draw the lines clearly at the level of the pair of feet. 

And so says the sage Garga: "Having laid down the cord, one should draw the line using the middle finger, the forefinger, or the thumb, with jewels, gold, silver, or mother-of-pearl." Similarly, the sage Bhrigu states: "The Brahmin at the head, the King <note>Kshatriya</note> at the mouth, the Vaishya at the thighs, and others at the feet; one should draw the lines clearly at the start of the ritual performance, moving clockwise." 

From the direction of Agni <note>Southeast</note> means "Agneyah." Thus it is stated in the Karika <note>a collection of ritual verses</note>: "The virtuous one should draw the line with the thumb or the forefinger, using gold, jewels, silver, or ivory; or with flowers, being compassionate. One must not use iron, nor ashes, nor pieces of wood, as these are considered defective and bring faults to the vision."
