This has been clearly stated regarding weapons, clay, dreams, and so forth. Thus, the elders in their manual <note>referring to a *Paddhati* or ritual guidebook</note> have said: if the design is drawn with a weapon, there is a fear of weapons; if in a dream, there is a fear of wet iron or bathing; if drawn with grass, there is a fear of thieves; if with wood, there is a fear of the king. If drawn on a wheel or similar object, it brings fear of enemies. If drawn with leather, it is considered cursed, and if with a tooth, it brings inauspiciousness or enmity to the creator. If written in a clockwise direction, however, it indicates prosperity.

Now, he describes the **Foundation Stone Ceremony** <term>Shilanyasa: the ritual laying of the first stone</term>:
They should perform the ritual for the paternal stone <note>the primary foundation stone</note> just as they do for the others. They should install them in a clockwise direction starting from the Southeast <note>the Agneya corner</note>. As Varaha <note>Varahamihira, the 6th-century polymath</note> says: "Having bathed in the Southeast corner, one should first lay the stone; the remaining stones and pillars should then be established in a clockwise order."

Now, following the discussion of the walls, he describes the **Doorway** <term>Dvara</term>:
On the East and other sides, the door should be placed at the third, sixth, half-fifth <note>4.5</note>, or fifth division. To the left of the length, marked with five parts... <insert>all is given by one's own self</insert>. 

The meaning is this: When the length is divided into nine parts, starting from the East and following the directions, the door is said by the sages to be placed from the left (the northern side) at the third, sixth, four-and-a-half, or fifth division. This means: three, six, and the one that is half of five <note>4.5</note>, and five—these are the ordinal numbers. 

This is what is being said: Having divided the length of the house into nine parts, if the house faces East, the door should be made at the third part from the left. If the house faces South, it should be made at the sixth part. If it faces West, it should be made at the four-and-a-half part. If it faces North, the door should be made at the fifth part. All is given by the self; in the fourth part, the door is said to be... <unclear>text corrupted</unclear>.

# On the Proportions of the Square Pavilion

The length of the square pavilion <note>original: "cavarahah"; likely a regional term for a square hall or pavilion</note> should be divided into nine parts. Within this space, a balanced arrangement is auspicious. In the first three-quarters of the area, or in the fourth and sixth directions toward the south, and from the back in the fifth parts, it brings joy to one's offspring. Thus says the <term>Yavana</term> <note>A term used in ancient Indian texts to refer to Greeks or Western foreigners, often cited as authorities on mathematical astronomy and architecture</note>: "When the measurements of the doors are in proper proportion to the cardinal directions, it brings happiness."

## The Consecration and Installation of the Pillar

Now, he describes the consecration of the pillar once it has been fashioned and is ready to be set in its foundation trench. The pillar should be erected in the direction of Fire <note>the Southeast</note> at an auspicious time and under a favorable wind. Regarding this, it is said that a pillar that is honored with sandalwood and similar offerings remains stable. Furthermore, it should be crowned with a "head-canopy" <note>original: "shirashchatra"</note>. This canopy is placed atop the pillar specifically to prevent the entry of crows and other such birds.

As the authority <term>Chavarah</term> states: "The pillar, joined with its canopy and scented with perfumes, should be established after being anointed with incense. The height of the pillar must be carefully crafted according to the rules prescribed for doorways."

Furthermore, the commentator <term>Utpala</term> <note>Bhatta Utpala, a famous 10th-century commentator on astronomical and architectural texts</note> remarks: "If birds such as crows or vultures, or snakes and similar creatures, should perch upon the top of the pillar, it bodes ill for the builder. Therefore, one should properly support a canopy or a fruitful branch atop the pillar, and place gems and other precious items in the foundation hole."

### Regional Customs and the Time-Serpent

In the region of <term>Gujarat</term> <note>original: "Gurjaradesha"</note>, some practitioners observe the position of the <term>Time-Serpent</term> <note>original: "Kalasarpa"; a symbolic serpent representing the movement of time and solar energy in the earth</note>. They perform the first pillar consecration in the direction of "Death" <note>the South</note>, but this is considered incorrect. Because of that observation, they also erect pillars at the corners of the wedding canopy <note>original: "vivahavedya"</note> for decoration; however, that is a local custom and is not widely accepted by the authorities.

According to the calculation of the Serpent's movement: "Leaving aside the creation, one should count the directions. From the transition of the sun into a new sign, the Serpent moves. If the pillar is fixed in the mouth of the Serpent, it brings death to the groom; if in the middle, death to the maiden; if in the tail, death to the kinsmen." This rule is strictly observed in the <term>Matsya</term> region <note>An ancient kingdom in central India, modern-day Rajasthan</note> when driving the foundation stake for the wedding canopy.

<summary>This page details the mathematical proportions for pavilions and the ritual requirements for installing pillars, including warnings against bad omens like birds and the dangerous positioning of the "Time-Serpent" during construction.</summary>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the ritual and structural foundations of residential architecture (Vastu Shastra), specifically focusing on the placement of the first pillar and the selection of timber.</meta>

The circumambulation <note>pradakshina: the ritual of walking clockwise around a sacred object</note> of the pillars of one's own house has been described by nine different house-builders starting from the Southeast. Thus, according to the words of Brahma, the laying of the foundation stone and the raising of the pillars should always be done between the South and the Southeast; so says the sage Kashyapa. 

Furthermore, in the *Sharngadhara* and the *Pranada*, it is stated: "In palaces and in all houses, one should always ceremonially install the first pillar in the Southeast according to the prescribed rites." 

The great teacher Varahamihira <note>A 6th-century polymath and author of the Brihat Samhita</note> also states: "Having performed the worship in the Southeast corner, one should first lay the foundation stone. Then, the pillars should be erected in a clockwise direction." 

This is also mentioned in the *Katyayana Grihyakarika*: "The householder should place each pillar upon the stone base using these specific mantras: 'Mriyo vasa taratayair...' <note>original: "म्रियो वसा तरतयैरि"</note> and so on. One should lay the foundation stones in the corners starting from the Southeast with these mantras." 

In the commentary by Harihara on house construction, it is said that this is sufficient detail, as it has been stated extensively elsewhere. Regarding the types of trees to be used, other scriptures state: "The Shriparni <term>Gmelina arborea: a sacred timber tree</term>, Rohini, Shala, Sarja, and Sarala are auspicious. Also recommended are the Patanga, Lodhra, Shala, Arjuna, Shimshapa, Sandalwood, Ashoka, Badari, Madhuka, Mango, and Kadamba. These are excellent for a householder's dwelling." 

However, using Neem, Bilva, or forest-grown wood for a house is generally avoided. According to Narada: "Seven types of wood are inferior and bring suffering, including those that are burnt, thorny, or inhabited by birds. Trees that are broken, or those associated with wild animals, are also considered unfavorable." 

Other trees to be used in house construction should be smooth and upright. Varahamihira further notes: "Trees growing in cemeteries, on paths, in animal sanctuaries, on anthills, in gardens, or at hermitages should be avoided. Likewise, trees from sacred groves, those growing near temples, those withered by fire, those entwined with creepers, those damaged by lightning or wind, or those fallen on their own or crushed by elephants are not to be used."

<summary>This passage details the ritual orientation for laying foundation stones and pillars, specifically emphasizing the Southeast corner, and provides an extensive list of auspicious and inauspicious timbers for construction.</summary>

Trees that are damaged, entangled with others, or infested with pests and honeybees should be avoided. Those that are glossy, bearing flowers and fruits, and possess a fragrance similar to Himalayan Cedar <term>Devadaru: a sacred cedar used in construction</term> or Sandalwood are auspicious. For the priestly class <note>Brahmins</note>, trees like the Flame-of-the-Forest are beneficial; for the warrior class <note>Kshatriyas</note>, the Catechu and Bael trees bring prosperity. For the merchant class <note>Vaishyas</note>, the Jivaka, Catechu, and Sindhuka trees yield good results. For the laboring class <note>Shudras</note>, the Sindhuka, Kesara, Sarja, and Sal trees are honored, or indeed, all types of trees may be used by them.

The sage Vyasa also supports this view, stating that one should only use wood from trees that have been properly inhabited <note>referring to the spirits or 'Vana-devatas' residing in trees</note> and not others. Regarding the dwelling, the wise should not reside in a house where the wood is older than three months. Sharngadhara <note>a famous 14th-century scholar</note> states: "In a new house, new wood is recommended; old wood is not praised. Using new wood in an old structure is better than using old wood in a new structure."

Regarding the prayer for cutting trees mentioned in other texts: "O Spirits who dwell here, having received this ritual offering, please relocate elsewhere." One should then recite the mantra: "Salutations to you all." 

In the context of the first pillar, the author of the *Ratnakosha* <note>The "Jewel-Treasury," a classical technical treatise</note> discusses the height of the house: The height should be the width of the house plus one-sixteenth part of that width. As stated in the *Ratnakosha*: "The height shall be the width plus its sixteenth part." Furthermore, Lalla <note>an ancient astronomer and architect</note> says: "One should not make the house excessively high nor excessively low, as per the desired success and beauty." 

Now, regarding the main entrance of the house and which direction it should face for various activities: bathing, waking up, sleeping, storing clothes, and eating. For the protection of cattle and wealth (gold, etc.), and for the gods—these directions are now explained.

# The Placement of Rooms and Water

The positions of these rooms should be established; the phrase "one should establish them in their respective directions in order" is understood here. As **Narada** states: The bathroom should be in the East; the <term>Nalaya</term><note>Likely referring to a room for water runoff or a laundry area</note> in the Southeast; the bedroom in the South; the dressing room in the Southwest; the dining hall in the West; the animal shed in the Northwest; the storehouse for vessels and wealth in the North; and the temple <insert>or house of worship</insert> in the Northeast.

Now, he describes the location for water: Water should be placed in the direction of the Lord <note>The Northeast, governed by Ishana/Shiva</note>, and not elsewhere. And so says **Lalla**: If water is placed in the East and other directions <note>meaning the incorrect directions</note>, there will be fear of fire, fear of enemies, quarrels with women, misfortune for women, anxiety, and loss of sons.

Next, he discusses other matters: "Supreme" refers to the most excellent or the entire collection of objects that should be arranged by the wise. He mentions others based on the principles of **Lalla**. Now, in the South-by-Southwest direction, a place for the deceased <note>a space for ancestral rites or mourning</note> should be made, especially if there is a shortage of space. These aforementioned rules should be applied within the main house or in the available space according to the relative importance of the rooms.

Now, regarding the limitation of resources and space, he mentions the opinion of other teachers: For those with little wealth or space, the masters have said that the division of the house into parts <note>the Vastu Purusha Mandala</note> should be done proportionately. For small houses that lack the capacity for full expansion, the mortar and pestle, the grinding stone, the hearth <note>fire-place</note>, and the water reservoir should be placed with effort in the South or Southwest, as should the place for washing feet.

Now, he explains in which houses the strict boundaries and rules must be observed and in which they need not: "Thirty-two" refers to houses where the measurement is more than thirty-two <term>cubits</term><note>A 'hasta' or cubit is a traditional unit of length, roughly 18 inches</note>. In a house exceeding thirty-two cubits, the fire-facing areas, the four-fold divisions, and the structural loads must be strictly calculated.

It also provides practical adaptations for smaller homes where strict adherence to the full grid is not possible.</summary>

</margin>

Regarding the **veranda** <note>original: "alinda"; the pillared gallery or porch of a house</note> and similar structures: if the veranda is integrated into the main hall—as is commonly understood in the world—it is considered an ornament of the house. In such cases, the specific mathematical formulas for dimensions <note>referring to the 'Ayadi' formulas used to determine the energetic suitability of a building</note> need not be calculated. As stated in the **Chintamani** <note>The *Muhurta Chintamani*, a 16th-century treatise on auspicious timing</note>: "Where the length of the houses exceeds thirty-two cubits <note>one cubit or 'hasta' is roughly 18 inches</note>, the wise man need not worry about the calculations of income, debt, or directional influences."

Furthermore, in the <term>Ratna Martanda</term> <note>The "Sun of Jewels," a classical astronomical/astrological text</note>, it is said: "One should not worry about income and expenditure calculations for a house made of grass (thatch)." Likewise, **Vasishtha** <note>A legendary Vedic sage and author of architectural canons</note> states: "Up to a length of eleven or even thirty-two cubits, one should consider the income and other mathematical factors; beyond that, or for smaller structures, they are not required."

Now, regarding the specific requirements for the house, it is said: "From the beginning..." <note>referring to the commencement of the residency</note>. The owner should enter the house during the months prescribed by the scriptures and according to the stated rituals. What kind of house? One that is fully roofed and enclosed by walls. What are its characteristics? It must be equipped with a finished door-frame and functional doors. When the doors are properly fitted, the structure becomes stable. Furthermore, it must be "honored"—decorated with auspicious garlands and arches.

As **Narada** <note>A divine sage often cited in legal and architectural texts</note> says: "One should not enter a house that lacks doors, is not fully roofed, or where the ritual offerings to the gods and spirits have not been made; such a house brings fear of the sky (elements) and is harmful." And **Chyavana** <note>An ancient sage</note> adds: "If the owner enters a house that is not properly bounded, disease follows; if the doors are not properly set, it leads to the ruin of the lineage; if the measurements and doors are too low, it results in poverty."

Now, regarding the specific months for entry: one should enter the house in the months of <term>Magha, Urja</term>, and **Shukla**. The month of "Urja" refers to <term>Kartika</term> <note>October/November</note>, and "Shukla" refers to **Jyeshtha** <note>May/June</note>. One should enter the house during these months, adhering to the rules established for the commencement of residency.

<summary>This page outlines the exemptions for architectural calculations in very large or very simple buildings and details the physical requirements—such as roofs, doors, and decorations—necessary before a housewarming ceremony can occur.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Vastu (architectural astrology), specifically focusing on the timing for entering a new or renovated home. It transitions from the section on building to the section on travel.</meta>

Just as the construction of a house is permitted in all months, the **entry** into the house is also prescribed for all months. Thus, the sage **Narada** states: "The entry is considered mediocre in the months of *Margashirsha* <note>November-December</note> and *Kartika* <note>October-November</note>; it is considered auspicious in the months of *Magha*, *Phalguna*, *Vaishakha*, and *Jyeshtha*." <note>These correspond to the late winter and spring months, generally considered most stable for new beginnings.</note>

Similarly, in the *Light of Astrology* <note>original: "Jyotishprakasha"</note>, it is said that the house entry should be performed under the same lunar mansions <term>Nakshatras: the 27 lunar mansions used in Vedic astrology to determine auspicious timing</term> and months as the commencement of the building itself.

Now, regarding the **First Entry** <note>original: "Apurva-pravesha," referring to entering a brand-new house for the first time</note>, it is said: "By the commencement stars." This means the first entry is declared by the great sages to be performed under the same stars that were used to begin the house construction. Here, the phrase "commencement stars" is an elliptical expression <note>original: "upalakshana"</note>; it implies that all factors, such as the lunar day <term>Tithi</term> and other astrological limbs used at the start of building, should be adopted for the first entry.

The entry into a new house is specifically called **Apurva** (unprecedented). It should be performed under **fixed stars** <note>stars of a 'steady' nature like Rohini or the Uttaras</note> and other auspicious lunar mansions.

Then there is the **Entry after Travel** <note>original: "Sapurva-pravesha," referring to returning to a home after a long journey</note>. This is performed upon the conclusion of a journey. According to the *Light of Astrology* and the works of Achyuta: "The **Dual Entry** <note>original: "Dvanyatmaka"</note> is declared to be performed with the same stars as the commencement." A "Dual Entry" refers to the act of re-entering a house that has been reconstructed after being destroyed by fire or other calamities.

Some authorities state: "The entry after travel should be done with the commencement stars, but some say it is merely mediocre." The sages declare three types of house entry:
1. **First Entry** (Apurva): For a brand-new home.
2. **Return Entry** (Sapurva): After a journey.
3. **Re-entry** (Dvandvahvayam): After repairs or fire damage.

Regarding the placement of the sun: If the Sun is in the eleventh sign from the house's sign, one should reside in a west-facing house. If the Sun is in the fifth sign, one should reside in a north-facing house.

Regarding the timing of travel, it is said: On the day of the week associated with a planet that is currently in a favorable major period, sub-period, or sub-sub-period <note>original: "dasha-antardasha-vidasha"</note>, and which yields auspicious results in the transit charts and the eight-fold planetary strength charts <note>term: Ashtakavarga; a system of points used to determine the strength of planets in transit</note>, travel is considered excellent and auspicious by the teachers. Some, however, say that travel is favorable during the constellations starting from Shravana. Specifically, the "good ears" refer to Shravana; "the end" refers to Revati; "the soft" refers to the group including Punarvasu and Pushya. Thus, Shravana, Dhanishtha, Revati, Ashvini, Punarvasu, and Pushya are the constellations intended. Regarding this, a specific rule is given: "Not in the birth star." Travel is generally not advised in one's birth constellation, yet some teachers claim that travel in Shravana is auspicious even if it is one's birth star. Now, those stars in which travel is not auspicious are listed: the Serpent <note>original: "Sarpa"; the constellation Ashlesha</note>, Ardra (well known), Fire <note>original: "Agni"; Krittika</note>, Yama <note>Bharani</note>, <gloss>do not go alone</gloss> the two Bhadrapadas, and the Fathers <note>original: "Pitara"; Magha</note>. In these, and in the three stars of Tvashtar <note>the Chitra triad</note>, making nine in total, the teachers say <term>travel</term> <note>original: "yana"; the act of setting out on a journey</note> is not auspicious. The remaining constellations are considered middling. Now, regarding the lunar days <gloss>in</gloss> which travel is auspicious: the "Empty" days <note>term: Rikta tithis; the 4th, 9th, and 14th lunar days, considered unfavorable for new beginnings</note>, the "Joint" days <note>the Full and New Moons</note>, and the 6th and 8th days are well known as unfavorable. The first day of the lunar fortnight is also considered inauspicious for setting out. By implication, travel on other lunar days is auspicious. Another point is mentioned: Mercury <note>original: "Jna"</note> and Venus. One should not travel toward the direction of Mercury or Venus; that is, one should not face these planets when starting a journey. Now, regarding the directions: In the East, etc., one should avoid certain stars. In the South, from the latter half of Dhanishtha, there are five constellations to avoid. Thursday <note>original: "Jiva-var"; the day of Jupiter</note> is for kings—

In the southern direction, one should avoid travel <note>The text uses 'tyajyet', meaning to abandon or avoid, referring to inauspicious directions for travel based on the day</note>. In the western direction, one should not avoid the 'Kaka Mita' <note>likely a specific astrological calculation or bird omen</note>. The following should be avoided in the west: Rohini <note>the 4th lunar mansion</note>, the Sun <insert>Aditya-vara or Sunday</insert>, and Venus <insert>Sukra-vara or Friday</insert>. This is the meaning of avoiding them in the west.

In the north <note>Udak</note>, one should avoid Mars, Mercury, and the 'Artha-snas'. Mars is Tuesday <note>Mangalavara</note>; Mercury is well-known <note>Budhavara/Wednesday</note>; 'Artha-matto' refers to the Nara Phalguni <note>the Purva Phalguni constellation</note>. These are to be avoided in the northern direction.

Now, regarding the **Yogini** <term>Yogini: a female deity or energy whose position in the sky changes daily, dictating which directions are auspicious or dangerous</term>, it is said: "Three, full, eight..." and so on. These refer to the lunar dates <note>Tithis</note>. Starting from the Northeast <note>Isanyatah</note>, one should place them in order. These are: thirty-eight (38) <note>likely a numerical code for specific dates</note> from the Northeast; nine (9) in the East; eleven (11) in the Southeast; thirteen and fifteen (13, 15) in the South; twelve (12) in the Southwest; six and fourteen (6, 14) in the West; fifteen and seven (15, 7) in the Northwest; and ten and twelve (10, 12) in the North. Thus it is explained.

I shall now explain the determination of the **Raudra** and others. Starting from the "Fire-star" <note>Krttika, the Pleiades</note>, there are groups of seven. This is what is meant: having drawn a "Seven-Seven Diagram" <note>Saptasapta-Chakra</note>, there are seven lines facing East. One should place the seven stars starting with Krttika from the Northeast. Then, place the next seven on the lines going South. Similarly, place them on the Western and Northern lines in sequence. Then, one should draw a line going from the Northwest to the Southeast. This is called the "Face Line" <note>Mukha-rekha</note> in the completed diagram.

The purpose of this diagram is stated: "The wind and fire stay in Krttika..." Wind <note>Vayu</note> and Fire <note>Agni</note> are well-known; their directions are the Northwest and Southeast respectively.

Remaining within those boundaries, one should not cross the perimeter. Now, he explains another purpose, saying "Now..." and so on. At any time, one should not travel toward the south during the <term>Nakshatras</term><note>Lunar mansions or star clusters used in Vedic astrology to determine auspicious timing.</note> associated with the west. One should not travel toward the north during the eastern Nakshatras. He mentions an exception, saying "Blameworthy..." and so on. One should not travel during those eastern Nakshatras if they are considered blameworthy. When is this? If the "desired direction" is favored by the <term>Ishta Lagna</term><note>The specific auspicious zodiacal sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of departure.</note>. If the Ishta Lagna is beneficial—meaning if the Ishta Lagna possesses the three characteristics and qualities to be described later—then one may go. Otherwise, one should not. What should be done? One must avoid the <term>Shula</term><note>Literally "the spike" or "spear"; refers to specific inauspicious directions associated with certain days or stars.</note> star. Even if the desired direction and the Ishta Lagna are favorable, one must not travel if the Shula star is present.

Now, he describes a special rule regarding even those stars deemed "blameworthy," saying "The Horse..." and so on. <term>Ashwini</term><note>The first Nakshatra, symbolized by a horse's head.</note> is well-known; <term>Anuradha</term><note>The 17th Nakshatra, associated with friendship.</note> is "the friend"; <term>Hasta</term><note>The 13th Nakshatra, symbolized by a hand.</note> is "the Sun"; and <term>Pushya</term><note>The 8th Nakshatra, considered very auspicious.</note> is well-known. With these stars, one may travel in all directions. This means the restrictions of the six senses <insert>or directions</insert> do not apply here.

Now he says something else: "From the mixed..." and so on. Dividing the day into three parts: in the first part, called "Mixed," one should not travel during the "Ordinary" or "Fixed" Nakshatras. In the second part, <unclear>one should avoid</unclear>... and in the final part (the third part), one should not travel during the "Sharp" Nakshatras. 

Now, regarding the night: a person should travel during the soft, <unclear>sharp</unclear>, and moving stars respectively. In the first part of the night, travel with the "Soft" stars; in the second part, with the <unclear>... </unclear>; and in the third part, a person should travel with the "Moving" stars. He mentions a special exception here: "In Shravana, Hasta, Pushya, and Mrigashira..." this rule does not apply. <term>Shruti</term><note>original: "Shruti"</note> refers to <term>Shravana</term>; <term>Arka</term><note>original: "Arka"</note> refers to <term>Hasta</term>; <term>Jya</term><note>original: "Jya"</note> refers to <term>Pushya</term>; and <term>Mriga</term> is well-known <note>Mrigashira</note>.

<page-num>3</page-num>
<meta>Catchword: Shu-</meta>

The meaning is that one may travel in all directions under these <term>Nakshatras</term><note>Lunar mansions or star clusters used in Vedic astrology to determine auspicious timing.</note>. Now, regarding the <term>Abhijit</term><note>The "intercalary" or 28th lunar mansion, located between Shravana and Uttarashadha; it is considered highly auspicious for overcoming obstacles.</note> lunar mansion and its effects: it is said that the stars belonging to the Universal Gods <note>original: "Vaishva," referring to the Uttarashadha Nakshatra</note> and the <unclear>Achyuta</unclear> <note>original: "Shravana," the star associated with Vishnu</note> form this period. Specifically, the first fifth of the tenth part of Shravana is known as Abhijit. This Abhijit period, occurring during the day, grants success in all directions except for the South. Even in times of distress, the Abhijit <term>Muhurta</term><note>A unit of time approximately 48 minutes long.</note> grants success, provided it is not at night. For those undertaking a journey <note>original: "Yatrukanam"</note>, it shall bestow the desired fruits.

Now, another point is stated regarding the favorability of the Sun and Moon's paths. The <term>Ayana</term><note>The half-year journey of the sun; either Northward or Southward.</note> of the Sun <note>original: "Arka"</note> and the Moon <note>original: "Chandra"</note> should be favorable for your journey, as ordained by the Sages. If there is a difference between day and night, they should be favorable in their respective order. 

The meaning is this: The Sun is the lord of the day, and the Moon is the lord of the night. If both are in the <term>Uttarayana</term><note>The Northern solstice/path, generally considered more auspicious.</note>, then traveling Northward during the day or night is praised. If both are in the <term>Dakshinayana</term><note>The Southern solstice/path.</note>, then traveling Southward during the day or night is praised. If the two are in different paths, then during the day, it is best to travel in the direction of the Sun’s path; at night, it is best to travel in the direction of the Moon’s path. Otherwise, it is not considered auspicious. 

When travel to the South is praised, travel to the West is also excellent. At that time, travel to the East is also excellent. Here, the West should be understood as connected to the North, and the East as connected to the South. Therefore, when Northward travel is praised, Eastward travel is also excellent. As stated in the <term>Ratnakosha</term><note>The "Jewel-Treasury," a classical Sanskrit text on astrology and omens.</note>: "When the Sun reaches Capricorn and the following signs <insert>the Northern path</insert>, one should go North or East; when it reaches Cancer and the following signs <insert>the Southern path</insert>, one should go South or West."

If the remainder is six, seven, or two, it is *Adala*. If the remainder is based on the mountain-count (seven) and the desired position, it is *Vidula*. According to the words of the divine sage Devala, travel during *Adala* or *Vidula* is not beneficial.

This means: by consuming grain and traveling by elephant toward the East, success is achieved. By consuming sesame-rice and traveling by chariot toward the South, success is achieved.

In the absence of these specific foods or vehicles, the decision is as follows: if one possesses the proper mindset even without the specific vehicle, it can still be a "Good Journey." Now, regarding the purification of the Ascendant <term>Lagna</term> <gloss>6</gloss>: one should speak of the auspicious Ascendant. A night-time Ascendant is considered stronger than others. <meta>This page continues the technical analysis of astrological signs and their orientation, specifically focusing on the "Rising from the Back" signs and the planetary lords of the directions for travel timing.</meta>

At the beginning of the page, the signs that rise from the back <note>original: "Prishthodaya"; these are signs that emerge on the horizon with their rear parts first</note> are listed: Taurus, Aries, Capricorn, Cancer, and Gemini. These are declared by the scriptures to be the "Back-Rising" signs, with the exception of Gemini <note>which is sometimes categorized differently depending on the school of thought</note>.

From a birth chart featuring a Back-Rising sign, one should not predict destruction. Regarding the Aquarius sign: if the lord of the birth sign and the lord of the path <note>the direction of travel</note> are enemies, it causes physical distress. This means the ruler of the birth sign and the ruler of the travel sign are at odds. If the planet is weak at the time of birth, or if the planet is weak during the journey, one should entirely avoid departing when such a planet occupies the ascendant of the journey.

Now he describes the Lords of the Directions: The lords of the directions, starting from the East, are the Sun, Venus, and so on, in their respective order. If a Lord of the Direction is situated in the <term>Four Cardinal Houses</term> <note>original: "Kendra-Chatushtaya"; the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses of a horoscope</note>, one may proceed. However, if the lord is "on the forehead" <note>a technical term for a planet blocking the path</note> in that cardinal house, travel is forbidden.

He explains the definition of "Forehead-Position": When a planet is at the front of its direction or "on the forehead," one should not enter upon a journey or reside abroad. When the Sun or Mercury are in the ascendant, or Saturn is in a particular position, they are said to be on the forehead. Furthermore, if Venus is in the 12th house (the house of loss), it is also considered to be on the forehead. If the Moon is in the 5th house, it is considered to be on the forehead in the Northwest direction. 

A retrograde planet <note>original: "Vakri"; a planet that appears to be moving backward</note> situated in a cardinal house is not a "maker of the night" <note>original: "Nishakara"; here used metaphorically to mean a bringer of auspiciousness or a successful journey</note> for the traveler. Even the day associated with a retrograde planet is not auspicious for travel. If it is in a cardinal house, it will not bring benefit to the one undertaking the journey. However, the divisional chart <note>Varga</note> of the ascendant itself is not necessarily inauspicious. <meta>The final lines contain fragmented references to the "Janma-Nakshatra" or birth star and its relationship to planetary pillars.</meta>

### On the Birth Stars of the Planets

Now, regarding the birth stars <term>Nakshatras; the 27 lunar mansions of the zodiac</term> of the planets: The birth stars of the planets are as follows:
*   **Vishakha** is the birth star of the **Sun** <note>original: Surya</note>.
*   **Krittika** is the birth star of the **Moon** <note>original: Chandra</note>.
*   **Purva Ashadha** is the birth star of **Mars** <note>original: Bhauma</note>.
*   **Shravana** is the birth star of **Mercury** <note>original: Budha</note>.
*   **Purva Phalguni** is the birth star of **Jupiter** <note>original: Guru</note>.
*   **Pushya** is the birth star of **Venus** <note>original: Shukra</note>.
*   **Revati** is the birth star of **Saturn** <note>original: Shani</note>.
*   **Bharani** is the birth star of **Rahu** <note>the North Node of the Moon</note>.
*   **Ashlesha** is the birth star of **Ketu** <note>the South Node of the Moon</note>.

If the birth star of a planet is the same as the current presiding star, and that planet is situated in the Ascendant, it causes harm. However, there is an exception: if the second sign is Gemini and it is occupied by an auspicious planet in the Ascendant, it becomes favorable. At the time of birth or a "re-birth" <note>likely referring to a significant life event or initiation</note>, those planets that are joined with auspicious influences are considered very beneficial.

From the birth Moon <note>the sign the Moon occupied at birth</note>, the planets situated in the 3rd, 6th, 10th, and 11th houses <term>Upachaya; houses of growth and improvement</term> are exceedingly auspicious. Similarly, planets situated in these same "growth houses" relative to the birth Ascendant are also very beneficial. Even naturally "malefic" planets <note>such as Mars or Saturn</note> become very auspicious if they are in their own sign, their sign of exaltation, or a friend's sign.

The Lord of the Birth <note>the planet ruling the birth sign</note> and the Lord of the Birth Ascendant are considered very auspicious if they are placed in the "angles" <term>Kendra; the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</term>. A planet is beneficial when it is in the Ascendant during its own day or the day of a friendly planet. Conversely, it may be harmful if it is in the body of an enemy. As the sage **Chyavana** <note>An ancient Vedic seer credited with medical and astrological knowledge</note> says: "The lords of the birth should be employed in the Ascendant of the journey to ensure success for the traveler..." and so on. Now, regarding the protection offered by the Ascendant of the journey... <page-num>55</page-num>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of planetary positions (Muhurta) for auspicious timing, specifically focusing on house entry and marriage rituals. It transitions from general planetary warnings to specific exceptions for travel and domestic rites.</meta>

With a single verse, the author explains: "The noble ones have departed." The malefic planets <note>original: "khala," referring to naturally "cruel" planets like Mars or Saturn</note> are situated in the 3rd, 6th, and 11th houses, as well as the 2nd and 8th. Others are in the 12th, 4th, 5th, 8th, 9th, and 10th. When malefic planets—specifically Saturn, the son of the Sun—are positioned in the 6th, 8th, or 12th houses from the Ascendant <term>Lagna: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at a specific moment</term>, they cause distress. 

If the Moon is in the 7th, 4th, 12th, or 5th houses, or if Venus is in the 7th, or if Jupiter is "destroyed" <note>meaning combust or obscured by the Sun's rays</note>, or if Mercury is in the 12th house joined with malefic planets—whether joined with one, two, or many—these positions each become "soul-destroyers." This means they grant death or various types of ruin. However, if they are in other positions, such as the 3rd, 6th, or 11th, they are always givers of true happiness. 

When should these be avoided? If a planet is "combust," it must be shunned. 

Now, the author explains the exceptions regarding Mercury and Venus: "In one's own village..." <margin>Exceptions for local travel</margin>. One's own village is well-known; a single town is well-known; and one's own home is like one's self. In a house with four verandas <note>original: "chatur-asya-alinda," a traditional architectural style</note>, and in the seven types of entries, or in marriages—specifically the "Entry of the Bride" <term>Vadhupraveśa: the ritual first entry of a bride into her husband's home</term>—if there is fear of the King <note>political instability</note> or the pain of famine, the usual planetary restrictions do not apply. 

Furthermore, when going on a pilgrimage, or by the command of a master, or for those belonging to the lineages <term>Gotra: patrilineal clan descent</term> of Bhrigu, Vashistha, or Angiras—specifically the Bharadvaja and Vatsya lineages—travel is beneficial even if Mercury or Venus are positioned directly in front of the traveler. 

Regarding the "Entry of the Bride," the fault of an opposing Venus <note>Shukra-dosha: an inauspicious alignment of Venus for travel</note> is not mentioned here if she is traveling with her husband. The fault only applies if she travels without her husband. As stated in the *Bhima-Parakrama*: "For a newly married woman..." <insert>the text cuts off here</insert>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the "Counter-Venus" (Prati-shukra) phenomenon and the rules for a king's journey.</meta>

# Page 56

Regarding the direction of the lord, it is said that **Counter-Venus** <note>original: "Prati-shukra"; an astrological position where Venus opposes the direction of travel, usually considered inauspicious</note> does not exist. Likewise, in another text, it is mentioned that for a **newly married bride** <note>original: "navodha"</note>, this restriction is not applicable. This should be understood by the wise through the strength of the traditional scriptures. 

As **Lalla** <note>An ancient astronomer, author of the Shishyadhivaldida</note> says: when entering one's own home, or during the conflict of the planets, or in a marriage, or when a new bride enters her home, there is no consideration of Counter-Venus. 

**Vashistha** <note>A legendary sage and author of astrological treatises</note> states: Among the lineages of Kashyapa, Bhrigu, Angirasa, Bharadvaja, and Vatsya, the Counter-Venus does not exist.

Now, another exception is stated regarding the **Moon**: When the Moon is in **Taurus or Aries** <note>original: "Rishabha-Mesha"</note>, Venus becomes "blind" <note>meaning its negative influence is neutralized</note>. Specifically, when the Moon is in the constellations of Revati or the sign of Aries, traveling facing Venus is *auspicious*. 

As **Garga** <note>One of the oldest authorities on Indian astronomy/astrology</note> says: As long as the Moon stays in the signs from Krittika to the fire-deity's constellation, Venus remains blind; therefore, both entry and departure are auspicious.

Next, the necessity of movement is described regarding the **East and West**: Due to the *reversal of Venus* in the East or West, one may travel for essential duties. This means: when Venus has gone to the eastern horizon, one may go **West**, and when it has gone to the western horizon, one may go **East**. 

As stated in the ***Light of Astrology*** <note>original: "Jyotih-prakasha"</note>: As long as the planet remains in its respective horizon, one should travel in the opposite direction; if it is situated in the West, one may travel East.

### Now, the Rule for the King’s Journey is stated:
The **King** should travel one, four, or six *koshas* <note>A traditional unit of distance, roughly 2 miles or 3 kilometers</note> each day. This means: *on the first day, he should go one kosha; on the second day, four koshas; and on the third day, six koshas.* Thereafter, he may travel as he pleases. 

What kind of King? One who has **entrusted the fire and the treasury** <note>original: "vahni-bhastra"; likely referring to the sacrificial fire and the bellows or bags of wealth</note> to his companions. The "bellows" or "bags" refer to his life-long friends and kinsmen; he who has entrusted the fire and the treasury to their hearts...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Grahapravesha (the ritual of first entry into a new house), focusing on the timing and astronomical alignments required for a prosperous home life.</meta>

# On the Determination of Day and Night for Rituals

Regarding the decision between day and night in matters of house entry, it is said that auspicious work should generally be performed during the day. Any significant task is ordained for the daytime; it is never prescribed for the night. Only in cases of extreme necessity <note>original: "sahati-bhave," implying an emergency or unavoidable circumstance</note> is entry at night permitted, and not otherwise. As the sage **Vasishtha** <note>A legendary Vedic sage and author of several astrological treatises</note> says: "Entry during the day brings auspiciousness, including the growth of sons and grandsons. Entry at night is a fault caused by the Sun's absence." By the strength of the "Fish-Banner" <note>likely a reference to a specific yogic or planetary alignment</note>, the day is preferred.

### On the Purification of the Vastu and the Pot Worship

Now, the purification of the **Vastu** <note>The architectural deity or the site itself</note> and the worship of the sacred pot are described. Regarding the "Sun-Tooth" <note>original: "Surya-danti," a technical term for a specific calculation</note> cycle: in a group of five constellations <term>Nakshatras: lunar mansions or star groups used in Vedic astrology</term>, one must calculate from the Sun's position. If the entry occurs under certain constellations—specifically the eighth constellation from the Sun—it is not auspicious. This implies that other constellations are favorable for entry.

### On the Auspicious Ascendant for Entry

Now, the **Lagna** <term>Ascendant: the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of the ritual</term> for entry is discussed. House entry is auspicious when the Ascendant is a "Fixed" sign <note>signs like Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, or Aquarius which represent stability</note> and is in an **Upachaya** position <note>the 3rd, 6th, 10th, or 11th houses from the birth sign, associated with growth and increase</note> relative to the homeowner's birth star and birth Ascendant. The entry should be performed when the planets are positioned as they were at the start of the building's construction.

Furthermore, the entry is successful when the Sun is in a "Leftward" position. What are the characteristics of the constellations facing the house? Entry is ordained through those constellations that face the direction of the house's door.

### On the Characteristics of the "Leftward" Sun

Now, the definition of the "Leftward Sun" is explained. From the various houses of the zodiac—the eighth, fifth, second, and eleventh—the Sun's position is calculated. When the Sun is situated in the eighth or fifth house relative to the direction the house faces, it is considered "Leftward" and therefore auspicious for the owner. Specifically, for a house facing East, the Sun is "Leftward" when it resides in the signs from the eighth to the fifth. For a house facing South, the calculation shifts accordingly from the eleventh to the fifth. <note>hook-shaped line filler</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the ritual preparations for a royal or significant journey (Yatra), focusing on the offerings, social obligations, and the psychological state required for success.</meta>

<margin>

57

</margin>

On the second lunar day, the traveler becomes successful. Having performed the fire sacrifice with fuel sticks, sacrificial porridge, and sesame seeds <note>original: "samid-caru-tila"</note>, and having satisfied the earthly gods <term>Bhusuras: a traditional term for Brahmins</term> with priestly gifts <term>Dakshina: ritual honoraria</term> and other offerings; and having satisfied friends, sons, and others with garments and ornaments, one should depart. This is the core idea. What should one be doing? One should abandon the desire to return out of affection <note>original: "snehan-nivrittim"</note>. One must let go of the thought of returning home immediately. After going outside for the sake of the journey, one should not look back at the house out of affection for sons and others. How should he be? The king himself, well-fed, should not depart—so it is heard—if his heart is filled with anxiety or longing for the task at hand. 

Likewise, it is said in the *Trivali*: from the house, from running, from the village, one should avoid the crying of beloved ones and dogs. One should reach the learned ones. And so says **Brihaspati**: "Having honored the gods and the powerful wise ones, and having performed a circumambulation <note>walking clockwise around a sacred object</note>, one should remain focused on the goal." 

Likewise, in the *Jyotish-Prakasha* <note>Light of Astrology</note>: "Having satisfied the Brahmins of one's own village, and being pure in speech and mind, wearing white garments and flowers, one should depart." 

And so says **Kashyapa**: "At the time of travel, if there is an unfavorable planetary alignment, one should worship with cows, land, gold, and clothes, and honor friends and the astrologer." 

In the *Brihad-Yatra* <note>The Great Journey</note>: "Bathed, anointed, adorned with all ornaments, wearing white clothes, the glorious traveler should be full of courage. A king's favorite should not insult Brahmins, nor should he approach his own wife for intimacy once the journey has begun, especially if he desires success." 

**Lalla** <note>An 8th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note> says: "He who, having set out on a journey, insults Brahmins—his life and wealth will not return with him." 

In the *Brihad-Yatra*: "He who has set out on a journey should not rejoice with his wife, nor should the wife rejoice <insert>in his presence</insert> at that time."

<summary>This page outlines the ritual and behavioral protocols for embarking on a journey, emphasizing the satisfaction of Brahmins, the abandonment of domestic attachments, and the warnings against insulting the learned or engaging in domestic pleasures after departure.</summary>
<keywords>Yatra, Brihaspati, Kashyapa, Lalla, ritual offerings, Brahmins, travel ethics, astrology, Brihad-Yatra</keywords>

# On the Ritual of Return and Departure

Regarding the ritual of return, it is stated in the *Jewel Necklace* <note>original: "Ratnamala," a famous medieval treatise on astrology and ritual</note>: On the first day, one should travel a distance of two miles <note>original: "krosha"</note> or perhaps half that. On the second day, the King should not travel more than eight miles <note>original: "yojana"</note> before halting. On the third day, he may travel twelve miles <note>original: "sardha-yojana"</note> while mounted upon his Great Jewel <note>original: "Maharatna," likely referring to a royal elephant or a consecrated vehicle</note>. After that, the journey may proceed as desired.

Now, regarding the rules for the journey, it is said: One must avoid anger (which is well known), theft (well known), sexual pleasure and intercourse, exhaustion, distress, meat <note>original: "amisha"</note>, disease <unclear>passion</unclear>, death, <unclear>fainting</unclear>, and foul smells (well known). One must also avoid liquor <note>original: "asava"</note>, and the breaking of vows or musical instruments, which causes fear. One should avoid dark-colored garments, <insert>which are inauspicious</insert>, and instead wear red or other bright colors. One must avoid vomiting, oil (well known), and pungent spices like black pepper.

Next, he explains for how many days these things should be abandoned. Regarding theft and similar vices, one should abandon them for three to five days, or even a week. Regarding passion, one should abandon it as long as it persists. Menstrual contact should be avoided for as long as it lasts. On the day of departure, one should avoid dark-colored tilaks <note>ritual forehead marks</note> and instead use white or other auspicious substances.

Even during the Ritual Departure <term>Prasthana: a symbolic start to a journey made at an astrologically auspicious moment when the actual travel is delayed</term>, one must practice these restraints. The Ritual Departure means moving some cherished object at the exact auspicious moment <note>original: "muhurta"</note>. As stated in the *Jewel Necklace*: "A seeker of victory should move his parasol, weapons, or whatever is dear to his heart toward the direction of conquest." This is done when practical circumstances prevent immediate travel. However, in such a symbolic departure, the full merit is not obtained.

Thus, in the *Jewel Treasury* <note>original: "Ratnakosha"</note>: "If the King himself cannot go due to business, some teachers say he should send his parasol, weapons, or a cherished victory-sign in his stead." And the Sage Garga says: "When a man departs in person, even if filled with fear, the full fruit of his journey is successfully realized."

Furthermore, regarding the rules of movement in the texts: one should not travel at night. Garga says: "One should not cross the boundary of the kingdom at night." In the autumn, according to Bharadvaja and Vasistha <note>ancient Vedic sages and authorities on law and ritual</note>...

<summary>This page outlines the ritual distances a king must travel during the first three days of a journey and lists physical and moral prohibitions, such as avoiding meat, anger, and dark clothing. it also explains the concept of 'Prasthana,' where a king may symbolically begin a journey by moving sacred objects if he is personally delayed.</summary>
<keywords>Prasthana, royal ritual, astrology, Ratnamala, Garga, Vasistha, travel prohibitions, Maharatna</keywords>

<page-num>58</page-num>
-> 58 <-

One should go according to the prescribed rules. Even if a journey has been initiated, if one must depart on another day, the *Light of Astrology* <note>original: "Jyotih-prakasha," a classical astronomical treatise</note> states: if an obstacle arises during the departure, one should avoid the journey on days marked by great hunger or unfavorable planetary alignments, unless there is a "womb-junction" <note>term: Garbha-yoga; a specific astrological configuration relating to the formation of results</note>, and one should perform a ritual bath upon seeing the time. Even during such a departure, one must renounce anger and other vices.

**Garga** <note>An ancient sage and authority on astronomy/astrology</note> says: "One should abandon anger, shaving, lust, greed, the shedding of tears, childbirth impurities, meat, jaggery, and oil when setting out."

In the *Jewel-Casket* <note>original: "Ratnakosha"</note>, it is said: "One should not desire a journey at that moment if there is yellow sandalwood, asafoetida, saffron, red sandalwood, or yellow and red garments."

In the *Great Treatise on Travel* <note>original: "Brihad-yatra"</note>: "Eating pungent oil, eggs, or meat, or eating out of delusion while traveling, leads to returning as a sick man. One who is intoxicated, diseased, terrified, exhausted, angry, or arrogant should not set out on the road; likewise, one dressed as a eunuch should not go."

In the *Jewel-Necklace* <note>original: "Ratnavali"</note>: "Whether it is one's own woman or another's, or even a man—if one strikes them while leaving, his life comes to an end."

**Bhrigu** <note>One of the premier Vedic sages</note> says: "One should avoid milk for three nights, and blood-letting for five days. On the day of departure, one should avoid washing the head, and for an equal number of days, one should renounce sexual intercourse."

The signs of departure during a journey should be known as spoken by **Garga**: "When the auspicious moment <term>Muhurta</term> arrives, one should depart after considering the movement of the breath <note>original: "hamsa-sanchara," referring to yogic breath-control or the 'swan-step'</note>. Surrounded by astrologers, ministers, Brahmins, elders, and kinsmen, the king should first perform the rites of departure. After walking thirty-two steps, he should mount his vehicle and proceed."

Now, he describes the omens <term>Shakuna</term>: "Garments and ornaments—specifically white garments—are auspicious. Among the omens: gold-made items, the *Eni* <note>a species of deer</note>, the *Kalapa* <note>a peacock's tail or ornament</note>, the sound of musical instruments, a man with a son, the *Vardhamana* <note>a specific mystical diagram or a 'prosperous' person</note>, a mirror, a king, a sleeping person, an elephant, a cow, and a fortunate woman <note>a 'Saubhagyavati,' usually meaning a married woman whose husband is alive</note>."

<summary>This page details the ethical and ritual prohibitions for travelers, such as avoiding certain foods and emotions, and lists auspicious omens and the proper protocol for a king's departure.</summary>

# On Auspicious Omens

The primary white grains are considered gentle and auspicious. These are listed in the middle of the traditional verses. By the phrase "and so forth," one should include gold and similar treasures. A swing <note>dola</note> is favorable. A lamp is favorable. One should observe a flame that is "without smoke," meaning a steady, clear light. Fragrant substances include items such as musk, sandalwood, and others. A "good bull" refers specifically to a white bull. 

The text mentions "canopy cloths" <note>vitana</note> which are well-known, as are the "fly-whisks" <term>Chamar: a ceremonial whisk made from the tail of a yak, used as a symbol of royalty or divinity</term>. A young maiden is favorable. "Fish" refers to those recently taken from the water. "Gallstone of a cow" <term>Gorochana: a bright yellow pigment found in the gallbladders of cattle, used in tilaks and medicine</term> is also favorable.

Now, the author speaks with brevity: Whatever is seen that causes "absolute satisfaction to the heart" <note>svanta: the inner self or mind</note>—whatever generates deep joy—that should be considered a good omen. What more needs to be said? This summarizes the principle that a donor's feeling of satisfaction is a key indicator.

The ancient teachers have also listed the following among the omens:
*   Dung taken from the earth <term>Gomaya: cow dung, considered purifying in Vedic tradition</term>
*   Earth or clay <note>mrittika</note> taken from the ground
*   A companion who is a "bearer of matted hair" <note>likely an ascetic or yogi</note>

Regarding the water-pot: a person should not travel with a companion carrying an empty water-pot. If a traveler encounters one, they should turn back, as it signifies a "hollow" or failed purpose. 

Other favorable sights include:
*   A washerman carrying washed (clean) clothes
*   Sacred grass <term>Darbha: Desmostachya bipinnata, used in sacrificial rituals</term>
*   A chariot or vehicle on the ground
*   Meat <note>pala-mamsa</note>, specifically when it is fresh and bloody
*   Cooked rice, provided it is not associated with mourning or "crying"
*   White mustard seeds <term>Siddharthaka: literally 'that which brings success'</term>
*   Curds, ghee (clarified butter), and honey

As stated before, "whatever causes absolute satisfaction to the heart" is the guiding rule. Therefore, reading these lists is not a fault but a way to understand what brings peace. Other authorities agree; as the sage Bharadvaja <note>a legendary Vedic seer</note> says: "Curds, grains, Durva grass, a full water-pot, white mustard, sandalwood, a mirror, ornaments, meat, fish, cow-gallstone, cow-dung, and earth taken from the ground are all auspicious." So too are a King, a Brahmin, and a pregnant woman.

<summary>This page catalogs various auspicious omens (shakuna) such as white animals, specific plants, and ritual objects, emphasizing that the ultimate test of a good omen is the "satisfaction of the heart." It cites the sage Bharadvaja to validate the list of lucky sights for travelers and practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>omens, shakuna, Bharadvaja, cow dung, white mustard, Chamar, auspicious sights, Vedic ritual, travel omens</keywords>

A young maiden, a woman recently delivered of a child, a man, a woman, an elephant, a horse, or a white bull; a cow of any other color tied with a rope, and especially a cow with her calf; lifted cow dung <note>used for purification and fuel</note>, fish, a memory of something pleasant, or a glass vessel carried by someone; a washerman with washed clothes, a vessel filled with urine <note>In certain tantric or folk contexts, this was occasionally viewed as a sign of 'fullness' or fertility, though more often it refers to 'Purna-Kumbha' or water jars</note>, the sound of a wheel, the sounds of victory and auspiciousness, the recitation of peace prayers <note>Shanti Patha</note>, a small lamp or a blazing torch; a full water-pot, a river, a large fish, meat, blood, and various types of wine; gems, earth, betel leaves with nuts <note>Tambula</note>, clothes and ornaments, or silver, copper, pearls, and gold; a mirror, a canopy or an awning, a copper vessel, clarified butter <note>Ghee</note>, a fan, a flag, a palanquin, or a beautiful tree; a son, a parrot, a clean person, an astrologer, or one's own household deity—these things inspire enthusiasm. Having seen these, a man should set out on his journey; he shall then attain all his goals. All these things mentioned are auspicious when seen on the right side. If one encounters them on the right side, the journey will be very successful. If they appear on the left side, one should not proceed.

Now, the inauspicious omens are described: ashes, a heap of dust, a barren woman, or trees that bear fruit only from their bark <note>without flowers</note>; stones, rocks, salt, or a person from a garden or a meadow; a blacksmith <note>literally: a worker in iron</note>, oil-cake <note>pressed sesame seeds</note>, medicine, black grains such as black gram <note>Masha</note>, molasses, oil, iron, or loose hair; a person who is intoxicated, cotton, or red flowers like the China rose <note>Japa</note>; weeping, a person with a shaved head, or fire accompanied by smoke; a person who is arrogant, one who has vomited, one who is hungry, or a killer of living beings; a wicked person, a person with a completely shaved head, one with matted hair, or a person suffering from disease; one who is lame in the feet, one who is deformed or missing a limb, a naked mendicant, an ascetic <note>Sanyasi</note>, or a wandering monk wearing ochre-colored robes. 59

# On Omens and Obstructions

The head of Time <note>original: "Kāla"; likely referring to the personification of death or the astrological moment</note> is a well-known thief. One who is not a devotee, one smeared with oil, one wearing a garland, one who is soiled, or an outcast who has lost their caste status—these are all seen as ill-omened. A pregnant woman, a cow intended for slaughter, or a man carrying a wooden bolt <note>original: "argalā"; a bar used to fasten a door</note> are also signs of obstruction. 

The black snake is a serpent tied to darkness. The frog <note>original: "marūka"</note> and the chameleon <note>original: "jāhuka"</note> are remembered by the wise as "the circling ones" <note>original: "maṇḍalī"</note>, as stated by the authority Halayudha. In common language, these are known as "mountain-dwellers."

### Obstructions on the Path

The buffalo, the donkey, the camel, and the female buffalo are considered heavy omens. An empty pot, a severed head, or the trembling of a living being's head or limbs are signs of impending failure. 

**Obstructions of the Way** <term>mārgavedha: a technical term for an obstacle that "pierces" or blocks the success of a journey</term> occur when the path is blocked by spiders and similar creatures. A battle or combat is indicated by the falling of buffaloes and others, or the slipping of one's own clothes. If a vehicle, such as a horse, flees, it indicates a quarrel. 

A eunuch <note>original: "ṣaṇṭha"</note>, or the sight of a cow's tooth in a burning place, or the sound of "haha" and "sphūri" are considered "blocking omens" <note>original: "pratibandhakāḥ"</note>. These prevent the completion of a task. Signs involving severed limbs are givers of death. One should not engage in activities involving grass, oil, or medicine when these omens appear.

### Auspicious Animal Signs

Other omens are described hereafter: The deer, the bee <note>original: "bhramara"</note>, and the cuckoo. The camel and the "King Parrot" <note>original: "rājaśuka"</note> making its sound, as well as clawed animals like dogs, and small birds like sparrows—if these appear on the **left side**, they grant the heart's desires.

The black buffalo, the rooster, and the "crested one" <note>the peacock</note> are well known. The mongoose, the lion, and the ram—these, along with the skylark <note>original: "bhāradvāja"</note> and others, are fruitful if they are seen while one is moving. The meaning is this: even the sight of a donkey or a wild ass can grant half of the desired result under specific conditions.

<summary>This manuscript page catalogs various animal omens and physical signs that predict success or failure for a traveler, distinguishing between "blocking omens" and those that grant desires based on their position.</summary>
<keywords>omens, divination, animal signs, travel obstacles, Sanskrit medicine, astrology, Halayudha, path-piercing</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on omens and augury, specifically focusing on when "bad" omens are actually favorable and the remedies required if one encounters multiple negative signs before a journey.</meta>

->The Giver of Desired Objects<-

Now, the author describes those specific activities in which "reverse omens" <note>omens usually considered unlucky, such as those appearing on the left side</note> are actually auspicious. In the case of *Nayutara* <note>a specific astrological calculation or timing</note>, or in situations of fear, entering a village or a new place, in battle or conflict, in gambling, or when searching for a lost object, and in the treatment of diseases—in all these matters, reverse omens are givers of good fortune. 

The meaning is that those omens which are considered "bad" for setting out to acquire wealth are actually auspicious for *Nayutara* and the other activities mentioned. Furthermore, when the *Ida* breath <note>the "moon" breath flowing through the left nostril, associated with cooling and mental activity</note> is active, reverse omens are auspicious. When the *Ida* is active, every omen on the left is favorable. Conversely, when the *Pingala* breath <note>the "sun" breath flowing through the right nostril, associated with physical heat and action</note> is active, every omen on the right is praised. In battle, in entering a new home, in releasing a prisoner, in looking for lost items, in illness, and in crossing rivers, the opposite of the usual rules for travel applies. In these cases—*Nayutara*, fear, war, entry, and finding the lost—reversed omens are praised among men.

Now, the author explains what must be done if a bad omen occurs at the very start of an undertaking. If a first bad omen occurs, one should turn back, sit in a clean place, and having purified oneself by washing hands and feet and performing *Achamana* <note>the ritual sipping of water for purification</note>, one should perform *Pranayama* <note>breath control</note> eight times and then proceed. 

If a second bad omen occurs, one should turn back, rest for a long time, and regulate the breath sixteen times <note>doubling the previous eight</note>. If a third bad omen occurs, one should give a gift of gold and then proceed. This applies only to journeys that are absolutely necessary. 

As stated in the *Jyotish-nibandha* <note>a classical compendium on Vedic astrology and omens</note>: "Upon a contrary omen, one should first perform eight breath-restraints. Upon the second, do double that. Upon the third, one should not go anywhere at all." This is repeated for emphasis: upon a contrary omen, perform eight breath-restraints; upon the second, double that.

<summary>This page details specific exceptions where typically "bad" omens are considered lucky—such as during war or illness—and outlines the ritual breathing and purification required to neutralize bad omens encountered before a journey.</summary>
<keywords>omens, augury, Pranayama, Ida and Pingala, Jyotish-nibandha, purification rituals, travel omens</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on auspicious timing (Muhurta), transitioning from the rules of travel to the rituals of royal consecration and the installation of deities.</meta>

One should not travel anywhere <note>without consulting the omens</note>. Thus, Vasantaraja <note>a renowned 12th-century authority on omens</note> states: "In a place known for many omens, after performing the purification of hands and feet and sipping water <term>Achamana</term> (ritual purification), one should stand beneath a sturdy tree and observe the various omens." If a bad omen occurs, one should give gold to a virtuous person and then proceed without fear. Thus ends the Chapter on Travel in the Commentary on the *Sun of Auspicious Moments* <term>Muhurta Martanda</term>.

Now, desiring to speak on miscellaneous topics, the author discusses the timing for royal consecrations and religious vows. One should not perform these on the "Birth Day" <note>the day of the week or lunar day corresponding to one's birth</note>. When the birth-related factors—such as the birth sign, the current planetary period <term>Dasha</term>, and the birth stars—are strong and favorable, and when the "soft" and "fixed" constellations are present, the establishment of a king on his throne is praised. This should occur on days other than those ruled by Mars <note>the "son of the earth"</note>. Specifically, the planetary periods of the Sun, Venus, and Mercury are considered best. The strength of the Ascendant <term>Lagna</term> should be evaluated as previously described in the section on the "Lotus of the Heart."

Next, regarding the installation of deities and the commencement of fasts: In the auspicious months, excluding the month of Chaitra <note>March-April</note>, and specifically during the five months starting with Margashirsha <note>November-December</note>, on days other than those ruled by Mars, the installation of a deity should be performed. This should be done under the deity's own specific constellation or under "fixed," "movable," or "soft" stars. This applies to the installation of all divine images. The strength of the Ascendant is as stated before.

Now, regarding the wearing of new clothes and their first washing, the results depend on the constellations such as Uttara: One should wear new clothes under Dhanishtha, Punarvasu, the five "Hand" stars <term>Hasta</term>, Shravana, Revati, Ashwini, and the "Fixed" stars.

<summary>This page concludes the section on travel omens and details the astrological requirements for royal consecrations and the installation of religious idols, emphasizing the strength of specific planetary periods and constellations.</summary>
<keywords>Vasantaraja, Muhurta Martanda, royal consecration, deity installation, astrology, auspicious timing, constellations, planetary periods</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of auspicious timing (Muhurta) for clothing and jewelry, specifically focusing on washing garments and the omens of damaged fabric.</meta>

<margin>Om 62 61</margin> The previously mentioned <term>Nakshatras</term> <note>lunar mansions or star clusters used in Vedic astrology</note>, when they fall on a Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday, are excellent for wearing new clothes; one should not wear them at any other time. As it is said: on Sunday, Thursday, and Wednesday, one gains water and lotus-like beauty; on Tuesday, sweat and heat; on Wednesday, wealth; on Thursday, knowledge; on Friday, happiness. Listen to the strength of the <term>Lagna</term> <note>the ascending zodiac sign</note> as previously described. 

Now, the rules for washing clothes are explained: The same stars mentioned for wearing clothes—excluding the constellation Swati—are suitable for washing. How should this be done? It is said that one should avoid Wednesday for washing. Even for gold ornaments and pillars, all such auspicious actions are prescribed, yet for washing, Wednesday is forbidden. Regarding bathing and washing, it is said: "Washing clothes on a Monday or Wednesday destroys the eighth and seventh generations of one's family." <note>This reflects a common astrological taboo against washing clothes on specific weekdays to avoid bad luck.</note>

Next, the results of clothes being eaten by rats are described: If a garment is "rat-eaten"—meaning bitten by a mouse—in the middle section, it is considered <term>Asata</term> <note>inauspicious or bad</note> and will yield fierce results. This means: if you fold the cloth into three parts and the damaged portion falls in the middle section, it is defective. "Rat" is used here as a general category; this also applies to clothes burnt by fire, torn by thorns, or stained by ink and such. As it is said: "A garment that is burnt, torn, or smeared with cow dung, fish, or mud, or bitten by creatures with tusks or teeth, is inauspicious if the damage is in the middle section."

Now, regarding women wearing red clothing and ornaments: The mention of "red" here implies excellence. It is said: A woman should wear red garments and gold ornaments. Under what conditions? In the <term>Dhruva</term> <note>fixed</note> constellations, excluding Punarvasu and Pushya. As it is stated, the fault of a woman wearing red clothes and ornaments is removed in these specific timings. Thus it is said: "In the primary solar and fixed constellations, gold and red garments are auspicious." 62

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological discourse on auspicious timing (Muhurta), specifically focusing on the planetary influences over clothing and the commerce of livestock.</meta>

For these individuals, the strength of the ascendant <note>The zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon at the time of an action</note> should be considered as previously described. Now, the author explains the excellence of Mars and the other planets. Regarding Mars and the Sun: Mars and the Sun <note>original: "Kujarkau"</note> are the causes for wearing blood-red garments. This means one should wear clothes the color of red blood on their days. Similarly, on the day of Saturn <note>Saturday</note>, one should wear dark or variegated clothes, and on Monday, one should wear white garments and ornaments like pearls. The implication is that on Monday, white clothing should be worn. Here, it is said that "all should be worn" according to the specific planetary dictates.

Now, regarding the rules for men wearing new clothes, the text describes the specific lunar mansions <note>Nakshatras</note>. In a fixed ascendant, for the benefit of a man's adornment, one should avoid certain times. Specifically, one should avoid the "windy pair" and the "physicians." This refers to the stars Svati and Vishakha <note>the windy pair</note> and Ashvini <note>the celestial physicians</note>. One should wear clothes in constellations other than Svati, Vishakha, and Ashvini. The strength of the ascendant remains as previously stated.

Now, the second part of the verse explains a special rule. Regarding Brahmins <note>the priestly class</note>, receiving clothes through marriage or as a gift results in gain. When clothes are obtained through the favor of a king, from a Brahmin, or out of affection during a festival, they may be worn even if the astrological timing is otherwise prohibited. As Vyasa <note>a revered legendary sage</note> has said: "Clothes received through royal favor, from a priest, or out of love during a great festival should be worn immediately; the prohibitions regarding the lunar day <note>Tithi</note> and other factors do not apply here."

Finally, the latter half of the verse discusses the buying and selling of animals. In the constellation of Mitra <note>Anuradha</note>, and in the sign of the Water-bearer <note>Aquarius</note>, one may engage in trade. Excepting the sign of Aquarius, when the ascendant is favorable and the wealth-house is pure <note>free from the influence of "malefic" planets like Mars or Saturn</note>, the buying and selling of animals—such as horses, elephants, and cattle—should be performed. One should not expand their business unless these conditions are met. When one's own wealth-house is purified, the rest of the results follow as previously described. So it has been stated in the tradition.

<summary>This folio outlines the astrological requirements for wearing new clothing based on planetary colors and lunar mansions, while providing exemptions for gifts. it also details the auspicious zodiac signs and planetary purities required for the successful trade of livestock.</summary>

When the tenth and eleventh houses are occupied by the Ascendant <term>Lagna</term>, and the houses of wealth, centers <term>Kendra</term>, and trines <term>Trikona</term> are occupied by auspicious planets, one should perform work related to trade, excluding the rising of the eighth sign. Now, regarding the latter half of the verse, he speaks of the prohibitions during the five-day period <term>Panchaka</term> when the moon is in Dhanishtha: In the matter of **cremating a corpse** <note>original: "Pretajvalane"</note>, etc., when the moon is in the four signs starting from the eleventh <note>Aquarius and Pisces</note>, one should not perform any such acts. This is clear.

Now, in the latter half of the verse, he discusses service <note>employment or devotion</note>. Service is desired under the **fixed and other constellations** <note>original: "Dhruvadibhir nakshatraih"</note>; this is easy to understand. On which days? On the days of the Sun or Mars. That is to say, when the Sun or Mars are in the tenth or eleventh houses from the specific Ascendant <term>Lagna</term> in which the service is commenced, that day is considered auspicious for beginning service. The rest is as previously stated.

Now, with the fourth line, he discusses **Renunciation** <note>original: "Sanyasa"</note>. Renunciation is desired during fixed constellations and in a fixed Ascendant. By whom? By the "wicked ones" being powerless—meaning when the malefic planets are devoid of strength <note>The third case ending is used here in the sense of the seventh case/locative</note>. When does this occur? When **Venus** <note>original: "Bhargave"</note> is in the sixth or eighth house. The remaining strength of the Ascendant is as described before. It is also said: "When Saturn is in the third, sixth, or eleventh, and Venus is in Aries, and the Lord of Dharma <note>Jupiter</note> is strong in a center <term>Kendra</term>, it creates a brilliant ascetic."

Now, with two verses, he discusses **agricultural work** <note>original: "Krishikarma"</note>. The "earth-born" constellations <note>Mula, etc.</note> are eleven in number. The "movable" ones are well known. Vishakha is mentioned twice. The **light constellations** <note>original: "Laghubhani"</note> are also well known. Regarding the planets: excluding Saturn, Mercury, and Mars. This means on a day that is free from the influence of Saturn, Mercury, and Mars. This is easy. The seventh house should be Virgo. This is easy. In Gemini, the seventh house is Pisces.

<margin>62</margin>
