# On the Commencement of Agriculture

The Bull <note>Taurus</note> and the Maiden <note>Virgo</note> are well-known and considered favorable by me. When the Ascendant is active, that which is "without a spot" <note>original: vyanka; referring to a flawless astrological calculation</note> is sought. One should avoid the ninth lunar day; therefore, the "spotless" time is that which is an odd-numbered lunar day, excluding the ninth. <margin>Odd days are generally considered dynamic or "male" in Vedic astrology.</margin>

When the moon is in a favorable position, specifically on the 2nd, 3rd, 5th, 7th, 10th, 11th, 12th, 13th, or 15th lunar days, agriculture shall be auspicious. As stated in other texts:

-> When the Moon is auspicious, on a favorable lunar day and during a beneficial planetary alignment—specifically on Thursday, Monday, Friday, or Sunday—and when the Ascendant is in Leo, Gemini, Taurus, or Virgo, one should proceed with the matters of agriculture. <-

Regarding the specific lunar days <term>Tithi: a lunar day in the Hindu calendar</term>:
The 10th, 11th, 3rd, 13th, 5th, 7th, and the 1st are the days that bring success. However, the 6th day is hard on the bullocks, the 9th day destroys the harvest, the 8th day harms the "Lord of the Night" <note>the Moon/the farmer's vitality</note>, and the 14th is equally avoided. One should perform the ploughing on the days of the Sun, Jupiter, Moon, or Venus. The days of Saturn and Mars do not bestow fruitful results.

### The Ritual Method
Now the procedure is described: The practitioner, being skillful and dressed in white garments <insert>symbolizing purity</insert>, should offer salutations to the birds, the earth, the deer, and the gods. Having bowed with mind, speech, and action, he prepares the plough. The plough should ideally have "golden horns" <note>likely referring to gilded decorations or symbolic value</note> and be fitted with a golden-colored share.

The bulls used must be well-proportioned, strong, and free from disease. With these excellent bulls, one should draw five lines <note>furrows</note> in the earth while facing the North. These five lines are described as follows:
1. The first movement represents a boat <note>crossing over difficulties</note>.
2. The second is a turning back.
3. The third is a forward movement again.
4. The fourth is a turning.
5. The fifth completes the movement.

In this manner, the lines are produced. One should not do more than what is required. The "Somi" <note>a ritual measurement or vessel</note> should be moved in six steps to ensure the lines are made with unbroken horns <note>referring to the integrity of the ritual tools</note>.

<margin>Om 63 63</margin>

As it has been stated: under auspicious lunar mansions and planetary alignments, one should bathe and dress in clean white garments. Having honored the Earth and the planets with scents and flowers—specifically offering to the Earth—one is reborn <note>likely referring to a spiritual renewal or the "birth" of the crop</note>. After circumambulating the sacred fire, a priestly gift <term>Dakshina: a ritual fee or gift given to a priest</term> should be given. 

One should employ two stalks of Kusha grass <note>Desmostachya bipinnata, a sacred grass used in Vedic rituals</note> with clarified butter. With the tip of the plow polished or adorned with gold, one should create an unbroken furrow. Facing the North, a long line should be drawn for the sake of prosperity. One should avoid using a plow that is broken or a bull that is reddish-brown <note>Kapila: specifically a tawny or reddish cow/bull, sometimes considered ill-omened for specific tasks despite being sacred</note>. 

In the work of driving the plow, one should use bulls that are free from disease. If the plow or the equipment breaks at the start, it will not be auspicious. If the bulls become agitated, it brings about confusion and loss. Therefore, one should perform the work without obstacles to ensure victory and success. 

The third furrow is said to be the heart of the teacher; the fifth furrow is the one that yields the fruit of the harvest. Beyond this, one should not count <note>referring to the ritual first plowing</note>, or great faults may arise. Venus is to be remembered by word, and the Moon is the lord of the beasts. The plowman who performs his work with dedication sees the merit of all sacrifices and penances. 

Now, the omens are described: if an accident occurs while the plow is moving, it brings constant misfortune. If the furrow is broken during plowing, it threatens the life of the practitioner. If the yoke breaks, it signifies the loss of property; if the leveling wood breaks, a child may die. If the plowshare breaks, there is a risk of great sorrow and loss of sons. 

If a bull falls down while the plow is in motion, or if it seeks to escape the bond as soon as it is released, one should predict fever, dysentery, or the breaking of the agricultural enterprise. If the bull digs the earth with its hooves immediately upon being harnessed, or if it bellows after being released, the harvest will be abundant.

Next, regarding the subject of agriculture, the "Plow Chart" <term>Langala-chakra: an astrological diagram used to determine the best placement and timing for plowing</term> is explained: starting from the Sun's position, three units are placed in each of the eight locations... 63

When the stars <note>Nakshatras</note> are positioned such that there are three inauspicious ones, the dual result occurs repeatedly. This is what is meant: starting from the star occupied by the Sun, the first three stars are "blind" <note>Kanan</note> and inauspicious, yielding undesirable results. After that, the next three are "virtuous" and auspicious. Then follow three inauspicious ones where no good occurs, followed by five virtuous ones. Then in the "approaches," three are inauspicious, followed by three virtuous ones in the "consumer of offerings" <note>Hutabhuj, an epithet for Fire/Agni</note>.

Now, he describes the <term>production of seeds</term> <note>Vijotpatti</note> through agriculture. It is said that the <term>sowing of seeds</term> <term>Vijavapa</term> is auspicious during the <term>agricultural stars</term> <term>Krishi-nakshatras</term>, such as the "moving" ones and so on. Why is this mentioned for men? Regarding the agricultural stars, it is said in the context of marriage that the stars of Rudra, Varuna, and the stars of Hari <note>Vishnu</note> are used as a characteristic mark. Therefore, everything regarding the timing of speech and action for agriculture is stated there. 

As it has been said: "There is fear of mice when the Moon is in certain positions, and fear of locusts and insects. One should avoid these especially when the Moon is waning. One should sow all crops when the Ascendant <note>Lagna</note> is Taurus, Pisces, Virgo, or Gemini, if one desires prosperity." This refers to the strength of the timing.

Now, regarding the <term>sowing of seeds</term>, he describes the <term>Serpent Diagram</term> <term>Phani-chakra</term>. From the star occupied by the Sun, the third one, and from there the nineteenth <note>Atidhriti</note>, and then by groups of eight—specifically eight from the third and eight from the nineteenth—these stars yield virtuous results for the sowing of seeds.

Now, he mentions a third variation according to "others" <note>other authorities</note>. By other teachers, it is said that the sowing of seeds is auspicious during the twelve stars measured from the seventh star from <term>Rahu</term> <note>The North Node of the Moon, personified as a celestial serpent</note>, known as the "interval of the hood." This is what is meant: the star in which Rahu is located, the seventh star from that is called the "Seventh of the Serpent." From that "hood" <note>the seventh star</note>, the next twelve stars are auspicious. The name "Serpent" <note>Phani</note> for Rahu is well-known in other treatises. The great teacher <term>Varahamihira</term> has also described the various forms and alternatives of Rahu...

As stated in other texts regarding the unseen enemy <note>original: "adrishtarittah"; referring to Rahu, the eclipse-causing shadow planet</note>, in his own Compendium <note>original: "Samhita"</note>, it is said that even though the head of the Demon <note>original: "Asura"; referring to Swarbhanu/Rahu</note> was severed by the Discus, it did not lose its status as a planet. Because it was cast out from the solar and lunar orbs, it remains in the sky, appearing only during eclipse periods. According to the Creator <note>original: "Kamala-yoneh"; literally "Lotus-born," referring to Brahma</note>, its body is divided into a head and a tail and is perceived in the form of a serpent. Because of this serpent-like description, the name "The Hooded One" <note>original: "Phani"</note> is well-known. Also in the *Jewel-Garland* <note>original: "Ratnamala"</note>, it is clear that the seed-sowing period should avoid the influence of the Hooded One. Similarly, in another scripture <note>original: "Agama"</note>, it is said that one should avoid sowing from the sign occupied by Rahu up to the eighth sign.

Now, he describes the harvesting of grain: "by the stars of harvesting and sowing." It is stated that these actions should be done under the stars prescribed for sowing seeds. Now, a doubt arises: since the characteristics of these stars were already mentioned previously, is this not a repetition? It is true, but there is a slight distinction between the two regarding the dark fortnight; to remove the doubt of which actions should be performed then, he specifies "by the stars of harvesting and sowing."

Next, in the proper sequence for the wealth of grain, he discusses the installation of the threshing pillar <note>original: "Medhi"</note>. One should install or fix the <term>Medhi</term>—which is the central pillar erected in the threshing floor for the purpose of treading the grain—under the stars prescribed for marriage, such as Mula and others. The word *Medhi* refers to the post to which the oxen are tied for the purpose of treading grain; as the *Amarakosha* <note>A famous Sanskrit thesaurus</note> states: "The *Medhi* is the pillar in the threshing floor to which the cattle are bound." This should be done under the marriage stars, excluding the "Upas" <note>a technical abbreviation</note>. "Upa" refers to the star Uttara Phalguni; therefore, it should be done excluding the two Uttaras <note>Uttara Phalguni and Uttara Ashadha</note>. It is also said: Rohini, Revati, Mula, Swati, Hasta, Magha, as well as Ashadha, the Uttaras, and Bhadrapada are praised for the sowing of all seeds. <insert>64</insert>

->**On the Ritual of New Grains and the Arts**<-

In the entry into a house, the constellations <term>Radha</term><note>original: "Radha," another name for the constellation Vishakha</note> and <term>Uttara Phalguni</term> do not grant happiness and are like a forest fire. The <term>Shalmani</term><note>The Silk-cotton tree</note>, the <term>Audumbari</term><note>The Cluster Fig tree</note>, the <term>Dhatri</term><note>The Amalaki or Indian Gooseberry</note>, and those trees that exude a milky sap or bear feminine names should be avoided by farmers, as they do not yield the desired fruit.

### The Ritual of First Grains (Navanna)

Now, the ritual of <term>Navanna</term><note>The ceremony of eating the first harvest of the season</note> is explained through the second stage of the <term>Annaprashana</term><note>The ritual of a child's first feeding of solid food</note>. Wise men state that the <term>Navanna</term> rite should be performed during the annual festival or in accordance with the rules of <term>Annaprashana</term>. Therefore, the lunar days (tithis) and days of the week prescribed for a child's first feeding should be adopted here. Without this ritual, the consumption of the nine types of newly harvested grains—such as rice—is considered a transgression; thus, the rite is mandatory.

The nine grains are:
1. **Brahmi** <note>likely a variety of rice or sacred grain</note>
2. **Barley** (Yava)
3. **Wheat** (Godhuma)
4. **Niwar** (Vara) <note>wild rice</note>
5. **Millet** (Kangu)
6. **Vairaka**
7. **Rice** (Shali)
8. **Mung beans** (Mudga)
9. **Urad beans** (Masha)

Among these, grains that grow during the rainy season should not be eaten without the <term>Agrayana</term><note>The Vedic first-fruits sacrifice</note> of rice. Those growing in the spring, such as barley, should not be eaten without their respective rites. This is established in the religious codes (Dharmashastras) and Puranas. The <term>Navanna</term> should be performed in the sixth or eighth month, following the lunar dates previously mentioned.

### On Commencing Dance and Music

In the latter half, the commencement of dance and music is described. One should begin the study of dance under the following constellations:
*   **Anuradha** (Maitra)
*   **Hasta** (Arka)
*   **Revati** (Antya)
*   **Dhanishtha** (Vasavo)
*   **The three Uttaras** <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>
*   **Pushya** (Ijya)
*   **Shatabhisha** (Varuna)
*   **Jyeshtha**

Music and dance should be initiated when the Ascendant (Lagna) is favorable and when Jupiter is in a strong position. Specifically, it is auspicious when the "benefic" planets <note>such as Mercury, Venus, or Jupiter</note> are situated in the fourth house (Hibuka). 

Regarding the performance for the deities: the constellations for lighting the lamps and beginning the performance are **Vishakha** (Dipa), **Hasta** (Arka), **Punarvasu** (Aditi), **Pushya**, and **Dhanishtha** (Vasavo).

The constellations of Shiva <note>Ardra</note>, Ashwini, and the first part of the others; between these lies the fire <note>Krittika</note>, the auspicious Shatataraka, Indra <note>Jyeshtha</note>—by these groups of three, the "Lion-less" signs are determined. In those zodiac signs which are devoid of the Lion <note>Leo</note>, when they serve as the rising sign <term>Lagna|The specific zodiac sign ascending on the eastern horizon at a given moment</term>, the performance of various efforts is declared auspicious. This means that activities such as buying and selling are said to be favorable at such times.

Now, regarding the exceptions for the latter half: On the days of the Sun <note>Sunday</note>, the Moon <note>Monday</note>, and Mercury <note>Wednesday</note>, one should avoid the combination of the "Three Uttaras" <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>, Rohini, and Chitra. 

Furthermore, on the New Moon <term>Amavasya|The lunar phase of the new moon</term> and the "Empty" days <term>Rikta|Tithis or lunar days considered 'empty' or unfavorable for new beginnings: the 4th, 9th, and 14th</term>, as well as the well-known eighth lunar day, the performance of various efforts is not declared beneficial. 

Now, regarding the days of the week: If the day matches the ruling planet of the constellation, the flaw <note>Dosha</note> is neutralized; in such a case, there is no fault in performing specific actions. Regarding the strength of the rising sign, the Lord has said: On the days of the Sun and Mercury, if the Sun is "burnt" <note>combust</note>, actions are hindered. On the eighth day, or during the constellations of Chitra or the fixed stars <note>Dhruva</note>, or during the yoga of Vaidhriti or Patas, or when Mercury is in Leo, or during a solar transition <term>Sankranti|The passage of the Sun from one zodiac sign to another</term>, or on an "Empty" day, one should avoid performing good deeds. 

When a benefic planet is rising, or in the "soft" constellations, all types of activities involving livestock and trade are remembered by the Sages as being "complete" or successful. Regarding the three days—Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday—cows should not be moved, and the exchange of horses and other animals should be avoided. On the days of Venus and Saturday, the trade of clothing, cattle, and buffalo is discussed; the movement and speed of various types of horses and animals are considered fulfilled through these specific rules. 

Now, regarding the performance of one's own work: Punarvasu, Pushya, Dhanishtha, and Shatataraka are the stars...

# On the Auspicious Timing for Equine Activities

The constellations <term>Mṛgaśīrṣā</term> <note>The 'Deer's Head' constellation</note>, <term>Anurādhā</term>, <term>Revatī</term>, <term>Svātī</term>, <term>Puṣya</term>, and <term>Hasta</term> are favorable. When the Sun, Mars, Jupiter, or Venus preside over the day—specifically when Jupiter or Venus are rising in the <term>Lagna</term> <note>The ascendant or the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</note>—it is highly auspicious. This means the ascendant should be occupied by Jupiter or Venus. Furthermore, if auspicious planets occupy the second, fourth, seventh, or tenth houses from the ascendant, the buying and selling of horses will result in prosperity.

## The Wheel of Horse Astrology

Now, the "Wheel of the Horse" is described. Even among the previously mentioned constellations, one must calculate from the <term>Nakṣatra</term> <note>Lunar mansion</note> occupied by the Sun. Counting sequentially, the five constellations following the Sun's position represent the body of the horse. When the moon passes through the three constellations including <term>Abhijit</term> <note>An intercalary or 'victory' constellation often used in electional astrology</note>, the purchase or training of a horse is deemed most excellent.

Having spoken generally, the specific duties are now addressed. Any action taken regarding horses that does not align with these celestial harmonies will not result in the desired nourishment or success. One must act according to the prescribed constellations. Wise men should perform these tasks—such as training or trading—under the influence of the aforementioned stars.

As the sage **Nārada** stated:
-> "In moveable signs, in gentle constellations, or in fixed signs, the work concerning horses is powerful, especially on the day of the Sun (Sunday)." <-

It is further said that for the first feeding of ghee, salt, milk, or grass, one should choose a time when a gentle planet is rising, similar to the ceremony for a child's first solid food. For grooming, clipping of hair, or administering medicine, the rules for tonsure or medical treatment apply. For the mating of horses, one should observe the proper planetary alignments. The construction of the stable should be undertaken when the planets are auspiciously positioned. Under the guidance of a teacher, and when the omens are favorable, leatherwork and the fitting of ornaments should be done during a "Yoga of Victory" <note>Jaya-yoga</note> and an auspicious rising sign. In this same manner, all matters concerning camels and other mounts should be managed.

->**The Prohibited Times for Ritual Oil Baths**<-

The sage Bhrigu states that one should avoid an <term>Abhyanga</term><note>A ritual oil massage or bath, considered essential for health but spiritually sensitive to timing</note> during the following times: the <term>Bhadra</term><note>An inauspicious period in the lunar calendar</note> period, the <term>Sankranti</term><note>The moment the sun enters a new zodiac sign</note>, and the astronomical yogas of <term>Vyatipata</term> and <term>Vaidhriti</term>. One should also avoid Fridays, Thursdays, Sundays, and Tuesdays. Furthermore, specific lunar days (<term>tithis</term>) are forbidden: the 6th, 10th, 8th, 12th, 1st, 5th, 2nd, 13th, 14th, and the 30th (New Moon).

If there is no pressing reason, one should avoid an oil bath on the first and second days of the lunar fortnight. However, if there is a valid cause, one may proceed. As it is said: "One should not touch oil on the 7th, 9th, or 1st days." Specifically, the 8th and 6th days are to be avoided. Another text states: "On the 4th, 8th, 6th, Full Moon, and the Sun's transition (Sankranti), one should not perform an oil bath, as it leads to the loss of children, kin, and wealth."

<margin>Vriddha Manu</margin>
The ancient authority <term>Vriddha Manu</term> says: "One whose parents are living should not bathe on the New Moon. Nor should one bathe on the 9th unless there is a specific ritual requirement. On the 1st day, it destroys wealth; on the 2nd, it harms children; on the 10th, the bather becomes poor; and on the 13th, it destroys everything."

The text <term>Ratnamala</term> explains the exceptions for an oil bath, specifically for auspicious occasions. These include <term>Mangalya</term><note>Auspicious events like weddings</note>, victory festivals, and the 10th day of the bright half of the month of Ashvin (Vijayadashami). Other exceptions include the New Year's Day and the famous <term>Deepavali</term> (Diwali) festival. In these cases, the usual prohibitions do not apply.

As stated in the <term>Vyavahara Sara</term>: "At the start of the year, during the Sun's transition, during great festivals, and on the 14th day during the Festival of Lights (Diwali), the restrictive rules for oil baths are not mandatory." Similarly, the <term>Skanda Purana</term> notes: "On <term>Vijayadashami</term><note>The festival celebrating the victory of good over evil</note>, even if it is a forbidden day, the ritual oil massage must be performed." <margin>66</margin>

<summary>This page details the specific astrological days and lunar dates when ritual oil baths are prohibited to avoid misfortune, while also listing major festivals like Diwali and Vijayadashami as mandatory exceptions to these rules.</summary>
<keywords>Abhyanga, oil bath, ritual timing, Sankranti, Diwali, Vijayadashami, Vriddha Manu, Ratnamala, Hindu calendar, astrology</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on ritual purity and daily conduct, specifically focusing on the rules for oil massage (Abhyanga) and the auspiciousness of specific days and substances.</meta>

Out of respect, he now states the exceptions to the prohibition of oil massage. Regarding the phrase "from what has been stated": in the case of daily oil massage using the oils previously mentioned, it is indeed auspicious. <gloss>Note: This refers to the standard daily practice.</gloss>

By the use of the word "etcetera," it is understood that oils such as mustard oil or oils scented with fragrant flowers are protected <note>meaning they are exempt from the usual prohibitions</note>. Thus, using these stated oils is auspicious even on days usually forbidden. As it is said: "Mustard oil, scented oil, and oil infused with flowers or other substances are never defiled." Even on Sundays, Fridays, and Tuesdays, or on forbidden lunar days <term>Tithi: a lunar day in the Hindu calendar</term>, if one must protect the body with oil during a bath, one should use specific additives: on Sunday use flowers, on Thursday use white mustard, on Tuesday use earth <note>clay or soil</note>, and on Friday use cow dung. Adding these makes the oil bath conducive to happiness.

Regarding the specific rules for daily massage, it is said: "In the case of a daily practice, the entire oil <note>the restriction on specific oils</note> does not apply." It is also stated: "When a daily bath is a duty, the faults associated with the lunar day do not apply," and so on.

Now, in this context, he lists the forbidden lunar days for bathing with sesame and Myrobalan fruit <term>Amalaka: the Indian Gooseberry, used for ritual purification</term>. On the tenth, the eleventh <note>dedicated to Vishnu</note>, the thirteenth, the seventh, the second, the ninth, and the new moon day—on these lunar days, one who desires prosperity should not perform a bath with Myrobalan fruits. For it is said: "He who bathes with Myrobalan on the tenth day will suffer the loss of his sons; on the thirteenth, the loss of wealth; on the second, the loss of wealth; and on the new moon, the ninth, and the seventh, the destruction of his lineage."

Next, he mentions the prohibition of oil massage for women on Wednesdays and other specific times. On Wednesday, during the menses <note>the menstrual period</note>, or under the ancestral star <term>Magha: a specific lunar mansion or Nakshatra</term>, oil massage is not good. A woman who does so becomes a "husband-killer" <note>a traditional expression indicating it brings great ill-fortune to the spouse</note>. As it is said: "Those women who bathe when the moon is in the Shatabhisha star <note>the 'physician' star</note> will surely be widows and unfortunate for seven births." This is according to the tradition of Trivikrama. One should also avoid Sundays, Tuesdays, Fridays, and the transition of the sun into a new sign <term>Sankranti: the solar transit</term>.

<summary>This manuscript page details the ritual exceptions and prohibitions for oil massages and baths using Myrobalan fruit, emphasizing how specific additives can neutralize "bad luck" on forbidden days. It warns of dire consequences, such as loss of wealth or family, for violating these astrological and calendrical rules, particularly for women.</summary>

On the sixth day, or the thirteenth, women should bathe. Now, regarding the prohibition of oil massage on Mondays as spoken by Sushruta: Atri and Sushruta state that one should not bathe on a Monday. It is said by Sushruta: "One should not always eat in one's own home like a glutton; similarly, the wise should always avoid oil massage on Mondays." <note>original: "somavāre tathābhyangaṃ varjayet"</note>

Now, in this context, the prohibitions for the post-partum bath <note>The ritual bath taken by a woman after childbirth to mark the end of the period of ritual impurity.</note> are stated. Yama mentions the following lunar mansions: Bharani, Magha, Tvashtra (Chitra), Indu (Mrigashira), Jyeshtha, the "mixed" ones (Vishakha and Krittika), and the star of the Man-eaters (Mula), and the star of Ishvara (Ardra). In this collection of stars, if a son is born, he may not thrive or may become a "bad son." Because of this, one should not bathe when a malefic planet is present. Furthermore, a woman who has recently given birth should not bathe at night or during the evening twilight. When the ascendant <note>term: lagna (the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon)</note> is auspicious and the fifth house is free from malefic planets, and great faults are avoided, then the post-partum bath should be performed.

Now, the preparation of medicine is discussed: "Mandara" refers to Saturn (Manda) and Mars (Ara). Excluding these two, on the days of the Sun, Moon, Mercury, Jupiter, and Venus, under the fixed, mobile, and swift lunar mansions, during the bright fortnight, and during the "Nectar Union" <note>term: Amrita Yoga (an auspicious astrological timing for success)</note> or other famous auspicious yogas, and when the centers (quadrants) are occupied by benefics and the eighth house is empty—in such a state, the physician <note>term: Bhishak or Vaidya (traditional titles for a doctor of Ayurveda)</note> should administer the treatment. The remaining strength of the ascendant should be as previously described. Now, the knowledge of medicine is stated: It should be given at the beginning of the day, etc., when the stars of Mercury and the Sun are avoided. Now, the specific details regarding blood flow are discussed.

# On Bloodletting and the Treatment of Wounds

The practice of <term>bloodletting</term><note>original: "asrk-srava" or "rakta-srava"</note> and the treatment of wounds should be performed on the days of the Sun and Mars <note>Sunday and Tuesday</note>. This should be done when these two planets—the Sun and Mars—are positioned in the <term>cardinal houses</term><note>the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a horoscope, known as Kendra</note> and are not afflicted. 

As it has been stated: excluding the day of Saturn <note>Saturday</note>, one should perform bloodletting under the <term>movable, brahminical, soft, and fixed lunar mansions</term> when the Sun and Mercury are in the cardinal houses. Alternatively, one may perform wound treatments during the <term>Hora</term><note>astrological hour</note> of Mars, or when Mars is in a cardinal house, or when Saturn and the Sun are weak. 

When Mars and the Sun are in cardinal houses, or on the eighth day of the lunar fortnight, or during the <term>Muhurta</term><note>an auspicious period of 48 minutes</note> of Mars, and when a <term>movable sign</term> is rising on the horizon, these actions are successful.

## On the Duration of Diseases

Now, in this context, the days for the duration of diseases are described:

The stars <term>Bharani</term><note>associated with Yama, the god of death</note>, <term>Ashlesha</term><note>associated with the Serpent</note>, <term>Swati</term><note>associated with the Wind</note>, <term>Mula</term>, <term>Shatabhisha</term><note>associated with the Waters</note>, <term>Revati</term>, <term>Jyeshtha</term><note>associated with Indra</note>, <term>Purva Ashadha</term>, and <term>Hasta</term>—diseases beginning under these <term>lunar mansions</term><note>Nakshatras</note> leave the patient after nine days. 

This is the opinion of the astrologers. Furthermore, it is mentioned in the commentaries that if one falls ill with a fever under certain stars, death is predicted; while under others, the fever is released after nine days. To pacify the negative effects of these stars, specific rituals are prescribed, as different results are sometimes heard of elsewhere. 

Where it is said that a fever is released in seven, nine, or eleven days, the author has stated "nine days" here for the sake of brevity in the text, as the difference is slight and represents a middle value. Thus, the experts in astrology say: [the duration varies] by the influence of the Rudras, the Manus, and the sages <note>This refers to numerical systems used in Sanskrit verse to denote 11, 14, or 7 days respectively</note>.

### On the Recovery Bath

In the latter half of the verse, the bath for one who has been freed from disease is described:

The stars <term>Punarvasu</term><note>associated with Aditi</note>, <term>Ashlesha</term>, <term>Swati</term>, and the <term>Fixed stars</term><note>Rohini, Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note> are mentioned. Also <term>Revati</term>...

# On Post-Illness Bathing and Financial Transactions

<margin>68</margin>

In the cycle of lunar mansions <note>Nakshatras</note>, if one has recovered from a disease <term>Gada-tyakta-snana: the ritual bath taken after recovering from an illness</term>, taking this bath on a Friday or a Monday may lead to misfortune. It should not be performed everywhere or at all times. He explains: "When the lunar date <note>Tithi</note> or the mansion is unfavorable," specifically when "afflicted by malefic planets" <note>Khala: literally 'wicked' planets like Mars or Saturn</note>. If a planet is setting <note>Astagata: heliacal setting, becoming invisible due to proximity to the Sun</note>, and if the rising sign is fixed <note>Sthira-lagna</note>, then the outcome remains stable or balanced.

As it has been stated: "During the yoga periods of *Vaidhriti* and *Vyatipata* <note>two of the twenty-seven astrological yogas considered inauspicious</note>, or during *Bhadra* <note>a specific period of the lunar day</note>, or during the Sun's transition into a new sign <note>Sankranti</note>, a man who has been freed from disease should bathe." Even if the day, season, or lunar date is generally unfavorable, if it is joined with the strength of the Moon and is auspiciously viewed by benefic planets, it becomes beneficial for the post-illness bath. The strength of the Moon <term>Chandra-bala</term> must be applied in all cases. By saying "even when the lunar date or mansion is unfavorable," he suggests that one should not be confused regarding the strength of the Moon and other factors; the Moon's strength is indicated here as paramount. Since it was stated at the beginning of this book regarding the ceremony of conception <note>Garbhadhana</note> that "the Moon must be present," this rule should be understood for all ritual acts. Thus it is clearly said: "One freed from disease should bathe when the Moon and the stars are both favorable."

### On Financial Transactions

Now, he discusses the handling of wealth and goods in a single verse:

Regarding "Wealth" <note>Dravya</note>: In the transaction of gold and other assets, or in the exchange of sacred grass <note>Kusha</note> and other ritual items, one should use the "Light" and "Swift" lunar mansions <note>Laghu and Chara Nakshatras</note> for giving or paying. Where should the planets be? In the transit of the rising sign <note>Gochara-lagna</note>. Specifically, when Mercury is in the 9th, 8th, or 5th house from the self <note>the Ascendant</note>. This means when it is in the middle of the 9th, 8th, or 5th houses. All other wealth should be given accordingly.

Furthermore, regarding the recovery of stolen goods or debts: This should not be done on a Wednesday, or on a Tuesday <note>Kuja/Bhauma-vara</note>, or on the day of the Sun's transition <note>Sankranti</note>. However, it may be done during the *Tripushkara* yoga <note>a "triple-multiplier" alignment where actions are said to repeat three times</note>. It is also mentioned regarding the mansion *Bharani* and the *Panchaka* <note>a group of five specific mansions starting from Dhanishtha</note> that during the *Vriddhi* <note>increase</note> yoga, the recovery of wealth may be performed. These are the considerations for the "hands" <note>Hasta-kha, likely referring to the Nakshatra Hasta or manual dealings</note> and the calculation of the groups.

That which is given, inherited through family lineage, increases without loss, except on <term>Wednesday</term> <note>original: "budhadine"; the day of Mercury</note>, as it is said. And so it has been stated by the wandering sages: when the Ascendant is in a "moveable" sign, and <term>Mercury</term> <note>the planet of commerce</note> is in the ninth, fifth, or eighth house, the <term>employment of wealth</term> <note>original: "dravyaprāyoga"; investment or lending</note> through business and other means is remembered by the sages as auspicious. One should perform collections under the Bharani star, and with the "Five-fold" <note>Panchaka</note> or during the month of Chaitra. Regarding <term>debt-related actions</term> <note>original: "ṛṇakarma"</note>, the <term>clearing of debt</term> <note>original: "ṛṇacchedaḥ"</note> should be done on <term>Tuesday</term> <note>original: "kujehaniḥ"; the day of Mars</note> under "swift" or "moveable" stars. 

The wise say that under the "Five-fold" <note>Panchaka</note> configuration, results are multiplied five times; under the "Three-lotus" <note>Tripushkara</note> yoga, they are tripled; and in the "Pair" <note>Yamala</note>, they are doubled, whether in terms of loss or gain. One should not incur <term>debt on a Tuesday</term> <note>original: "ṛṇabhaumena"</note>, nor should money be given out on a <term>Wednesday</term> <note>original: "budhavāsare"</note>. If wealth is taken during a <term>Solar Ingress</term> <note>original: "saṅkrāntau"; when the sun moves into a new zodiac sign</note>, it leads to a continuous increase of debt in the family; thus it is known that one should not perform <term>debt clearance</term> at that time. 

Regarding the recovery of lost items according to the "Deer-Policy" and others: concerning <term>loss of wealth</term> and its recovery, the previously mentioned <term>planets</term> and "blind" stars, as well as <term>Vayu</term> <note>the Wind</note> and <term>Svati</term> <note>the star Arcturus</note>—in these configurations, wealth that is lost, used, or deposited, such as gold and other <term>assets</term>, does not return. Similarly, from the Rohini <term>lunar mansion</term> <note>original: "nakṣatra"</note>... <note>technical calculation involving the number 38</note> there is no <term>attainment of wealth</term>. When divided by four, if the remainder is three or six, that which is gone does not return. 

Now, regarding the <term>depositing of wealth</term>: it is said "give of yourself to the giver of wealth." This refers to money. In the twelfth and fourth houses, if they are free from <term>malefic planets</term> <note>original: "pāpagraha"</note> on a bright lunar day, one should give for the sake of prosperity. As it has been said: wealth is not recovered if given under the star <term>Svati</term>. <term>Narada</term> <note>a legendary sage and author of astrological treatises</note> says this applies to money given, employed, deposited, or lost. 

Next, the <term>auspicious time</term> <note>original: "muhūrta"</note> for the mastery of <term>Mantra-sciences</term> <note>original: "mantra-vidyā"</note> such as the <term>Narasimha</term> <note>the Man-Lion avatar of Vishnu</note> mantra, as well as the <term>auspicious time</term> for other observances: regarding <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "vidyām"</note>, the mastery of Mantra-science is as previously described. For the beginning of <term>Vedic studies</term> <note>original: "vedavidyā"</note> or the <term>completion of studies</term> <note>original: "vidyāmokṣa"</note>, it should be done when the day is joined with the appropriate <term>lunar day and star</term> <note>original: "tithibhe"</note> of one's own tutelary deity...

<margin>Om 89 69</margin> In that regard, one should not perform the commencement of studies <note>original: "mukhavagrahiya"</note>; this means that according to the traditional scriptures <note>original: "Agama"</note>, the fault of "non-study days" <term>Anadhyaya: specific days in the lunar calendar when sacred recitation is forbidden</term> does not apply here. The "Milky" stars <note>referring to stars associated with Vishnu</note>, the "Fixed" stars <note>Dhruva: Rohini, Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note> mentioned previously, the "Wind" stars <note>Marut: Swati</note> mentioned previously, Ashwini, Magha, the "Medium" stars, Hasta, Shravana, and Shatataraka—under these lunar mansions, one should plant trees for prosperity.

The authorities cite eleven specific stars: the three "Hari" stars <note>Shravana, Dhanishta, Shatabhisha</note>, Ashwini, Pushya, Chitra, and Punarvasu. In this group of stars, when the Moon is present, one should begin the construction of wells and waterworks. This should be done when Mercury <note>Budha</note>, Jupiter <note>Ijya</note>, or Venus <note>Bhrigu</note> are in a Watery Sign in the rising house, and on an auspicious day of the week.

"Chitra, Swati, Punarvasu, Mrigashira, Mula, Ashwini, Rohini, Hasta, Pushya, Dhanishta, Shatabhisha, Anuradha, the three 'Uttara' stars, and Revati—when the Moon is in these, and the rising sign is Capricorn or Aquarius, the construction of a step-well is successful." When Jupiter is in the rising sign or a friendly planet is in the house, and under the three Shravana stars, the Fixed stars, or the "Soft" stars, one should perform the first sighting of the water.

With the **benefic planets** <note>original: "saumya-grahe"; refers to gentle planets like Jupiter, Venus, or a waxing Moon</note> in two quadrants <note>the 4th, 7th, or 10th houses</note> from the Ascendant, the meaning is that auspicious planets are positioned in these centers. ### On Rites of Peace and Prosperity
Now, he speaks of the rites for **peace and nourishment** <note>Shanti and Paustika rituals intended to avert misfortune or increase wealth</note>. These are performed using the same lunar mansions <note>Nakshatras</note>—starting from Shravana and the following two—but excluding certain ones. In what manner? Along with the **moveable signs** <note>Chara signs</note> and during a **virtuous rise** <note>Sudharmodaye</note>. A "virtuous rise" means the ninth house is auspicious and free from malefic influence, and this occurs at the time of the Ascendant <note>Lagna</note>. The rest is as before.

### On Prohibited Times for Divine Ordeals
Now, in a half-verse, he discusses **divine ordeals** <note>Divya; judicial trials by fire, water, etc.</note>. One should not perform a divine ordeal during an **intercalary month** <note>Malimlucha; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note>, <column-break/> or when the **Moon, Venus, or Jupiter** are set <note>astronomically invisible/combust</note>. Specifically, when the Moon is in the dark fortnight, or when these planets are in the eighth house in transit. Furthermore, do not perform them on the days of **Saturn or Mars**, nor during the rise of a **malefic planet** or a **fixed sign** <note>Sthira-lagna</note>. If an ordeal is performed at these times, the result will be the opposite of the truth; for example, a liar might emerge victorious. The strength of the Ascendant for success should be determined as previously described.

### Exceptions to Planetary Weakness
Now, in another half-verse, he explains where the fault of Jupiter or Venus being "combust" <note>invisible due to proximity to the Sun</note> does not apply. At **Shrishaila** <note>A famous pilgrimage site in Andhra Pradesh</note>, specifically at the lingam named **Mallikarjuna**, the weakness of planets does not matter. Likewise, it does not apply during the **eclipses** of the Sun and Moon, the **solstices** <note>the transit into Cancer or Capricorn</note>, or the **equinoxes** <note>the transit into Aries or Libra</note>. It also does not apply to those of mixed or low castes, the **commencement of Vedic study** <note>Upakarma</note>, the ritual conclusion of study, the first entry of a bride and groom into a house, <column-break/> the **Godavari** river rituals, or the **daily rites** performed every year, such as the **Ishana-bali** <note>an offering to the deity Ishana</note> or the **Ahi-bali** <note>an offering to the serpents</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the determination of auspicious times (Kālanirṇaya), specifically addressing exceptions to the rules regarding planetary combustion (maudhya-doṣa).</meta>

<margin>Om <note>A sacred invocation used at the beginning of texts.</note></margin>

The offering to Bali, the sleep of Hari <note>original: "Hari-śayana"</note>, and the turning over of Vishnu: the sleep of Vishnu occurs on the twelfth lunar day of the bright half of the month of Shravana <note>July/August</note>, and the turning over occurs on the twelfth lunar day of the bright half of the month of Bhadrapada <note>August/September</note>. Daily ritual baths, regular travel, the renovation of old houses, the planting of *Damana* <note>Artemisia indica, used in rituals</note>, and the offering of sacred threads <note>original: "pavitrā-ropaṇa"</note> are also mentioned. Regarding the lunar vows and the specific months: the eighth lunar days of the months of Margashirsha, Magha, and Phalguna are considered "reproachable eighths" <note>original: "kutsāṣṭamya"</note>. Festive occasions such as the birth of a son and the first wearing of new garments <note>original: "aṃśuka-dhāraṇaṃ"</note>—in these matters, the fault of the combustion <note>original: "mūḍhatva-doṣa"</note> of Jupiter and Venus is not declared; it is not mentioned by the sages, this is the remaining sense. In these five types of actions, the fault of combustion does not exist. Not only in these, but in all upcoming sacraments from conception <note>original: "garbhādhāna"</note> to the first feeding of solid food <note>original: "annaprāśana"</note>, the fault of combustion is not declared. Although the procedure for conception and first feeding is mentioned in this verse, it is stated here even if out of the primary context.

And so says the sage Garga: "In daily baths, in an old house, in the wearing of clothes, in eating, in the entry of a bride, and in auspicious ceremonies, there is no consideration of the combustion of Jupiter and Venus."

The sage Bhrigu states: "The commencement of Vedic study <note>original: "upākarma"</note>, the offering of sacred threads and *Damana*, the descent of the serpents in winter, the Bali offering, the *Ashtaka* rites, the offering to Ishana <note>Shiva</note>, and the sleeping and turning of Vishnu—one should perform these even during the combustion of Venus or Jupiter; this is the settled rule."

In the text *Dharmapradīpa* <note>The Lamp of Dharma</note>: "At the Godavari river, at Gaya, at Shrishaila, and during eclipses of the two luminaries <note>Sun and Moon</note>, at the solstices and equinoxes, in the performance of vows, in all festivals, in the parting of the hair ceremony <note>original: "sīmanta"</note>, and in funeral rites, and for all those who have reached puberty—the fault of combustion does not exist."

In the *Kālanirṇaya* <note>Determination of Time</note>: "When Venus or Jupiter are set <note>combust</note>, or when Jupiter is in the sign of Leo..."

# On the Timing of Rites and Prohibitions

The half-portion should always be observed according to the custom of the <term>Pratyangula<note>A specific measurement or technical rule in ritual geometry or timing.</note></term>. In the **Matsya Purana**, it is stated that the <term>Maunji<note>The sacred thread ceremony (Upanayana) marking the beginning of Vedic study.</note></term> should be performed for the auspicious castes. The activities beginning with the sacred thread ceremony are best performed when **Jupiter** <note>original: "Surejya"</note> and **Venus** <note>original: "Shukra"</note> are in a favorable state.

### Prohibitions on Pilgrimage and Funeral Rites

Now, the prohibition regarding <term>Tirthayatra<note>Pilgrimage to holy sites or sacred waters.</note></term> and <term>Pretakarma<note>Funeral rites or ceremonies performed for the deceased.</note></term> is discussed. It is said that when Jupiter and Venus are in their "infant" or "aged" states <note>astrological periods of weakness near their setting/rising</note>, or during the birth month, the birth star, the month of Chaitra, or a <term>Malimlucha<note>An intercalary or "unclean" leap month in the lunar calendar.</note></term> (intercalary month), certain acts are forbidden.

Regarding the first visit to a pilgrimage site, one should not undertake it during these times, with the exception of **Kashi** (Varanasi), **Godavari**, and **Gaya**. At the Godavari and Gaya rivers, one may always perform rites. 

Regarding the prohibition of funeral rites: during the aforementioned periods when Jupiter and Venus are set, or during the month of Ashadha, one should not perform the rites for the deceased. However, the implication is that during the *first year* after a death, these rites **must** be performed even during the prohibited times. If the first year has passed, then even at Gaya or the Godavari, one should avoid these rites during the setting of Jupiter and Venus.

As it has been said:
> Whether in childhood or old age, or when Venus has set, or during an intercalary month—one should avoid visiting the deities.

**Vyasa** states:
> During an intercalary month, on one's birth star, or when Jupiter and Venus have disappeared (set), pilgrimage should not be undertaken, except to **Gaya** and the **Godavari**.

**Bhrigu** adds:
> Even during an intercalary month, one should avoid ritual bathing at a pilgrimage site. One remains pure regarding the secrets of the deceased and the deities only in the proper division of time.

In the section on Pilgrimage <note>original: "Tirtha-khanda"</note>, the fault arising from the setting of Jupiter and Venus is proclaimed for all pilgrimages. Regarding funeral rites, they are prohibited when Venus or Jupiter have set, **except during the first year** of mourning. 

**Mandavya** says:
> During an intercalary month or the birth month, the sacred thread ceremony... <note>text breaks off</note>

<summary>This manuscript page outlines astrological restrictions for performing sacred thread ceremonies, pilgrimages, and funeral rites, specifically highlighting exceptions for holy sites like Gaya and Kashi.</summary>
<keywords>Matsya Purana, pilgrimage, funeral rites, intercalary month, Jupiter, Venus, Gaya, Godavari, Vyasa, Mandavya</keywords>

# On Prohibited Times for Pilgrimage and Study

Pilgrimage <term>Tirthayatra; travel to sacred river crossings or sites</term> should not be undertaken when Jupiter or Venus are set <note>astronomically invisible due to proximity to the sun</note>, with the exception of Gaya and the Godavari River. During the combustion of Jupiter or Venus, in an intercalary month <term>Malimlucha; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</term>, or when the planets are in an unfavorable state, one should avoid rites for the deceased <term>Pretakarya</term>, except at Gaya and the Godavari. Specifically, the performance of ancestral rites, ritual bathing, and the chanting of prayers <term>Japa</term> are forbidden. This is stated in the author's own commentary on the *Muhurta Martanda* <note>The "Sun of Electional Astrology," a famous 16th-century Sanskrit text by Narayana Bhatta</note> in the "Mixed Chapter."

These are the "holes" or flaws of the Brahmins; through these flaws, sacred knowledge <term>Brahma</term> leaks away. It is shown that if a Brahmin performs these acts at the wrong time, his spiritual glory and strength are taken away. Therefore, one should avoid these times to ensure the desired fruit of the ritual.

Regarding the prohibition of study <term>Anadhyaya; days when the Vedas should not be recited</term>, the text explains the three types. The "Parva" days <note>specific points in the lunar cycle</note> are identified as the Full Moon and New Moon. Additionally, the fourteenth day and the eighth day of both the dark and light fortnights are included.

In the months beginning with Chaitra <note>the first month of the Hindu lunar calendar, occurring around March-April</note>, the wise have declared these days as unfit for study. Other prohibited times include:
The transitions of the sun <term>Sankranti</term>, specifically the entries into Aries, Cancer, Libra, and Capricorn <note>the equinoxes and solstices</note>. In the month of Chaitra, the third lunar day is noted. In the month of Jyeshtha, the second day of the light fortnight is forbidden. These thirteen specific dates, including the tenth of Ashadha and the twelfth of the month, are designated as times when study must be avoided to prevent spiritual fault.

<summary>This page outlines astrological restrictions for pilgrimages and Vedic study, citing the Muhurta Martanda. It emphasizes that rituals performed during the combustion of Jupiter or Venus or on specific lunar days (Anadhyaya) lose their spiritual efficacy.</summary>
<keywords>pilgrimage, Tirthayatra, Muhurta Martanda, Anadhyaya, astrology, lunar calendar, Gaya, Godavari River, ritual purity, Sankranti</keywords>

# On the Calculation of Lunar Days

In the month of **Ashadha** <note>June-July</note>, during the **bright fortnight** <note>Shukla Paksha; the waxing phase of the moon</note>, the second, tenth, twelfth, and thirteenth days are significant. Now, regarding the month of **Margashirsha** <note>November-December</note>, in the **dark fortnight** <note>Krishna Paksha; the waning phase of the moon</note>, the seventh and ninth days are noted.

The six limbs <note>original: "Shadanga"; likely referring to the Vedangas or components of the calendar</note> are like the three fires. In the month of **Ashwin** <note>September-October</note>, the second and ninth days are identified. In **Ashadha**, the tenth day is associated with the three fires. In the **bright fortnight** of **Ashwin**, the second, ninth, and third days are specified.

<margin>The "Ghosha" and "Arka" calculations</margin>

Furthermore, in the **dark fortnight**, the seventh and ninth days are observed. Finally, in the month of **Phalguna** <note>February-March</note>, during the **dark fortnight**, the ninth, seventh, and second days are brought together in a collection <note>original: "samahara"</note>. This means that in the dark fortnight, the seventh, ninth, and second days are the focus.

In the month of **Bhadrapada** <note>August-September</note>, the twelfth day, the third day of **Ashwin**, the second day, and the seventh day are all part of the **bright fortnight**. Regarding the **dark fortnight**, the seventh, second, and ninth days are established. In the month of **Kartika** <note>October-November</note>, the second, ninth, tenth, and eleventh days of the **bright fortnight** are dedicated to the Lord <note>original: "Ishvara"</note>. Following this, in the **dark fortnight**, the seventh, third, second, thirteenth, and twelfth days are noted.

In **Bhadrapada**, three specific days—the third of the bright fortnight and the seventh and tenth of the dark fortnight—along with the ninth and second, are significant. If the seventh, thirteenth, and ninth days coincide with the **Bharani** star <note>an auspicious but fierce lunar mansion</note>, it is associated with **Death** <note>original: "Mrityu"; likely referring to an inauspicious yoga or time-period</note>. The **Shravana** <note>a lunar mansion</note> of the bright fortnight is well-known; this refers specifically to the **Shravana** of the bright fortnight of **Bhadrapada**.

### Regarding the Decision on Study

Now, the decision regarding these days is as follows: no one should engage in study on a day designated as a **non-reading day** <term>Anadhyaya: days on which the recitation of the Vedas is prohibited</term>. Whatever the lunar date may be, if it is a **non-reading day**, one should bathe at that time. Therefore, on such a day, one should not recite the **Brahma Veda** <note>the Atharvaveda</note>...

<summary>This page details specific lunar dates (tithis) across the months of Ashadha, Margashirsha, Ashwin, Phalguna, Bhadrapada, and Kartika, concluding with rules regarding "Anadhyaya" or days when Vedic study is prohibited.</summary>
<keywords>Hindu calendar, Tithi, Shukla Paksha, Krishna Paksha, Anadhyaya, Vedic study, Ashadha, Bhadrapada, Shravana nakshatra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal and ritual discussion regarding Anadhyaya (prohibited times for Vedic study), specifically addressing exceptions for daily duties and the impact of specific animals or social encounters on study.</meta>

->Page 72<-

If the prohibition occurs for three <term>Kshanas</term><note>A unit of time; approximately 48 minutes</note> on another day, then on that day one should not recite the <term>Brahmayajna</term><note>The daily sacrificial offering of Vedic recitation</note>. However, during the eighth day of the lunar fortnight and other such times, the rule applies for two parts of the day. Regarding these subsequent prohibited times, one may still practice the <term>Vedangas</term><note>The six auxiliary disciplines used to understand the Vedas, such as grammar and astronomy</note>. In all these prohibited times, one should not recite the Rig or Yajur Veda, but one should still perform the daily obligatory rituals. As it is said: on the New Moon day, one should set aside the Vedic hymns, but one should preserve the <term>Gayatri</term><note>The most sacred Vedic mantra, considered the mother of the Vedas</note> or the ancestral mantras, the <term>Apratiratha</term> hymn, and other daily sciences. The daily rituals consist of the twilight prayers (<term>Sandhya</term>), the fire sacrifice (<term>Homa</term>), and the <term>Brahmayajna</term>.

And so says **Manu**<note>The legendary first king and traditional author of the Manusruti, a primary legal text</note>: "In the commencement of Vedic study, and likewise in the daily recitation, the fire-sacrifice mantras, and silent chanting, there is no prohibition." In the text *Smritiratnavali*, it is noted that there is no fault in daily silent chanting (<term>Japa</term>). Even during an eclipse, it is remembered that there is no prohibition for the mantras used in the worship of deities. One should chant during prohibited times for the sake of the body or in cases of poisoning. As the rule states: "One who is pure should perform the <term>Pavamanas</term><note>Purificatory hymns from the Rig Veda</note>."

In the *Smrityarthasara*: "On days other than the fourteenth, the eighth, the full/new moon, and the first day of the lunar fortnight, one should study the Vedangas, Logic (<term>Nyaya</term>), Hermeneutics (<term>Mimamsa</term>), and the Law Books (<term>Dharmashastras</term>)." In the *Kurma Purana*: "One should not perform the <term>Brahmayajna</term> in the village during the second part of the day; during prohibited times, one should chant only a very small amount."

Now, the **Occasional Prohibitions** <note>Anadhyayas triggered by specific events rather than the calendar</note>. Regarding these, it is said: if a cloth is torn by a mongoose, a dog, a cat, a deer, a water-spider... <note>The text lists animals whose presence or actions interrupt study</note> ...a goat, a ram, a female deer, a snake, or a donkey. Also, if one is in a vehicle such as a litter or palanquin. Likewise, if a horse, an elephant, or a mouse is present. Further prohibitions occur in the presence of an intoxicated person, a camel, or a chariot. Finally, prohibitions are triggered by the presence of those outside the four-fold caste system, such as a washerman, a tanner, or a wandering actor.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual purity and the specific conditions under which the study of sacred texts must be suspended (Anadhyāya). It focuses on physical obstructions, environmental omens, and social interactions that render a time or place unsuitable for Vedic recitation.</meta>

Fishermen and leather-workers are considered <term>Antyajas</term><note>Literally "last-born"; a historical term for social groups residing outside the traditional four-tier caste system.</note>. If one of these individuals passes between the teacher and the student, study must be suspended for three days and nights.

If the view of the stars or the horizon is obstructed by birds or by wood during the <term>Sandhyā</term><note>The junctions of the day, such as dawn and dusk, used for prayer.</note>, study is forbidden. One should not recite while a single person or a group is passing through. By the word "etcetera," this includes the passing of monitor lizards and similar creatures.

If the stars are obscured by these, study is forbidden. If a woman or a cow passes between [the teacher and student], study is suspended for the remainder of the day. <margin>This applies to women, cows, and herds of wild animals.</margin> If these pass through, study is suspended for a full day and night.

During festivals, great celebrations, or marriages, and at midnight, one should not study. One should not recite while on a path or near smoke. One should not study under the shade of a single Pipal tree, a Silk-cotton tree, a Madhuka tree, or a Shleshmantaka tree. By the phrase "and others," this includes the shade of the Bibhitaka or the Castor oil plant.

One should not study in the middle of a village if there is a disturbance. One should not study near a cloud. After eating the <term>Shrāddha</term><note>A funeral priest's meal or a feast offered in honor of ancestors.</note> meal, or while a digestion-related illness persists, one should not study. If a celestial body is moving in retrograde, one should not study.

From the time of an eclipse, one should not study. Likewise, for the duration of the day of the eclipse, one should not study. Following the <term>Upakarma</term><note>The annual ceremony for renewing the sacred thread and beginning the term of Vedic study.</note> and the <term>Utsarga</term><note>The ritual suspension of Vedic study for the season.</note>, one should not study.

In the event of an earthquake, study is suspended. <margin>Earthquake, lightning strike, and portents.</margin> If there is a lightning strike or a great portent, or if the village where one resides is on fire, one should not study. If one sees a <term>Gandharva-nagara</term><note>Literally "City of the Celestial Musicians"; a mirage or an unusual cloud formation in the sky.</note> or ghosts, one should not study. In the event of unseasonable thunder or a whirlwind, one should not study. If there is thunder during the twilight hours, study is forbidden.

One should not recite during the day if there is thunder, until the sun sets; if it thunders at night, one should not recite until sunrise. These are the final <term>Anadhyayas</term> <note>Periods when the study of the Vedas is prohibited</note> according to the original texts. Now, regarding the prohibitions for the <term>Pradosha</term> <note>The evening period, specifically the first part of the night</note>: if the thirteenth day of the lunar fortnight <note>Madanatrayodashi</note> or the seventh day <note>Munisaptami</note> falls during the middle of the night, or if it occurs during the first watch or within a period of three and a half hours, one must avoid recitation during the preceding periods. Following the <term>Upakarma</term> <note>The annual ritual of renewing the sacred thread and beginning Vedic study</note>, for one full month, one must avoid reciting at night. Recitation is always forbidden during the two twilights <note>dawn and dusk</note>. In the event of a disturbance by thieves, recitation is forbidden for one <term>Yama</term> <note>A watch of three hours</note> after the danger is perceived. If one has accepted a gift of a camel or a mule, one must avoid recitation for three days.

As stated in the verses of the *Shridhari* <note>A legal/liturgical commentary</note> and other traditional law books: On the eighth, fourteenth, and fifteenth days of the lunar fortnight, as well as on the first day <note>Pratipada</note>, there is a prohibition. Likewise, during times of great fear, during festivals, during illness, during a wedding, or while traveling on a road, one should not recite. One should not recite at midnight, nor when one is impure or has just eaten. During the <term>Upakarma</term> and the <term>Utsarga</term> <note>The ritual concluding the term of Vedic study</note>, and during eclipses of the moon or sun, a three-night period of non-study is ordained. Similarly, on the full moon days of the months of Phalguna, Chaitra, Shravana, Kartika, and Margashirsha, one should not engage in study. Furthermore, if a dog, a snake, a cat, a mongoose, or an elephant passes between the teacher and the student, or if one hears the sound of a donkey or a wolf, or if one is in the presence of those who are intoxicated or insane, the study must be interrupted.

# On the Prohibitions of Study

Even in the company of women, or if a <term>vajrapāta</term><note>original: "vajrapāta"; a lightning strike or thunderbolt</note> occurs, there shall be no study for a full day and night. If thunder occurs during the night, or during the late watch of the night, study is suspended for that day. In the event of a lightning strike, or the death of a person, or the passing of a king, the prohibition lasts for the entire day. On the seventh day, the prohibition lasts for six nights including the night of the event.

Study is also suspended upon the arrival of a <term>śrotriya</term><note>original: "śrotriya"; a learned Brahmin well-versed in the Vedas</note>; this immediate suspension lasts until they have departed. Regarding the <term>śrāddha</term><note>original: "śrāddha"; a ritual for deceased ancestors</note>, a priest who has eaten at the ceremony should not engage in study until the other priests have been formally dismissed. These are the prohibitions declared for the Vedic texts; now I shall speak of those pertaining to the Tantric traditions.

The eighth day (<term>aṣṭamī</term>) and the two fourteenth days (<term>caturdaśī</term>) of the lunar fortnight, as well as the two equinoxes and the two solstices, are times when one should not read. This applies to the <term>purāṇa</term><note>original: "purāṇa"; ancient historical and mythological texts</note> as well. On the eighth and fourteenth days, as long as the lunar day lasts, the Vedic prohibition is proclaimed.

On the <term>pratipada</term><note>original: "pratipada"; the first day of a lunar fortnight</note> and during a <term>saṃkrānti</term><note>original: "saṃkrānti"; the sun's transition into a new zodiac sign</note>, study is forbidden for the entire duration. During the bright fortnights of the months of Nabhas and Nabhasya <note>August and September</note>, there is a three-day suspension. A twice-born man should not study on the seventh or the thirteenth days.

The wise man should avoid the eighth, the fourteenth, and the first day of the fortnight when studying the <term>dharmaśāstra</term><note>original: "dharmaśāstra"; treatises on law and social duty</note>, the Purāṇas, or one's own specific scriptures. One must carefully observe these rules starting from the seventh day. However, in the study of the Purāṇas and similar texts, there is no fault in reading on the fifteenth day if it involves new recitations. The wise man should also observe the suspension of study during unseasonable thunder, on mountains, or during festivals.

<keywords>anadhyāya, ritual study, lunar calendar, lightning omens, śrāddha, Purāṇas, Dharmaśāstra, Brahmin duties</keywords>

The wise should perform ablutions during eclipses and similar events; however, one should never engage in study during these sacred transitions <note>original: "parva," referring to the new and full moon phases</note>. In another legal treatise <note>original: "Smrityantara"</note>, it is stated that one should not study during the *Pradosha* <note>the period of twilight or early evening</note>, nor on the thirteenth day of the lunar fortnight, nor on the first day of a fortnight. Regarding the definition of *Pradosha*: if the sixth or twelfth lunar days extend into midnight by even one *nadika* <note>a unit of time approximately 24 minutes</note>, one must not study during that *Pradosha*. If it occurs in the third part of the day, it lasts for nine *nadikas* after midnight; this is the meaning.

Now, regarding the occasional *Pradosha* mentioned in the *Ayatastah*: following the *Upakarma* <note>the annual ceremony for renewing the sacred thread and beginning Vedic study</note> on the full moon of the month of Shravana, one should not study during the *Pradosha* for an entire month. Thus says the sage Gargya: after the commencement of studies in Shravana, one should never study during the early night for the duration of that month. Narada also states: on the equinoxes, and when the Lord <note>Vishnu</note> goes to sleep or awakens, as well as on the anniversaries of the *Manvantaras* <note>ages of Manu</note> and the *Yugas* <note>cosmic eras</note>, one should observe a break from study.

The *Manvantara* dates are prescribed in the *Matsya Purana* as follows: the ninth day of the bright fortnight of Ashwayuja; the twelfth day of the bright fortnight of Karttika; the third day of Chaitra; the third day of Bhadrapada; the seventh day of the bright fortnight of Phalguna; the eleventh day of the bright fortnight of Pausha; the tenth day of Ashadha; the seventh day of Magha; the eighth day of the dark fortnight of Shravana; and the full moon days of Ashadha, Karttika, Phalguna, Chaitra, and the fifteenth day of the bright fortnight of Jyeshtha. These are the beginnings of the *Manvantaras*, and gifts given on these days are inexhaustible.

The beginnings of the *Yugas* are shown in the *Vishnu Purana*: the third day of the month of Vaishakha; the ninth day of the bright fortnight of Karttika; the thirteenth day of the dark fortnight of Nabhasya <note>Bhadrapada</note>; and the fifteenth day of Magha. For the *Upakarma* and the *Utsarga* <note>the ritual conclusion of the term of study</note>, a break of three nights is remembered. On the *Ashtaka* days <note>eighth days of the dark fortnights in winter</note>, the break is for a full day and night, and for the *Anvashtakas*, it is for three nights. This applies to the months of Margashirsha, Pausha, and Magha. These three 74

# On the Prohibited Days for Study

In the **Smritiratnavali** <note>A prominent medieval compendium of religious law</note>, the learned scholars have declared fourteen specific instances. Before the sun has risen even a quarter of its way, the one who knows the Law should observe a prohibition on study on the thirteenth day of the lunar fortnight. 

**Manu** <note>The legendary first lawgiver</note> has stated that when there is thunder in the sky, rain, or the falling of great meteors, study must be suspended for that duration. "Thunder" here refers specifically to the rumbling of clouds. From the middle of the constellation **Anuradha** <note>A specific lunar mansion in the night sky</note>, for as long as the Sun travels through it, the Sages recognize a period of suspension. 

If these signs are seen at the expected time, the suspension lasts for that moment; if they occur out of season, it lasts for three nights. When there is excessive rain, one should not study for three days. Regarding unseasonal rain that results in standing water on the ground, **Katyayana** <note>An ancient sage and grammarian</note> has said the suspension lasts for one night. 

### Environmental Signs
In the signs of **Aries** and **Taurus** <note>original: "Mesha" and "Vrishabha"</note>, if water merely drips from the tips of grass, one should observe the suspension. In the signs of **Taurus** and **Aquarius**, if the ground becomes saturated, study is forbidden. It is traditionally taught that after the water has dripped from the grass tips, the suspension remains for three quarters of a day. "Dripping from grass" refers to the flow of water from vegetation.

Furthermore, one should know that study is suspended at all times during:
*   Disturbances by thieves in the village
*   Battles or armed conflicts
*   Outbreaks of fire

If lightning, thunder, and rain occur together while the stars are still visible, study is prohibited for the remainder of the night. Similarly, in the event of a "downfall" <note>original: "nirghata" - likely a whirlwind or thunderclap from a cloudless sky</note>, an earthquake, or an eclipse of the heavenly bodies, these are considered physical portents requiring a halt to study. 

<margin>Sun</margin>

A "downfall" is defined as a specific, violent sound occurring in the atmosphere. The "eclipse of the heavenly bodies" refers to the halos surrounding the Moon and the <insert>Sun</insert>. 

One should not recite the Veda after receiving an invitation to a funeral rite for a single ancestor. One should not chant the sacred texts in a place of birth-impurity or death-impurity. This is also recorded in the **Puranas**: the same rule applies to the death of a fellow student <note>original: "sabrahmacharini"</note> or upon the passing of one's Teacher.

<summary>This page details the 'Anadhyaya' or periods when Vedic study is forbidden due to celestial omens (meteors, eclipses), weather (unseasonal rain, thunder), or social disturbances (war, theft). It emphasizes the spiritual connection between the natural environment and the sanctity of oral transmission.</summary>

<keywords>Anadhyaya, Vedic study, Manu, Katyayana, astrology, lunar mansions, ritual purity, Smritiratnavali, omens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on ritual purity and timing, specifically focusing on periods when Vedic study is prohibited and the omens associated with animals.</meta>

# On the Prohibition of Vedic Study

A fast of three nights is prescribed. **Yajnavalkya** <note>A famous ancient sage and legal authority</note> states: there shall be no study <term>Anadhyaya: days when the recitation of the Vedas is forbidden</term> when a blow has been struck, or upon the death of a disciple, a sacrificial priest, or a teacher. This also applies during the ceremonies of beginning and ending the term of Vedic study <term>Upakarma and Utsarga</term>, and upon the death of a learned scholar <term>Shrotriya</term> of one's own Vedic school.

Furthermore, study is forbidden during twilight, thunder, earthquakes, the falling of meteors, or upon completing the study of a whole Veda. If one hears the sound of a kill at night, or after studying, if a cow, frog, mongoose, snake, dog, cat, or rat passes between the teacher and the pupil, one must fast for a day and a night.

**Parashara** <note>Another influential legal legislator</note> says: regarding the snake, dog, mongoose, as well as the mongoose and cat, the rat, the camel, the frog, and women; if any of these—including a dog or a person of low caste—pass between [the teacher and pupil], or if their women pass through, one must observe a three-night fast and a three-night ritual bathing.

<margin>
75
</margin>

Immediate prohibitions of study occur during the braying of a donkey or camel, the hooting of an owl, the sound of the *Sama* chants <note>original: "Samavata"</note>, or the sound of a flute. Likewise, study is forbidden in the presence of impurity, the sound of weeping, or when dogs are nearby. Other occasions include:
* During a whirlwind or a flood.
* While one's hands are still wet from eating.
* In the middle of the night or during high winds.
* During a rain of dust or when the quarters of the sky appear to be "on fire" <note>likely referring to extreme atmospheric redness</note>.
* During twilight, mist, or while running.
* When a guest has arrived at the house.
* While mounted on a donkey, camel, elephant, horse, or boat.

These are known as the immediate causes for the cessation of study.

### Exceptions to the Prohibition
**Manu** <note>The primary lawgiver of ancient India</note> states: In the performance of the opening ceremony of the Vedas, in the daily recitation of the scriptures, and in the chanting of mantras for the fire sacrifice <term>Homa</term>, there are no restrictions regarding the prohibited days of study.

->Thus ends the section on the prohibition of study in the Commentary on the *Muhurtamartanda* <note>The "Sun of Electional Astrology"</note>.<-

### On the Results of a Lizard Falling on a Woman
Now, regarding the results of a lizard falling on a woman: If it falls on the hair, it brings distress; if on the back, it signifies death; if it falls on the feet, it brings wandering. If it falls on the private parts or the chest, it is generally auspicious. If it falls on the cheek, it is excellent. If it falls on the head, it is the best of all results.

# On the Omens of Lizards and Chameleons

Except for specific spots, it is generally auspicious; this means that in other places, it is favorable. On other limbs, it may be good, but on the sides and the back, it is not auspicious. Now, the specific rules for women are stated:

### The Rule of Reversal for Women
Regarding the results mentioned for men, one should understand them for women by reversing left and right. <note>In traditional Indian divination, the right side is often considered auspicious for men and the left for women.</note> Because of this reversal between the left and right limbs, what was stated as the result for a man’s left limb should be understood as the result for a woman’s right limb. What was stated for the right should be understood for the left. In places where there is no distinction between left and right, such as the navel, the result is the same as for a man. Regarding the private organs <term>Varanga: a respectful term for the genitals</term>, the results are equal for both, as there is no distinction between left and right results there.

### On the Climbing of Chameleons
Regarding the results of a chameleon <term>Sharata: a garden lizard or chameleon</term> climbing upon a person, the sages have said: the result that occurs when a chameleon climbs up is reversed if it falls off. Some teachers say the result is nullified <insert>fruitless</insert> in certain cases. Just as there is a result for a house lizard <term>Palli: the common wall gecko</term> falling, they say the climbing is the opposite. Some say that the result only applies to the lizard falling, and the climbing is of no consequence.

### Ritual Purification
Now the procedure for remediation is stated: One should perform a bath including the clothes one is wearing <term>Sachela-snana: a ritual purification bath taken without undressing to wash away bad luck</term> to bring about peace <note>Shanti: a propitiatory rite to avert evil</note>. After bathing with one's clothes on, one should perform the peaceful rituals prescribed in the scriptures.

### Specific Results from the Jyotir-Nibandha
As stated in the *Jyotir-Nibandha* <note>A famous medieval compendium on Hindu astrology and omens</note> and by the sage Varaha <note>Referring to Varahamihira, the 6th-century polymath</note> regarding the results of the house lizard:
*   **On the Head (the Brahma-spot):** One obtains a kingdom or high status.
*   **On the Face:** Obtaining sweet foods.
*   **On the Forehead:** Acquisition of wealth or jewelry.
*   **On the Eyes:** Seeing a loved one.
*   **On the Nose:** Acquisition of perfumes/fragrances.
*   **On the Mouth:** Eating sweet delicacies.
*   **On the Throat:** Something excellent happens.
*   **On the Jaw:** Acquisition of property.
*   **On the Hump of the Back:** A dispute or struggle.
*   **On the Arms:** Arrival of kinsmen.

Thus, it gives results to all. On the left [arm], it brings disease. On the [right] shoulder, victory is constant. On the left shoulder, the birth of a son. Profit is gained on the right arm, the elbow, and the wrist. On the left palm... <unclear>leha</unclear>

On the hollow of the throat, it signifies the loss of wealth; on the heart, royal honor; on the right breast, good fortune; and on the right side of the chest, the enjoyment of religious merit. Now, although the results of the lizard moving have been stated generally for both sides, specifically: movement on the right foot indicates profit, while movement on the left foot indicates loss. On the back, it brings pleasant news; on the left side of the back, loss; and on the right, wealth. If it wanders all around the body, it causes agitation. The results stated for men should be understood as the opposite for women. Wise men should consider the results of the lizard's movement and its falling as having the same effects. Whether the lizard climbs up or falls down, the result is produced through that contact. If this occurs during an inauspicious astrological timing—such as a "Death Yoga," on one's birth star, during the *Vishti* or *Vaidhriti* periods, when the Moon is in the eighth house, or under a cruel ascendant—the effects are intensified. If it climbs up the right side of the body and descends via the left, it causes trembling and loss. If it climbs halfway up the legs and then reaches the head, it signifies the attainment of a kingdom.
