If a lizard falls upon one's wife, it is a cause for particular concern and great anxiety. If it falls upon the vessels used for eating, or upon the toes of the feet, it indicates a loss of wealth.
<note>The text suggests that the specific location of the animal's contact determines the severity of the omen.</note>
However, if a lizard <term>sarata (lizard/chameleon)</term> falls on the right side of the body, it is auspicious and leads to the growth of affection and love. If it falls on the left side, it indicates separation or misfortune.

If an iguana <term>godha (monitor lizard/iguana)</term> touches any part of the body, it is generally considered auspicious and brings good fortune. 
<margin>Contact with an iguana is a positive sign.</margin>

If a chameleon <term>krikalasa (chameleon)</term> falls upon any limb, to pacify the ill effects of that direction, a learned man should chant the *Mrita-Sanjivani* Vidyā <note>original: "Mṛta-Sañjīvanī"; a life-restoring Vedic hymn or knowledge</note>. If a wife is touched by a lizard, one must perform a ritual bath while still wearing one's clothes. After drinking the five products of the cow <term>panchagavya (a purifying mixture of milk, curd, ghee, urine, and dung)</term>, one should gaze at their reflection in clarified butter (ghee).

If the omen is particularly bad, or if there is a discharge of blood, one should perform the peace-making ritual <term>shantikara (propitiatory rite)</term> to achieve merit. According to the prescribed rules, one should create an image of the creature out of gold.
<note>The use of gold images is a common "substitution" ritual to deflect bad luck.</note>

Wrap the image in a new cloth and worship it with fragrant flowers. Place a beautiful earthen pot filled with water upon a bed of grain. Decorate the pot with cloth and garlands. Depending on one's financial capacity, place the five nectars <note>milk, honey, sugar, yogurt, ghee</note>, the five products of the cow, five gems, and the decoctions of five barks into the water, and then invoke the deities.

One should worship the Guardians of the Directions <term>lokapala (world-protectors)</term> in sequence with flowers and other offerings. Having established the sacred fire, the ritual of the fire-sacrifice <term>homa</term> should be performed. Using the *Victory Over Death* mantra <note>original: "Mrityunjaya Mantra"</note>, one should offer wood from the Khadira tree, sesame seeds, and the sacred utterances <note>the "Vyahritis": Bhur, Bhuvah, Svah</note>. This should be done 1,008 times, or at least 108 times, according to the regulations.

Then, the priests should perform the ritual sprinkling <term>abhisheka</term> upon the patron of the sacrifice. By reciting the hymns to the god Varuna <note>original: "Varuna Sukta"</note>, the great faults are pacified. After putting on washed garments, one should give gifts of sesame seeds and other items. Finally, one should feed the Brahmins. Whoever performs this peace-making ritual will find their life-span and strength increased.

From the second house: fear, loss, wealth, enmity, sorrow, gain, fear, sin, profit, happiness, and loss.

### On the Determination of Reverse Obstruction (Vamavedha)

Now, the author explains the determination of *Vamavedha* <term>Vamavedha: a specific type of planetary 'piercing' or obstruction that neutralizes a planet's effect</term>. In the region between the Himalaya and Vindhya mountains, one should calculate the intense movement of the planets. In other regions and countries, it should be calculated from the birth sign of the person. 

As stated by the sage Kanva: "The rule applies between the Himalayas and the Vindhyas." So too says the authority Vaidyanatha. Regarding the birth signs: "Whichever planet is joined with a 'piercing' position (Vedha) relative to the Moon's birth sign, that has been described by Shaunaka and other ancient sages." This mention of the Moon is illustrative of all factors.

Now, the *Vamavedha* of all planets is described in two verses, beginning with "In the sixth and the last..." 
The sixth house is well-known; the "last" refers to the twelfth house. Between these two, a mutual obstruction (*Vedha*) occurs. Similarly, between the fourth house <note>original: 'jala' or water</note>, the third house, the eleventh house, and the ninth house <note>original: 'anka' or the number nine</note>. This is the obstruction pattern for the Sun <term>Ravi: the Sun</term>. 

Here, the explanation of the numerical layout is provided by those who perceive the truth of the seven [planets]. Just as those obstructions are known, so too:
The 6th and 12th; the 9th and 4th; the 5th and 11th. In these positions, the obstruction of the Sun occurs. In the 9th house, the obstruction of the benefics <note>auspicious planets</note> is considered. For those planets other than the Sun—the non-cruel or auspicious planets—this is how the mutual obstruction is understood: in the 8th, 1st, 5th, and 2nd houses.

In the first verse, in the first, third, sixth, seventh, tenth, and eleventh positions, the results are auspicious; in the others, they are inauspicious. <note>This refers to the 'Prastara' or spreading of planetary influences.</note> The number of auspicious positions is 5. ### The Chapter on Solar Transits

Now, the chapter on the Sun's entry into a new zodiac sign <term>Sankranti: the moment the Sun moves from one zodiac sign to another</term> is explained. Because the merit of the time depends on the specific sign, the author says: Regarding the upward-moving Capricorn <note>Makara</note> and the dual-natured signs—which are Gemini, Virgo, Sagittarius, and Pisces—at the time of the Sun's transition into these, the thirty <note>original: "kharaama," a numerical code where 'kha' (space) is 0 and 'raama' is 3, totaling 30</note> ghatikas <term>Ghatika: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> following the transit are considered holy.

In the fixed signs—Taurus, Leo, Scorpio, and Aquarius—and in the well-known sign of Cancer, the thirty ghatikas *prior* to the Sun's transit are holy. In the cases of Aries and Libra <note>the Equinoxes</note>, the thirty ghatikas both before and after the transit are holy. Thus, fifteen <insert>15</insert> before and fifteen after make thirty ghatikas in total. 

Furthermore, he describes the specific holy durations for all transits: In Capricorn, those thirty ghatikas are doubled and increased by ten, meaning seventy <insert>70</insert> ghatikas are holy. As stated in the *Jyotir-nibandha* <note>A classical compendium of light/astrology</note>: "The exact moment of transit is subtle and perceived only by the wise, not by others. Because of its occurrence, thirty ghatikas above and below are celebrated. Specifically, sixteen ghatikas before and sixteen after the Sun's transit are the holy time for ritual bathing, charity, and other works." In the fixed signs, the merit is great; in Cancer, it marks the beginning of the Southern Path <note>original: "Dakshinayana," the summer solstice</note>...

The northern transit of the Sun <note>original: "saumyayanam"</note> is divine; six signs are placed before it. In the month of Chaitra, the equinox does not fall in the middle; it is auspicious and one should not worship any other. Before the Sun enters the constellation of Ardra, that period is meritorious; if it occurs at night before Ardra, the merit follows on the next day. This applies to the solar transition. However, leaving aside the sign of Sagittarius, when the Sun enters Capricorn <note>original: "mṛga-saṃkramaṇe"</note> at dusk or at midnight, the enjoyment of the merit occurs on the following day. A human who does not bathe during the merit-period of the Sun's transition will be sickly for seven incarnations, a partaker of sorrow, and poor. 

Now, the complete results of the Sun's transition in the latter half are told: Cancer (7), Aries (the Goat), the last sign Pisces (the Fish), Taurus, <unclear>unclear</unclear>, and Capricorn. If the Sun moves through these during the night, it is not auspicious. If the Sun moves through the final sign during the day, the opposite is true—it is not good. If the Sun enters a sign on the third lunar day <note>original: "tṛtīya caro"</note> in the astrological periods <note>term: "karaṇa" (half of a lunar day)</note> of Kaulava or Kimstughna, or on the eighth day in Shakuni, that transition of the zodiac sign brings sorrow. 

As it is said: "When the transitions of Capricorn, Cancer, Aries, and Pisces occur at night, and the remaining signs occur during the day, the reverse becomes inauspicious." When a transition occurs while the Sun is present and Saturn is active, in that month fear arrives, along with famine, drought, thieves, and oppression by kings. If the Sun's transition occurs in the periods of Shakuni, Kaulava, or the Naga, or while the Sun is "sleeping" or "sitting," there will be great disease and famine. In a good transition, there is welfare. Such are the results of the timing of the transition. 

Now, in the context of the transition, the characteristics of the Intercalary Month <note>term: "adhimāsa" (an extra month added to the lunar calendar to align with the solar year)</note> and the Lost Month <note>term: "kṣayamāsa" (a month omitted when two solar transitions occur in one lunar month)</note> are given, regarding what should and should not be done. In the case of birth or death during an intercalary month, for the knowledge of the month: that month in which no solar transition <note>original: "saṃkramaṇa"</note> occurs is an intercalary month. A month with two transitions is a lost month. 79

<summary>This page details the astrological rules for the Sun's entry into various zodiac signs (Sankranti), warning of the spiritual and physical poverty that follows neglecting ritual baths. It also defines the astronomical conditions for Intercalary and Lost months used in the Hindu calendar.</summary>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of calendrical regulations, specifically focusing on the prohibitions and permissions during intercalary months.</meta>

A month in which two solar transitions <note>original: "saṅkrānti"; the entry of the sun into a new zodiac sign</note> occur, or a month lacking a solar transition entirely, is known as an intercalary month <note>original: "adhikamāsa"; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year</note>. These two types—the decreased month and the added month—are considered inauspicious for auspicious ceremonies. This means both months are forbidden for performing marriages or other festive rituals.

Regarding specific duties, it is stated: In the case of daily obligatory rites <note>original: "nitya-karma"</note> and annual ancestral rites <note>original: "śrāddha"</note>, one should not abandon them. For one who has died, during the first year of mourning, the "new ancestral offerings" <note>original: "nava-śrāddha"</note> and the "rite of sapindikarana" <note>original: "sapiṇḍīkaraṇa"; the ritual reception of the deceased into the community of ancestors</note> should be performed even in such a year. 

However, for other funeral rites delayed beyond the first year, one should avoid the intercalary year. In the section regarding "conception and other rites" <note>original: "garbhādhāna"</note>, it is mentioned that there is no prohibition for essential life-cycle rituals or funeral rites that have already begun. As the sage Garga says: "The installation of deities, the giving of gifts, vows, Vedic observances, the setting free of a bull <note>original: "vṛṣotsarga"</note>, the tonsure ceremony <note>original: "cūḍākaraṇa"</note>, the investiture of the sacred thread <note>original: "mekhalā"</note>, pilgrimages to divine shrines, marriages, and royal consecrations—these actions should be avoided in an impure month <note>original: "malamāsa"</note>."

Furthermore, for one who has died in an impure month, the ancestral offerings at holy sites and the rites for ancestors at Gaya should be performed. If an extra month occurs during the year of death, the monthly funeral offerings should be completed. One should not start any "desire-based" rituals <note>original: "kāmya-karma"</note>, the setting free of a bull, the thread ceremony, or the beginning of Vedic studies. If these are performed during an added month, the merit is lost and the effort is in vain. Therefore, one must avoid the intercalary month for such activities.

<summary>This text outlines the religious laws regarding the "intercalary" or "impure" month, detailing which life-cycle rituals and ancestral rites are forbidden and which must still be performed. It emphasizes that while festive events like weddings are prohibited, essential funeral duties and daily obligations should continue.</summary>
<keywords>intercalary month, adhikamasa, funeral rites, shraddha, Hindu calendar, Garga, religious prohibitions, samskaras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ritual timing and calendrical calculations, specifically addressing the complexities of intercalary and "lost" months in the Hindu lunar calendar. It transitions from legalistic ritual commentary to a genealogical or introductory praise section (Prashasti).</meta>

<margin>Section 8, Page 40</margin>

Some authorities, regarding the deviation of rituals, cite the *Parashara Smriti* <note>An ancient legal text attributed to the sage Parashara</note> concerning the "Mirror of the Household." In the context of the *Sapindikarana* <note>A crucial transition ritual performed after a death to unite the deceased with their ancestors</note> for those who have died or are usurers, one must perform the monthly rites individually. In the *Sapindikarana*, one should never omit the monthly rites; this is according to the *Smritiratnavali*. Regarding the occurrence of an intercalary month <note>Malamasa: an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar cycle, usually considered inauspicious for certain rites</note>, if a desired rite is begun before the intercalary month, it must be completed. If a death anniversary falls during an intercalary month, the ancestral rites must undoubtedly be completed.

Now, regarding the knowledge of the month for births and other events during a *Kshayamasa* <note>A "lost" or "decayed" month where two lunar months begin within a single solar month</note>: it is said that when birth or death occurs during such a defined "lost" month, the dates are divided between the prior and subsequent months. All the great sages agree that if the event occurs in the first half of the lunar day (*tithi*), it belongs to the preceding month; if in the latter half, it belongs to the succeeding month.

<margin>Aphorism</margin>

As stated in the *Kalanirnaya* <note>A treatise on the determination of auspicious time</note>: "The wise should know that the first half of the lunar day belongs to the former month and the second half to the latter month within the span of a 'lost' month."

Thus ends the section on *Sankriti* <note>Likely referring to the systematic arrangement of rites</note> in the *Shri Sarvabhanda Tika* <note>The "Commentary on the Universal Treasury"</note>.

Now begins the description of the lineage: Born of the illustrious *Kaushika* clan <note>A lineage tracing descent from the sage Vishvamitra</note>—those who are glorious and who purify their lineage. Among them was one whose soul was offered to the two feet of Lord Hari <note>Vishnu</note>. His heart was dedicated to the lotus feet of the Divine. "Soul" here refers to the body, the breath, and the inner self. From him was born Ananta; of what nature was he? He possessed virtues as appropriate as jewels. He was a protector of the gods and the Brahmins, endowed with fitting qualities. He was a master of his senses, a treasury of Vedic knowledge, and a practitioner of the laws (*Smriti*). He was characterized by the wealth of his virtues, such as generosity. From him was born Narayana. Whatever moment he acts within becomes auspicious for him...

<margin>8</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the technical rules for performing ancestral rites during leap months and "lost" months before transitioning into a poetic genealogy of a scholarly family, specifically praising a figure named Ananta and his successor Narayana.</summary>
<keywords>Parashara Smriti, Sapindikarana, Malamasa, Kshayamasa, lunar calendar, Kaushika gotra, Ananta, Narayana, ritual timing, Hindu law</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page concludes the commentary on the Muhurta Martanda, focusing on the colophon, the author's location, and the benefits of studying the work.</meta>

# The Sun of Auspicious Moments

The author of the *Martanda* <note>original: "Martanda," meaning "The Sun," referring to the title of the treatise</note> composed this work here, to the north of Devagiri <note>Modern Daulatabad in Maharashtra</note>. In that northern direction lies the famous and celebrated Temple of Shiva, known as the place of the *Jyotirlinga* <note>One of the twelve special radiant phallic symbols of Shiva; likely referring to Grishneshwar</note>. To the north of that Temple of Shiva is the village of Tapara; it was in this village of Tapara that he completed the work.

Now the author joyfully states the number of verses and the "Fruit of Hearing" <term>Phala Shruti: the traditional closing statement of a text describing the spiritual or material rewards for the reader</term> in this city. This collection was composed of one hundred and fifty verses (150). 

The man who recites this mantra-like text titled *Martanda* becomes worthy of worship by the learned <note>original: "Viprapujyo," honored by Brahmins or scholars</note>. Not only does he become honored by the learned, but he also attains happiness and wealth, such as fine clothing and offspring. It is appropriate that by giving the correct auspicious time <term>Muhurta: a specific favorable moment for starting an activity</term>, he becomes honored by the learned for the success of the work performed. Just as people achieve success in their tasks by choosing the right moment, they likewise obtain the happiness generated by those benefits, such as clothing and wine <note>original: "asura," though likely a scribal error for "sura" or "sukha" (happiness)</note>.

Furthermore, one who was of "diminished intellect" <note>original: "vikaladhi," meaning confused or lacking understanding</note> becomes "undiminished in intellect" <note>original: "avikaladhi"</note> through this work. Here, in the *Muhurta Martanda*, the rites from conception onwards are explained. Because it does not require the consultation of many other vast collections of scriptures <note>original: "Samhita"</note>, it is said to grant an undiminished intellect. Therefore, one attains the fulfillment of goals. This means that by studying a small book, one achieves the fulfillment of many great purposes. When the Guru of the Holy Place <note>Tirthaguru</note> grants his blessing, or when there is a "Holy Union" <note>Tirthayoga</note>, one attains the signs of happiness and success.

Finally, the author offers a blessing for the manuscript and states the date: In the year measured by the "Suns" (12) and the "Vedas" (4) <note>This is a chronogram, though the text explicitly clarifies the date below</note>, specifically in the year **1893** <note>This corresponds to approximately 1836-1837 CE in the Vikram Samvat calendar</note>, during the month of Tapas <note>The month of Magha, roughly January/February</note> or Chaitra, this work was completed.

<summary>This concluding section identifies the composition site near the Grishneshwar Jyotirlinga temple and the village of Tapara, stating the work contains 150 verses. It promises that readers will gain wealth, honor, and intellectual clarity without needing to consult larger, more complex astrological volumes.</summary>
<keywords>Muhurta Martanda, Devagiri, Jyotirlinga, Taparagrama, 1893 Vikram Samvat, astrology, Phala Shruti, intellectual clarity</keywords>
